- Context: with D-028 and D-029 resolved, every actual `pycc`/`pycc_codegen` test passed on Windows for the first time in this arc -- but `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` still failed, compiling an unrelated transitive proc-macro dependency (`manyhow-macros`), with `error: linking with \`link.exe\` failed` / `/usr/bin/link: extra operand ...` / `Try '/usr/bin/link --help' for more information` -- not an MSVC `link.exe` error message at all. Root cause: this step forced `shell: bash`; on Windows that's Git Bash, whose own startup reorders `PATH` such that its bundled coreutils `link.exe` (the Unix hardlink tool, at `C:\Program Files\Git\usr\bin\link.exe`) shadows MSVC's real linker that `ilammy/msvc-dev-cmd@v1` had already put on `PATH` correctly earlier in the same job -- a known Windows CI gotcha, confirmed directly by the error text itself (coreutils' own `link --help` message, not MSVC's).
- Decision: drop the explicit `shell: bash` override for this step. `cargo build`/`cargo test` already ran correctly on Windows earlier in the same job using pwsh (the OS's own default, no override needed), and this step's two commands (`rustup component add clippy`, `cargo clippy ...`) have no bash-specific syntax -- nothing is lost by letting each OS use its own default shell instead (bash on Linux/macOS, unchanged; pwsh on Windows, fixed).
- Alternatives: keep `shell: bash` and explicitly fix `PATH` ordering (e.g. removing/renaming Git's own `link.exe`, or setting a target-specific linker override to bypass `PATH` search entirely) -- rejected as more moving parts than simply not forcing a shell this step never needed bash syntax for in the first place.
- Consequences: none of the other `native-build-test` steps specify `shell:`, so this brings the clippy step in line with that existing pattern rather than introducing a new one. If a future step's script genuinely needs bash-specific syntax on Windows, this same PATH-shadowing risk applies and needs to be checked for explicitly, not assumed away.

## D-031: Linux's default `cc` (GCC) rejects clang-only `-target` syntax

- Status: accepted (PR-3 is the PR that depends on it)
- Context: PR-3's own code review (5 independent agents, plus pre-existing Codex review comments already on the PR) surfaced that `linker_command()` (`src/main.rs`) returned plain `cc` unconditionally on every non-Windows host, with no handling for an explicit `--target`. On Linux, `cc` is GCC by default (`ubuntu-latest`/`ubuntu-24.04-arm`, confirmed) -- GCC's driver does not understand clang's `-target <triple>` spelling at all and rejects it outright ("unrecognized command-line option '-target'"), for *any* value, including a triple naming this same host (not even genuine cross-compilation). This was untested: no existing test reached the linker with `target.is_some()` on Linux -- `targeting_a_valid_triple_with_no_local_pycc_rt_build_is_a_clean_error` (`tests/slice0.rs`) exercises `--target` on Linux but returns from `find_pycc_rt_lib_dir` before ever invoking the linker, and the macOS cross-compile tests never touch Linux's branch at all.
- Decision: on Linux specifically, `linker_command(target)` routes through the bundled `<LLVM_SYS_221_PREFIX>/bin/clang` (the same apt.llvm.org prefix `ci.yml`'s "Install LLVM 22 (Linux)" step already installs for `inkwell` itself -- no new dependency) whenever `target.is_some()`, and keeps using the system `cc` when it's `None`. macOS's `cc` is left exactly as-is even for `target.is_some()`, since macOS's system `cc` already *is* Apple clang and D-026 already proved it handles `--target` correctly (`cross-compile-build`/`cross-compile-verify`) -- routing macOS through the bundled clang too would change a tested path on an unverified assumption. A new `tests/slice0.rs` test exercises this directly on Linux: not real cross-compilation (the triple matches the runner's own arch/OS), but the minimal repro of the actual failure -- an explicit `--target` reaching the real link step on Linux, which nothing else in the suite reached. `native-build-test`'s two Linux legs gain a matching `cargo build --target <own-triple> -p pycc_rt` step so that test's `pycc_rt` build actually exists there.
- Alternatives: route every non-Windows host through the bundled clang whenever `target.is_some()` (rejected -- would change macOS's already-proven `cc` path based on the untested assumption that "system cc is already clang, so switching is transparent," the same kind of unverified assumption that cost several CI round-trips earlier in this same PR, see D-028/D-029). Reject cross-OS/same-host `--target` on Linux with a clean error instead of fixing linking (rejected -- the case that actually needs to work here includes `--target` equal to the host's own triple, which is not cross-compilation and must succeed, not just fail cleanly).
- Consequences: Linux's `--target` link path now depends on the same bundled-LLVM install already required for `inkwell` itself -- no new install step. macOS and Windows behavior is unchanged. This decision assumes `pycc_rt_lib_filename`'s target-keying (this same PR) as a prerequisite, not a separate concern: linking only succeeds once the lookup finds the right file first.

## D-032: Branch protection requires one aggregate `ci-gate`, not per-matrix-leg checks

- Status: accepted (PR-3 is the PR that depends on it)
- Context: PR-3's own Codex review (on the head commit that added the Tier-1 matrix) flagged that branch protection required only `build-test-coverage` and `audit` (confirmed via `gh api repos/rotnov/pycc/branches/main/protection`) -- none of `native-build-test`'s four Tier-1 legs, `cross-compile-build`, or `cross-compile-verify` were required checks. A pull request could therefore merge with a broken Windows, Linux, or macOS native build, or a broken cross-compile proof, as long as the two originally-required checks passed -- leaving SPEC.md's invariant 5 ("a feature doesn't exist until it's green everywhere") unenforced for exactly the jobs added to prove it.
- Decision: add a single `ci-gate` job to `ci.yml` that `needs: [build-test-coverage, native-build-test, cross-compile-build, cross-compile-verify]` and fails unless every one of those four results equals `'success'` literally, checked per job name rather than via `contains(needs.*.result, 'failure' | 'cancelled')`: `cross-compile-verify` has no `if:` of its own, so when its dependency `cross-compile-build` fails, GitHub marks `cross-compile-verify` itself `'skipped'`, not `'failure'` -- a failure-only blocklist would miss that cascade and let the gate pass. Checking each name for literal `'success'` catches `skipped`/`cancelled`/`timed_out`/`failure` alike without enumerating every non-success state.

  Branch protection's actual required-checks switch (from `build-test-coverage` to `ci-gate`) is **deliberately not done in this same step**: flipping it while `ci-gate` exists only on this PR's branch would immediately affect every other currently-open pull request against `main` (confirmed several are in flight in parallel), each waiting on a required check (`ci-gate`) that doesn't exist in their own tree yet -- a shared-governance change with a real blast radius beyond this PR, surfaced to the user rather than applied unilaterally (the same category of call as the mid-review `origin/main` divergence earlier in this arc). The switch happens once `ci-gate` exists on `main` itself, i.e. after this PR merges.
- Alternatives: add each `native-build-test` matrix leg plus `cross-compile-build`/`cross-compile-verify` directly to branch protection's required-checks list (rejected -- a matrix job's GitHub-generated check name bakes in its matrix values verbatim, e.g. `native-build-test (ubuntu-latest, x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu)`; renaming an `os` value, changing a `target`, or adding a new Tier-1/Tier-2 leg later would leave the old name permanently required-but-nonexistent -- silently blocking every future merge with no clear cause -- while the new leg goes unenforced. A single job whose own name never depends on the matrix avoids this class of staleness entirely). Flipping branch protection to require `ci-gate` immediately, in the same PR (rejected for the blast-radius reason above).
- Consequences: adding, removing, or renaming a `native-build-test` matrix entry (or any other job this PR's Tier-1 work added) needs no branch-protection change -- only `ci-gate`'s own `needs:` list, reviewed the same way as any other workflow edit. `ci-gate` runs on `ubuntu-latest` and needs no elevated permissions (it only reads `needs` context, never checks out or executes code), so it passes the existing CI permissions checker (D-020) unchanged. Until the required-checks switch happens (tracked, not silent -- see TESTING.md), a failing or pending `native-build-test`/`cross-compile-build`/`cross-compile-verify` does not by itself block a merge into `main`. (The switch has since happened -- see D-033, a new entry rather than an edit to this one.)

## D-033: Branch protection's required-checks switch to `ci-gate` is live

- Status: accepted
- Context: D-032 added `ci-gate` and proposed switching branch protection's required check from `build-test-coverage` to `ci-gate` once `ci-gate` existed on `main`, specifically to avoid blocking other pull requests mid-flight against a `main` tree without that job. D-024 separately documents protected `main`'s controls and names the required check as `build-test-coverage`, as it stood when that entry was written. A follow-up attempt to note the switch by editing D-024's `Decision` text directly was caught and reverted during review (flagged twice, independently) as violating this log's own rule against editing an accepted decision -- this entry exists specifically to record the fact without touching either D-024 or D-032's original text.
- Decision: after PR #19 (which added `ci-gate`) merged to `main`, branch protection's required status checks were updated via the GitHub API to `["ci-gate", "audit"]`, replacing the direct `build-test-coverage` requirement. `ci-gate` (D-032) transitively requires `build-test-coverage`'s success, so this is not a weakening of coverage enforcement -- it now also enforces the whole Tier-1 matrix through the same required-check slot. `docs/TESTING.md`, `docs/ROADMAP.md`, and `docs/REPOSITORY_GOVERNANCE.md` describe this current state directly; D-024's and D-032's own historical text is left exactly as originally written.
- Alternatives: edit D-024's or D-032's text directly to reflect the current state (rejected -- explicitly against this log's own edit rule, and the specific thing review caught when it was tried).
- Consequences: a reader relying on D-024 alone for the current required-check name needs this entry's context; future required-check changes should follow the same pattern (a new entry recording the fact, not an edit to the entry that first proposed it) rather than repeating this correction.

## D-034: Roadmap evidence is audited from the trusted base revision

- Status: accepted
- Context: a checked roadmap item is a release claim under D-013, but a checker and coverage job executed from the pull-request head can be replaced together by the same untrusted pull request. Keeping the required GitHub check names while turning both implementations into successful no-ops would make an in-branch evidence gate self-attesting.
- Decision: regular pull-request CI still runs the head checker for fast feedback, but it is not the authority. The required read-only `Workflow policy` job checks out the base commit, downloads the head revision's workflow files and `docs/ROADMAP.md` as non-executable data, and runs the base revision's roadmap tests and checker against that data. The trusted checker binds each accepted claim to its exact heading path, claim text, and workflow proof; it ignores any replacement checker present in the head revision. It validates the hard 100% coverage workflow unconditionally, even when the corresponding roadmap claim is unchecked or absent, so removing all markers cannot weaken the merge invariant. Changes to the trust-anchor workflow retain D-020's staged SHA-256 allowlist process.
- Alternatives: trust the pull-request copy of the checker (rejected because the same change controls both policy and proof); rely only on required check names (rejected because names say nothing about the commands that ran); require human review of each roadmap marker (rejected as non-deterministic and incompatible with the solo-maintainer workflow).
- Consequences: a pull request cannot authorize a new evidence type or weaken an existing proof in one self-contained change. The trusted checker and prospective trust-anchor digest land first; only a later pull request may activate the audited head inputs or mark the claim complete. Every new evidence type needs a mutation proving the base checker rejects a forged head proof. Once an approved trust-anchor replacement becomes active, its reviewed fixture remains a byte-identical audit snapshot and the superseded digest is retired.

## D-035: PR-4 is frontend-only; MIR gains no new lowering logic

- Status: accepted (PR-4 is the PR that depends on it)
- Context: DELIVERY_PLAN.md's PR table splits "PR-4: Frontend depth" from "PR-5: Codegen depth: full v0.1 feature set (int/float/str/bool, arithmetic, control flow, recursion, f-strings)" -- the *same* feature list PR-4's own row implies growing (via "full v0.1 grammar"). Read literally, PR-4 grows the grammar the *frontend* accepts and type-checks; PR-5 grows what the *backend* can actually compile and run. `pycc check` (CLI_SPEC.md: "frontend only: parse + types + ownership; ruff-fast, no codegen") is the concrete CLI surface that only needs the frontend, confirming this split is intentional, not an oversight.
- Decision: `pycc_hir` grows a real small-IR shape for the full v0.1 grammar. `pycc_mir::build()` is updated only mechanically, so it still compiles against the wider `HirStmt`/`HirExpr` enums (Rust's exhaustive matching forces this) -- every new construct gets an explicit `panic!("pycc_mir: <construct> codegen lands in PR-5")` arm, not real lowering. `pycc build`/`pycc run` therefore keep working on exactly the subset they already support (module-level `print(<i64 literal>)`, zero-arg function definitions/calls) and fail loudly, with a clear message naming the reason, on anything new PR-4's frontend now accepts but PR-5 hasn't implemented lowering for yet.
- Alternatives: extend `pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen` in lockstep with every new HIR construct so `pycc build` supports the full grammar immediately (rejected -- this is literally PR-5's scope restated, and doing it here would make PR-5 an empty PR while making PR-4 unboundedly large). Build a second, parallel expression tree used only by `pycc check` so `pycc_mir` never needs touching at all (rejected -- creates two divergent ASTs that must be reconciled again in PR-5 anyway, redundant work for no real benefit given the mechanical MIR update is small).
- Consequences: `pycc build`/`pycc run` on any of PR-4's new grammar (arithmetic, control flow, functions with arguments, f-strings, etc.) panics with a clear, PR-5-referencing message rather than silently miscompiling or producing wrong output -- a real, intentional, and temporary gap, not a silent one. Every `tests/slice0.rs` CLI-level test added in PR-4 for new grammar must go through `pycc check`, not `pycc build`/`pycc run`.

## D-036: Diagnostic snapshot tests are hand-rolled, not the `insta` crate

- Status: accepted (PR-4 is the PR that depends on it)
- Context: TESTING.md's Layer 3 row says diagnostic fixtures use "insta-style snapshots." DELIVERY_PLAN.md's PR-4 row says "first diagnostic codes with snapshot tests." Neither document requires the literal `insta` crate; "insta-style" describes comparing rendered output against a checked-in expected file and failing on any diff, which `std::fs::read_to_string` + `assert_eq!` already does without a new dependency.
- Decision: each `tests/diagnostics/dNNNN_slug.py` fixture pairs with a `tests/diagnostics/dNNNN_slug.expected.txt` file holding the exact expected human-format diagnostic output (CLI_SPEC.md's format). The test harness (`tests/diagnostics_test.rs`) runs `pycc check` on the fixture and asserts the captured stdout equals the expected file's contents exactly.
- Alternatives: add `insta` as a dependency now (rejected -- `insta`'s own workflow (`.snap.new` files, `cargo insta review`) is a genuine ergonomic win at scale, but is a new external dependency this PR doesn't need to introduce yet; revisit if/when the fixture count grows large enough that manual maintenance becomes the bottleneck, as its own new ADR entry).
- Consequences: adding a new diagnostic fixture means hand-writing its exact expected output once, verified by running `pycc check` on it and copying the real output in (never hand-typed from imagination) -- slightly more manual than `insta`'s auto-generate-and-review flow, acceptable at this fixture count.

## D-037: `pycc_testkit`/`tests/conformance/` remain deferred to PR-6

- Status: accepted (reaffirms D-018 for PR-4 specifically)
- Context: D-018 already deferred `pycc_testkit` "past PR-1/PR-2... built for real at PR-4/PR-6," leaving genuine ambiguity about which of those two PRs. PYTHON_STANDARDS.md's PEP matrix is still 100% `☐` (unstarted) and DELIVERY_PLAN.md's own task breakdown names PR-6 "Conformance + benchmark gate" -- a dedicated PR for exactly this harness.
- Decision: PR-4 does not create `pycc_testkit` or `tests/conformance/`. It creates only `tests/diagnostics/` (D-036), which DIAGNOSTICS.md and PYTHON_STANDARDS.md's "rejected by design" table already specify as its own, separate concern from PEP conformance fixtures.
- Alternatives: stand up a minimal `pycc_testkit` now too (rejected -- no PEP matrix exists yet for it to check against, the same YAGNI reasoning D-017/D-018 already used; would be structure without function).
- Consequences: PR-6 is where the PEP-by-PEP conformance harness and its `tests/conformance/pyXY/` fixtures actually get built; PR-4's diagnostic fixtures are unaffected by that later work since they live in a separate directory with a separate, already-real purpose.

## D-038: A leading underscore marks a top-level function "private" for T0001

- Status: accepted (PR-4 is the PR that depends on it)
- Context: TYPE_SYSTEM.md's strictness rule 1 requires annotations on "every public function/method," rule 2 says "locals and private helpers" are inferred -- but the document never defines what makes a *module-level function* public or private (there is no Python keyword for this, unlike a class's `_`-prefixed attribute convention, which IS an established Python-ecosystem norm this extends).
- Decision: a module-level function whose name does not start with `_` is public and requires a fully annotated signature (T0001 on any missing parameter or return annotation); a leading `_` marks it private, eligible for local inference instead.
- Alternatives: treat every top-level function as public, always requiring annotations regardless of naming (rejected -- forecloses the "private helper" case TYPE_SYSTEM.md's rule 2 explicitly names, with no way to opt out); require an explicit `pycc.toml`-level allowlist (rejected -- no `pycc.toml` support exists yet in v0.1, per CLI_SPEC.md's own `[project]`/`[build]` sections being unimplemented; premature complexity).
- Consequences: `def _helper(x): ...` (unannotated) type-checks via inference; `def helper(x): ...` (unannotated) raises `T0001`. Revisit if a real `pycc.toml` visibility mechanism is designed later -- this convention becomes the default, not a permanent rule.

## D-039: Function signatures are pre-registered into the module `Environment` before body checks run

- Status: accepted (PR-4 Task 9, `pycc_types`)
- Context: Task 9's own plan snippet for `check()` calls `check_function(item)` for each `HirItem::Function`, and `check_function` binds the function's own signature into a brand-new, isolated `Environment` (needed so a function's parameters don't leak into sibling scopes, and so recursive self-calls resolve). That isolated environment is discarded once `check_function` returns -- it's never merged back into `check()`'s own module-level `env`. Implementing the plan exactly as written and then running the full workspace test suite surfaced a real regression: `tests/slice0.rs`'s `build_and_run_explicit_call_to_main` (`def main() -> None: ...\n\nmain()\n`) started failing with `T0021: call to undefined function `main``, because the top-level `main()` call is checked via `check_stmt(&mut env, stmt)`, using the module `env` that Task 9's `check_function` never populated.
- Decision: `check()` does a first pass over every `HirItem` in the module, registering each `HirItem::Function`'s signature into its own module-level `Environment` via `bind_function`, *before* the second pass that actually checks each item's statements/body. This matches ordinary Python semantics (a function can be called from code that runs after its own `def`, and forward references at module scope work once the module has finished executing top to bottom) for the one case current tests exercise: top-level code calling a previously-defined function.
- Alternatives: thread the module `env`'s function registry into `check_function` so sibling functions can also call each other (rejected for now -- no existing fixture or test exercises non-recursive function-to-function calls; adding it would be speculative surface area beyond what Task 9's own scope or test suite calls for, YAGNI); leave `check_function` fully isolated and only fix the specific failing test by special-casing top-level calls (rejected -- doesn't generalize, and the two-pass registration is the more honest fix for the actual gap: signatures need to exist before anything can reference them).
- Consequences: any top-level statement can call any module-level function regardless of where in the file that function is defined. A function body still cannot call a *sibling* function it doesn't already know about via its own self-registration (only recursion is proven to work, per the existing `recursion_is_supported_since_the_function_s_own_signature_is_in_scope` test) -- revisit if/when a fixture needs one function to call another, non-recursively.

## D-040: Function bodies resolve sibling calls against the module's shared function registry

- Status: accepted (PR-4 Task 9 follow-up, `pycc_types`) -- supersedes D-039's "Alternatives" deferral of sibling-to-sibling calls
- Context: D-039 deliberately deferred sibling function calls, reasoning that no fixture exercised the case. That reasoning didn't hold up: `def helper(x: int) -> int: return x + 1` plus `def main() -> None: print(helper(5))` plus `main()` is ordinary, valid Python -- and, verified directly against the built `pycc` binary, it was rejected with `T0021: call to undefined function `helper``. The root cause is exactly what D-039 flagged as a known gap: `check_function` (called from `check()` for every `HirItem::Function`) built its own `Environment::new()` from scratch, so only a function's *own* signature (self-registered for recursion) was ever visible inside its body -- a sibling's signature, even though already present in `check()`'s module-level `env`, was never threaded through. A compiler that rejects one ordinary function calling another is a correctness defect in a PR-4 headline feature (function definitions and calls), not a documented scope boundary.
- Decision: `check_function` is split into a thin public wrapper (unchanged signature, still seeds a fresh, self-only `Environment` -- so its own existing unit tests keep testing exactly what they tested before) and an internal `check_function_in(module_env: &Environment, function: &HirItem)`, which starts from `module_env.child_for_function()` -- a new environment with empty local bindings but the *module's full function registry* cloned in. `check()` now calls `check_function_in(&env, item)` (its own module-level `env`, already populated by D-039's pre-registration pass) instead of the old `check_function(item)`. `Environment::child_for_function` is a private helper (`bindings: HashMap::new(), functions: self.functions.clone()`) -- local variables never leak into a function body's scope (correct, since Python functions don't see their caller's or the module's locals without `global`/`nonlocal`, which v0.1 doesn't support), but the function registry does, in both directions (a function can call one defined earlier or later in the file, matching D-039's own forward-reference reasoning).
- Alternatives: keep `check_function` fully isolated and special-case `main`-only top-level calls (rejected -- this is exactly the "doesn't generalize" alternative D-039 already rejected, and it would leave the newly-confirmed sibling-call bug unfixed); thread the whole module `Environment` (including top-level bindings) into every function body (rejected -- would incorrectly let a function body read module-level variables as if they were closures, which isn't real Python scoping and isn't in TYPE_SYSTEM.md's rules).
- Consequences: any module-level function can now call any other module-level function, regardless of definition order, closing the gap D-039 left open. `check_function`'s public behavior (self-only visibility, used by its own pre-existing unit tests) is unchanged and covered by `check_function_the_public_api_still_has_no_sibling_visibility`. New regression tests `a_function_can_call_a_sibling_function_defined_before_it` and `a_function_can_call_a_sibling_function_defined_after_it` cover both directions at the `pycc_types` level; an end-to-end CLI test isn't added because D-035 keeps PR-4 frontend-only -- lowering a function body with a `Return(BinOp(...))` isn't wired up in `pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen` until PR-5, so the fixture above type-checks cleanly but still panics at the documented "codegen lands in PR-5" boundary if actually built.

## D-041: Function bodies also resolve reads of module-level global variables

- Status: accepted (PR-4 Task 9 follow-up, `pycc_types`; D-055 supersedes the `UnboundLocalError` deferral recorded below) -- corrects a factually wrong claim in D-040's own "Alternatives" section, does not merely extend it
- Context: D-040's "Alternatives" paragraph rejected threading module-level bindings into function bodies, reasoning "Python functions don't see their caller's or the module's locals without `global`/`nonlocal`." That statement conflates two different things: a *caller's* locals (correctly never visible to a callee -- Python has no dynamic scoping) and the *module's* globals (visible for *reading* in any function with no declaration needed at all; `global`/`nonlocal` is only required to *rebind* a name from inside a nested scope, not to read one). Verified directly against the built `pycc` binary: `x = 5` at module level followed by `def f() -> int:\n    return x` -- ordinary, extremely common Python (any module-level constant used inside any function has this exact shape) -- was rejected with `T0021: name \`x\` is not defined`, the same class of defect D-040 itself had just fixed for sibling function calls. The root cause was identical in kind: `check_function_in`'s scope (then called `child_for_function`) reset `bindings` to an empty map, discarding every top-level assignment the module-level `env` had already accumulated.
- Decision: `Environment` gains `#[derive(Clone)]` and the dedicated `child_for_function` helper is removed in favor of a plain `module_env.clone()` -- there is no longer any field that should be reset when entering a function body, since both the function registry (D-040) and the global-variable bindings (this decision) are meant to carry over in full; only the function's own subsequent `bind` calls (its parameters, then any local assignments in its body) mutate the *clone*, never the original module `env`, so nothing leaks back out or across sibling function checks. `check()` is restructured from one interleaved pass into three: (1) register every function signature (D-039, unchanged), (2) check every `HirItem::TopLevelStmt` in source order, growing `env`'s bindings as module-level assignments are encountered -- preserving real top-to-bottom evaluation order for top-level code, so a genuine top-level forward reference to a not-yet-assigned name is still an error -- then (3) check every `HirItem::Function`'s body against a clone of `env` as it stands once *all* top-level statements have been processed, regardless of whether that function's own `def` appears before or after the global it reads. Pass 3's "regardless of position" rule mirrors D-039's own justification for function signatures: real Python only evaluates a function body when the function is *called*, and by the time any call machinery could plausibly run, the module has typically already finished executing top to bottom.
- Alternatives: interleave global-registration with function-body-checking in a single pass, exposing only globals whose `Assign` textually precedes the function's `def` (rejected -- weaker than real Python, which has no such positional restriction for a *called* function, and it would still incorrectly reject the "def appears before the global" direction, i.e. exactly `a_function_can_read_a_module_level_global_defined_after_it`); model Python's actual `UnboundLocalError` rule (a name assigned anywhere in a function body, without `global`, is local for the *entire* function scope, even before the assignment) precisely (rejected as speculative scope for this fix -- no fixture or planned v0.1 acceptance criterion needs it, and function-local `Assign` already correctly shadows a same-named global via ordinary last-write-wins `HashMap` semantics on the cloned, independent copy, which is all current tests require).
- Consequences: a function body can read any module-level global regardless of source-code order, matching real Python semantics for the common "module-level constant used inside a function" pattern. A function parameter (or a function-local `Assign`) of the same name still correctly shadows a same-named global, verified by `a_function_parameter_shadows_a_module_level_global_of_the_same_name`. New regression tests `a_function_can_read_a_module_level_global_defined_before_it` and `a_function_can_read_a_module_level_global_defined_after_it` cover both directions. Real Python's `UnboundLocalError` nuance (assigning to a name anywhere in a function body makes it local for the whole function, retroactively) is *not* modeled -- pycc is currently more permissive than CPython here; revisit if a fixture ever needs that exact error.

## D-042: Frontend performance gate is a hand-rolled regression check, informational-only in CI

- Status: accepted (PR-4 Task 14)
- Context: DELIVERY_PLAN.md's Performance gate section requires "every subsequent PR records a `pycc check` timing run... and fails if it regresses >2% against the previous PR's baseline," starting with PR-4 (the first PR with a non-trivial frontend to measure). The plan's own Task 14 draft flagged, but did not resolve, whether `criterion`'s CLI sets a failing exit code when it detects a regression via `--baseline`/`--save-baseline`. Verified directly against `criterion` 0.8.2's own source (`~/.cargo/registry/.../criterion-0.8.2/src/lib.rs` and `report.rs`): its only `std::process::exit(1)` calls are for CLI-argument-parsing errors (e.g. an unsupported flag combination under `cargo-criterion`, an invalid `--profile-time`); `ComparisonResult::Regressed` (`report.rs`) only changes the printed report's text/color, never the process exit code. A CI step that ran `cargo bench -- --baseline previous` and trusted its exit code would therefore never fail on a real regression -- silently not gating anything, while looking like it does.
- Decision: `scripts/check_perf_regression.rb` (matching the existing `scripts/check_ci_permissions.rb` convention: a small, directly-invokable Ruby script with its own `scripts/test_check_perf_regression.rb` unit-test file, `require_relative`'d, run via `ruby scripts/test_check_perf_regression.rb`) reads two criterion `estimates.json` files (`current`/`previous` baselines) directly, computes `(current.mean.point_estimate - previous.mean.point_estimate) / previous.mean.point_estimate * 100`, and exits 1 if that exceeds a 2% threshold (configurable via an optional third CLI argument), 0 otherwise, 2 on a usage/IO error. Both the regression-detected and within-threshold exit paths are exercised by real, passing unit tests (`test_exits_one_when_regression_exceeds_threshold`, `test_exits_zero_when_within_threshold`, plus an improvement case and a custom-threshold case) -- this is the part of the mechanism that is actually proven, independent of any live CI run. The new `frontend-perf-gate` CI job (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`) runs the benchmark every PR, restores the most recent prior run's `estimates.json` via `actions/cache/restore@v4` with a `run_id`-keyed cache entry and a `restore-keys` prefix fallback, invokes the script when a previous baseline exists, and always saves its own run's result via `actions/cache/save@v4` as the next run's fallback baseline.
- Alternatives: trust criterion's own `--baseline previous` comparison output/exit code directly (rejected -- confirmed above to never fail on a real regression); design and attempt to fully prove a canonical "always compare against `main`'s last baseline" cache lifecycle with a `push`-vs-`pull_request` split before merging this PR (rejected as scope creep for this task -- this sandboxed environment cannot execute a real, multi-run GitHub Actions round trip to confirm `actions/cache`'s restore-key fallback behavior across separate workflow runs; over-engineering an unverifiable design is worse than shipping the plan's own minimal sketch, informational-only, and promoting it once real CI history exists to check).
- Consequences: `frontend-perf-gate` is **not** added to `ci-gate`'s `needs:` list -- a regression cannot yet block a merge, only be visible in that PR's own CI run. The cache-based "compare against the immediately preceding run" lifecycle relies on GitHub's documented per-branch cache scoping (a `push`-to-`main` run's cache is visible as a fallback to PR branches based on it) but this specific restore/compare/save cycle has not been confirmed by an actual multi-run round trip -- this PR's own first run has no prior cache entry at all, so it can only exercise the "no previous baseline yet" path locally and in its own first CI run. Promote `frontend-perf-gate` into `ci-gate`'s required checks once a live round trip (e.g. two consecutive merged PRs) confirms the cycle behaves as designed.

## D-043: PR-4's known gaps -- Optional/narrowing/containers, help-text suggestions, real spans

- Status: accepted (PR-4's own final self-review task)
- Context: reviewing this PR's full implementation (Tasks 6-14) against TYPE_SYSTEM.md and DIAGNOSTICS.md in full found three things it does not close: (1) TYPE_SYSTEM.md rule 4 (no implicit Optional, no untyped containers, no implicit numeric narrowing) has no surface to violate yet, since v0.1's grammar (this PR's own scope, per DELIVERY_PLAN.md's TDD sequence: arithmetic -> comparisons -> if/while/for+range -> functions/recursion -> f-strings) never introduces `Optional`, containers, or narrowing constructs at all; (2) DIAGNOSTICS.md's quality bar calls for a `help:` suggestion "when one is safe" on every diagnostic, but no diagnostic this PR produces ever populates one -- `render_json` always emits `"help": []` and `render_human` has no codepath that would render one, confirmed by grepping every `Diagnostic::error`/`Diagnostic::warning` call site across `pycc_hir`/`pycc_types`/`pycc_parser`/`src/main.rs`; (3) every diagnostic's `Span` is still `Span::new(0, 0)` regardless of where the real error is -- 14 call sites across the workspace construct a diagnostic this way as of this task, confirmed by `grep -rn "Span::new(0, 0)" crates/ src/`, spanning every `T0001`/`T0002`/`T0021`/`T0023` diagnostic Tasks 6 through 13 added (only the parser's own `L0001` gets any real position information, and even that predates this PR). Threading a real span through the whole HIR-lowering/type-inference call chain -- carrying a `Span` alongside every `HirExpr`/`HirStmt` node -- is a structural change this plan deliberately did not take on given its already-large scope.
- Decision: ship PR-4 with these three gaps named here rather than silently implied-complete. (1) needs no action until a later PR's grammar actually introduces `Optional`/containers/narrowing. (2) and (3) are real, valuable, and tracked as explicit follow-up work, not blocking PR-4's own merge, since every diagnostic this PR produces is still correct and useful without a real span or a help suggestion -- just less precise than the spec's eventual bar. `tests/diagnostics/`'s six `.expected.txt` fixtures (D-036, Task 13 plus the T0024 review correction) bake in the `line 1, column 1` placeholder location for exactly this reason -- consistent, honest, but not yet precise. Every emitted error does carry and render a primary label; missing labels are not one of the accepted gaps.
- Alternatives: block PR-4 until all three are fully closed (rejected -- (1) is not actionable yet given v0.1's own grammar scope, and (3) alone is large enough to be its own PR-sized effort spanning every HIR node gaining a `span: Span` field, which this plan's already-substantial scope should not also absorb).
- Consequences: a future PR (naturally, whichever PR next touches `pycc_hir`/`pycc_types` significantly, likely PR-5 or a dedicated diagnostics-quality PR) should thread real spans through HIR before DIAGNOSTICS.md's quality bar can be honestly claimed as met; until then, every diagnostic's `-->` line points at line 1 column 1 regardless of the real error location, a known, user-visible imprecision. Once real spans land, `tests/diagnostics/`'s `.expected.txt` fixtures must be regenerated (re-run `pycc check` on each fixture and re-copy its real stdout), not hand-edited to guess the new span.

## D-044: Frontend performance is required by the aggregate merge gate

- Status: accepted (PR-4 review correction; supersedes D-042's informational-only consequence)
- Context: ARCHITECTURE.md says a frontend regression over 2% blocks merge, and DELIVERY_PLAN.md repeats that the PR-4 baseline starts a per-PR check whose later regressions fail the pull request. D-042 correctly established a benchmark and an executable JSON comparison because Criterion does not fail on regressions by itself, but then made the job informational until a later live cache round trip. That consequence weakened an existing hard contract inside the implementation pull request instead of implementing it. A temporarily absent previous baseline cannot justify making the whole job optional: the job already handles the bootstrap run explicitly by recording the first baseline and succeeding.
- Decision: add `frontend-perf-gate` to `ci-gate.needs` and require its result to equal `success`, exactly like every other job in the aggregate. A restored baseline that measures more than 2% regression makes `scripts/check_perf_regression.rb` exit non-zero, fails `frontend-perf-gate`, and therefore fails the branch-protected `ci-gate`. When no previous baseline exists, the job records one and succeeds; that is the only bootstrap behavior, not an exemption from the required job.
- Alternatives: leave the job informational until two production runs prove cache restoration (rejected because it directly contradicts the already-accepted merge invariant); require `frontend-perf-gate` as a separate branch-protection context (rejected because D-032 deliberately centralizes `ci.yml` jobs behind one stable aggregate); fail whenever no previous baseline is restored (rejected because the first legitimate run has nothing to compare).
- Consequences: PR-4 cannot merge while the performance job is missing, skipped, cancelled, pending, or failing. Cache lifecycle defects now fail closed at the aggregate boundary instead of silently removing the performance invariant. D-042 remains unchanged as historical rationale; this entry is the explicit superseding decision.

## D-045: Private helpers use explicit, monomorphic inference variables

- Status: accepted (PR-4 review correction; completes D-038's promised v0.1 inference behavior)
- Context: D-038 makes underscore-prefixed functions eligible for the local inference required by TYPE_SYSTEM.md, but the initial implementation lowered every missing private parameter and return annotation to `Ty::None`. That made an unannotated identity helper behave as though it accepted and returned only `None`: `def _identity(value): return value` followed by `_identity(1)` failed with `T0021`. The placeholder also erased the semantic distinction between an explicit `-> None` annotation and an unknown type, so no later checker pass could recover the intended signature.
- Decision: add the internal HIR marker `Ty::Infer` for missing private-helper annotations and resolve every such marker before ordinary type checking. A module-local union-style solver links inference variables through call arguments, local bindings, returns, `range` operands, and forward/reverse binary-expression constraints. The resulting signature is monomorphic for the module's v0.1 primitive subset; `bool`/`int` constraints merge to `int`, while incompatible parameter constraints produce `T0021` and incompatible returns produce `T0022`. A helper with no value return infers `None`; any other unconstrained variable fails with an annotation-requesting `T0021`. `pycc_types::check_and_resolve` returns a cloned HIR with those concrete signatures materialized, and the driver gives only that resolved module to MIR.
- Alternatives: keep `Ty::None` as the placeholder and special-case it in calls (rejected because explicit `None` becomes indistinguishable from unknown and the workaround cannot express identity relationships); merely document private inference as deferred (rejected because PR-4's delivery contract explicitly includes real local inference); implement fully polymorphic Hindley-Milner generalization in v0.1 (rejected because the current HIR and backend expose only a small concrete primitive set and no generic representation or monomorphization pipeline yet).
- Consequences: unannotated private identity, arithmetic, `range`, recursive, and implicit-`None` helpers can be checked from real constraints without admitting `Any`. An annotated result can constrain an otherwise-unresolved binary operand (for example, `_inc(x) -> int` infers `x: int`). A single private helper cannot silently acquire unrelated concrete signatures at different call sites; code that needs polymorphism must use the later generic surface or explicit annotations once supported. `Ty::Infer` exists only in the unresolved lowering result; the typed driver path materializes concrete HIR before MIR/codegen.

## D-046: Only successful main pushes publish the frontend-performance baseline

- Status: accepted (PR-4 Codex review correction; supersedes D-042's per-run publication lifecycle)
- Context: GitHub Actions caches are scoped by ref. A `pull_request` run writes to its merge ref, and later runs of that pull request search that scope before the default branch. Publishing a baseline from every successful PR head would therefore make the next head compare with the same PR's previous head. Several individually sub-2% regressions could ratchet beyond 2% relative to the last merged code without any one comparison failing.
- Decision: split the gate into `frontend-perf-measure` and `frontend-perf-gate`. The measurement job executes PR code and uploads only Criterion's `estimates.json`. The gate job has no source build: it sparse-checks out only the Ruby comparator and its tests, verifies their reviewed SHA-256 digests, restores the `frontend-perf-main-v1-` namespace, downloads the measurement as untrusted JSON data, self-tests the comparator, and applies the threshold. The new namespace deliberately excludes every baseline written by the earlier PR-local lifecycle. Pull requests never promote or save; only a successful `push` on `refs/heads/main` may publish the next canonical baseline. Both jobs are explicit `ci-gate` dependencies. The first run before any canonical baseline exists remains the one permitted bootstrap: the PR reports no baseline, and the successful post-merge `main` run publishes it.
- Alternatives: let each PR head publish and compare incrementally (rejected because it weakens the cumulative merge threshold); use a privileged PR workflow to overwrite the `main` cache (rejected because low-trust PR code must not write trusted shared state); store baselines as repository artifacts or commits (deferred because the main-scoped cache already supplies the required canonical comparison without write credentials).
- Consequences: repeated updates to one PR always compare against the latest successfully published `main` measurement rather than a PR-local predecessor. Untrusted compiler/build code cannot modify the comparator that interprets its measurement. A failed comparison cannot advance either PR-local or canonical state. If the post-merge `main` run fails or is cancelled, it leaves the previous canonical baseline intact and the failure remains a release-blocking signal.

## D-047: The frontend performance gate's early-start timeline is overridden; it moves to PR-6

- Status: accepted (timing supersession of D-042/D-044's deliberate "starting immediately, not deferred to a single check in PR-6" schedule from `docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md`'s Performance gate section, resolving tracked audit issue #12; D-042/D-044/D-046's design and rationale are left intact and unedited)
- Context: `docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md` explicitly chose to start the frontend performance gate in PR-4 rather than wait for PR-6, specifically to close audit issue #12 ("missing early performance gate"). PR-4's branch accordingly built its own `frontend-perf-measure`/`frontend-perf-gate` CI jobs (D-042, D-044, D-046) to honor that schedule. Merging `origin/main` into this branch surfaced a structural trust-anchor validator (`TRUSTED_PERF_LIFECYCLE_STEPS`/`validate_perf_gate_baseline_lifecycle` in `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb`) that a concurrent, independent automated contributor added for a `frontend-perf-gate` job shape incompatible with this branch's own D-046 design, and whose whole-file SHA-256 allowlist (`TIER1_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256S`, audited from the base ref under `pull_request_target`, immune to in-branch edits) had already moved on from this branch's earlier staged digest. Reconciling the two designs would have required a third staged-digest round trip against a validator a second actor keeps evolving independently -- two prior reconciliation attempts this same merge already failed to converge. Diff-checking confirmed the *entire* delta between this branch's `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and `origin/main`'s own copy was exactly the frontend-perf-gate job and its two `ci-gate` fan-in lines; nothing else in PR-4's actual deliverable (frontend HIR/types/diagnostics work) touches CI.
- Decision: this is a genuine override of the DELIVERY_PLAN's explicit early-start choice, not a relocation of misplaced work -- accepted anyway because continuing to chase a validator owned and evolving under a different, unpredictable automated actor was actively blocking PR-4's real deliverable with no guarantee of convergence. Revert `.github/workflows/ci.yml` to be byte-identical to `origin/main`'s copy (dropping the `frontend-perf-measure`/`frontend-perf-gate` jobs and their `ci-gate` fan-in entries entirely from this PR), and take `origin/main`'s own `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb`/`scripts/test_check_roadmap_evidence.rb` wholesale rather than reconciling this branch's parallel edits into them. PR-4 merges with zero CI-trust-anchor delta -- its digest is already the currently-allowlisted `b77ab0c1c3bcc69e69d3cb8f08e081f6eae246e7d5d19c9356455db1ff4291d2`, so no staging PR is needed. The performance-gate feature (measurement job, hash-verified gate job, structural lifecycle validator, D-046's cache-scoping fix) is deferred whole to PR-6, re-designed and re-reviewed there against whatever `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb` shape exists on `main` at that time; D-044's "required in `ci-gate`" requirement activates only once it actually lands there.
- Alternatives: do a third staged two-PR digest update to reconcile this branch's D-046 shape with the newer structural validator (rejected -- the validator is owned and evolving under a concurrent, independent contributor per the user's "an automated process, continue synchronizing" instruction, and forcing it to match this branch's preferred shape mid-merge is an unsynchronized fight, not synchronization; a third round with no guarantee the target stays stable is the thrashing this entry exists to stop); unilaterally rewrite the validator in this branch to accept D-046's guard (rejected -- `audit` checks the validator out from the base ref, so an in-branch rewrite has zero effect on this PR's own gate and only creates a second, conflicting definition of the same trust anchor); keep chasing convergence with further staged-digest rounds (rejected -- two rounds already failed to converge against a moving target, with no evidence a third would).
- Consequences: PR-5 loses the early, continuous per-PR frontend-regression detection that DELIVERY_PLAN.md's Performance gate section deliberately required starting at PR-4 -- a real, accepted regression-detection gap during PR-5, not merely a documentation update. `docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md`'s Performance gate section, and `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s "Quality gates" and "Repository governance" rows, are corrected in this same change to stop claiming the frontend-perf-gate is enforced in `ci-gate` today. No v0.1 acceptance-checklist item cites frontend-perf-gate evidence, so nothing in the checked acceptance criteria becomes false. D-042/D-044/D-046 remain accepted as written; only *when* D-044's requirement takes effect changes.

## D-048: Stage and activate the performance gate with exact-predecessor artifacts

- Status: accepted (supersedes D-047's timing deferral and D-046's Actions-cache transport; preserves D-044's required-gate invariant and D-046's successful-main-only provenance)
- Context: PR-4 has merged, so its compiler delivery is no longer coupled to the competing trust-anchor branch that forced D-047's temporary deferral. Review of the deferred workflow also found that ref-scoped Actions-cache fallback could select PR-local or non-predecessor state, and that treating every cache miss as a bootstrap would silently disable the >2% merge block after eviction or a failed publication. GitHub run artifacts provide immutable run identity, an explicit source run, retention metadata, and an API surface that can be filtered and independently revalidated against the exact predecessor commit. The first split-workflow pull request and its first `main` push still cannot have such an artifact, so activation needs one narrowly bounded transition rather than a reusable missing-baseline rule.
- Decision: land the transition in three reviewed phases. First, while the pre-split workflow remains active, check in inert byte-exact fixtures for both the activation workflow and its bootstrap-free steady-state successor; the trusted base checker binds their whole-file digests and validates the complete `frontend-perf-measure`, `frontend-perf-gate`, and aggregate `ci-gate` shapes for each. Second, create the activation branch from the refreshed post-staging `main` and replace `ci.yml` byte-for-byte from the activation fixture; after tests and independent review, an administrator confirms `PERF_ACTIVATION_HEAD` is absent, sets that repository Actions variable to the one final head SHA, reads it back, and records the base SHA, head SHA, setter, timestamp, and verified value in the activation PR. No later commit is permitted on that attempt. If the base or head must change, delete and verify removal of the variable, close the attempt, refresh/re-review, and only then set a replacement value. Pull requests query successful `push` runs of `ci.yml` on `main` for the exact `pull_request.base.sha`; main pushes query the exact `push.before` SHA. The gate revalidates every returned `head_sha`, skips expired artifacts, and downloads `frontend-perf-current` by explicit repository and run ID. A missing exact-predecessor artifact fails closed except during activation: the pull-request half requires run attempt 1, the exact variable-bound head, target `main`, a base SHA equal to the live `main` head, and the pre-split predecessor digest. The activation push half requires `refs/heads/main`, matching event/checkout/live-main SHAs, and that same predecessor digest; retries of that exact push remain eligible so a flaky or cancelled first run cannot permanently prevent baseline publication, while any different or superseded main SHA fails closed. Third, after a successful activation push publishes the first 90-day artifact, immediately delete and verify removal of the variable, record that evidence, replace `ci.yml` byte-for-byte from the already-reviewed steady-state fixture, and remove both bootstrap branches plus the pre-split and activation digests while retaining only the steady-state digest.
- Alternatives: retain D-046's main-scoped cache (rejected because restore-key lookup is ref-sensitive and cannot prove an exact predecessor); compare with the newest successful main run (rejected because overlapping workflows can make that run older than the PR's actual base); check in generated timing data (rejected because noisy machine-dependent output would become source state); hardcode the activation commit in its own workflow (impossible because the commit SHA depends on the bytes that would contain it); keep a mutable bootstrap variable permanently (rejected because an administrative transition value must not become a feature flag).
- Consequences: D-047's PR-6 delay ends once the staged activation completes, but the staging commit itself must remain honest that the gate is not active yet. Performance measurements are untrusted data, while comparator code and baseline provenance remain main-owned and hash-verified. Strict up-to-date protection determines sequencing: the activation head is finalized only after the staging merge, then recorded and audited once; any later base or head change invalidates that attempt and requires the documented delete/refresh/re-review/set lifecycle. The transition is incomplete until the first main artifact exists and the cleanup removes all bootstrap state. After cleanup, a missing, expired, cancelled, or non-exact predecessor artifact is a merge-blocking failure, never a new bootstrap.

## D-049: Performance-transition tests use an inert pre-split workflow fixture

- Status: accepted (corrects D-048's transition-fixture inventory; D-048's activation semantics and three-phase lifecycle are unchanged)
- Context: D-048 checked in byte-exact activation and steady-state fixtures, but its shell-level bootstrap tests simulated the predecessor-workflow API response by reading the live `.github/workflows/ci.yml`. Those tests passed only while the pre-split workflow was active. Replacing the live file byte-for-byte with the activation fixture made the same tests feed the activation digest back as its own predecessor, so the three legitimate bootstrap cases failed before the activation pull request could reach CI. A transition test cannot use the state it is designed to replace as its supposedly historical input.
- Decision: preserve the trusted pre-split workflow bytes as inert `tests/fixtures/d48-pre-split-ci.yml`, bind that file's SHA-256 to `PRE_SPLIT_PERF_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256`, and make the bootstrap test helper use it by default. Explicit negative tests may still supply a different file to prove that an activation or unrelated predecessor fails closed. The active workflow remains unchanged in this corrective staging change.
- Alternatives: derive the predecessor from the live workflow (rejected because activation changes that state); reconstruct a minimal synthetic workflow with the same digest (impossible because the proof is over exact bytes); read an old Git commit during tests (rejected because it adds Git history and network/repository-shape dependencies to a hermetic unit test); weaken the predecessor check (rejected because the exact trusted digest is the activation boundary).
- Consequences: the corrective staging tests pass both before and after the activation replacement, and the fixture proves exactly which retired bytes the bootstrap accepts. The pre-split fixture, its digest, and the activation-only shell tests are transitional evidence: the D-048 cleanup removes them together with every bootstrap branch after the first exact-main artifact exists.

## D-050: Workflow-only trust-anchor activation pre-stages phase-aware status documentation

- Status: accepted (narrow documentation-lifecycle rule for D-048; does not weaken the rule that ordinary implementation changes update their affected documentation in the same commit)
- Context: `docs/ROADMAP.md` and the root README must describe the repository tree in their containing commit, and the public `/status/` page is the documented projection of enforced gates and the next delivery slice, but D-048 deliberately requires the exact-head activation commit to change only `.github/workflows/ci.yml` byte-for-byte from the reviewed fixture. The previously staged roadmap wording named the pre-split workflow as the current state, while the README and website placed the performance gate after code-generation/runtime work. Leaving any of them unchanged in the activation commit would make that commit's documentation false, while editing them there would break the reviewed workflow-only transition and change the head after administrative binding.
- Decision: before finalizing the activation head, land phase-aware repository README, roadmap, and public status wording plus complete negative validator mutations in a separate reviewed prerequisite change. The tracked `ci.yml` selects the current state by byte identity with the pre-split, activation, or steady-state fixture: pre-split means the performance jobs are not active; activation means they are required through `ci-gate` and the activation-only bootstrap path for the first exact-main artifact remains present both before and after that artifact is published; steady state means the jobs remain required with no bootstrap and an unconditional exact-predecessor baseline. Workflow bytes do not encode whether the first artifact has already been published, so current-status wording must not infer that external state. The activation commit remains workflow-only. The cleanup commit replaces this transitional mapping in the README, roadmap, governance, and public status page with direct steady-state claims while it removes the retired fixtures and bootstrap state.
- Alternatives: update status documentation in the activation commit (rejected because the commit would no longer be the exact reviewed workflow-only head); leave pre-split or future-sequencing claims stale until cleanup (rejected because documentation is part of the implementation contract); use a vague statement that does not identify how to determine the active phase (rejected because it would evade rather than satisfy commit-relative status); rewrite D-048 silently (rejected because accepted decisions are superseded or clarified by a new ADR).
- Consequences: README, roadmap, governance, and public status text remain true across the byte-only activation boundary and the later first-artifact publication boundary, the administrator can bind one final workflow-only head, and cleanup remains responsible for simplifying the status text once only the steady-state digest survives. This exception is deliberately transition-specific and is not permission to separate ordinary code and documentation changes.

## D-051: Pair frontend predecessor and candidate timing on one hosted runner

- Status: accepted (staged successor to D-048's cross-run timing transport and D-042's mean estimate; D-044's greater-than-2% hard merge threshold and isolated review boundary remain unchanged)
- Context: D-048 correctly made baseline provenance exact and fail-closed, but its absolute Criterion estimate comes from a prior `main` runner while the pull-request estimate comes from a different hosted runner. That leaves machine-to-machine scheduling and host variation indistinguishable from compiler work. PR #107 exposed the defect with a documentation-and-Ruby-test-only head that changed no Rust source, benchmark, manifest, lockfile, or active workflow. Against the exact-base estimate of `7673.79 ns`, three complete workflow attempts measured `13875.91 ns` (`+80.82%`), `8657.32 ns` (`+12.82%`), and `8233.64 ns` (`+7.30%`). The third run's narrow 95% confidence interval (`8160.75`–`8316.24 ns`) shows that Criterion was internally stable on that host while still far from the predecessor host. Re-running until one sample happens to fall below 2% would select a convenient machine rather than prove the patch fast enough.
- Decision: supersede D-048's cross-run artifact comparison with a paired measurement, using the same two-phase workflow trust process as other byte-exact CI changes. The staging phase authorizes both the active D-048 digest and the inert full-workflow fixture `tests/fixtures/d51-paired-ci.yml`; it does not change `.github/workflows/ci.yml`. A separate fresh-base activation pull request must replace the active workflow byte-for-byte with that reviewed fixture and retire the D-048 digest and fixture. The paired `frontend-perf-measure` job resolves the predecessor exclusively from `pull_request.base.sha` or `push.before`, rejects missing, zero, and unsupported-event inputs, checks out both that exact SHA and `github.sha` with pinned actions and no persisted credentials, and verifies both revisions. Before executing untrusted benchmark code it requires the benchmark-definition and build-configuration contract to be identical: `benches/`, the root `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock`, both supported root toolchain filenames (`rust-toolchain.toml` and `rust-toolchain`), root `.cargo/`, every workspace-member `Cargo.toml`, and every tracked local `build.rs`. It runs the predecessor Criterion benchmark first on `macos-14` and uploads that one JSON file through the pinned v4 artifact action before candidate code runs. It then benchmarks the candidate on the same runner with a separate Cargo target directory and uploads its one JSON file separately. The isolated `frontend-perf-gate` checks out the comparator from the exact predecessor, hash-verifies the dedicated reviewed median comparator and its tests, binds each download to the exact artifact ID returned by its trusted upload step so deletion or replacement fails closed, requires exactly two regular-file estimates with no symlinks or extra files, and applies the unchanged greater-than-2% threshold. This statistic is part of the reviewed transition because a local paired validation of identical Rust and benchmark code produced a `-2.94%` mean difference when one side accumulated 15 severe high outliers, while the medians differed by only `-0.56%`. Both jobs remain literal-success dependencies of `ci-gate`.
- Alternatives: keep re-running the existing job until a favorable host passes (rejected as selection bias); increase Criterion's within-run sample or measurement duration (rejected as insufficient for the observed between-host shift, whose confidence interval was already narrow); lower or average away the 2% threshold (rejected because D-044 defines it as a hard product invariant); compare against the newest convenient `main` artifact (rejected because it loses exact-predecessor semantics); require a self-hosted benchmark machine (deferred because the repository has no reviewed, available self-hosted runner or operating procedure).
- Consequences: the staging commit still runs and documents the active D-048 artifact gate; the paired workflow is inert until its separate byte-exact activation merges. Activation needs no administrative variable or missing-baseline bootstrap because every run carries its own exact predecessor/candidate pair. After activation, old cross-run artifacts remain historical audit evidence but no longer determine merge outcomes; each run's sealed `frontend-perf-previous` and untrusted `frontend-perf-current` artifacts retain the two same-run inputs for 90 days. Measuring two revisions adds benchmark time, removes cross-machine variance, and makes isolated high outliers less able to decide the gate without weakening the threshold or comparator isolation. A change to any bound benchmark-contract path is deliberately fail-closed, even when a manifest or lockfile change is otherwise a legitimate implementation dependency update: it requires its own reviewed gate transition instead of silently redefining the performance test inside an implementation pull request.

## D-052: Share immutable function registries across type-check environments

- Status: accepted
- Context: D-041 correctly requires each function-body check to clone the completed module environment so local parameter and assignment bindings cannot leak across functions. The function registry in that environment is immutable once `check_with_signatures` begins its body-check pass, but a plain `HashMap` clone still copied every function name, parameter vector, and return type for every function. The required frontend benchmark measures this path, and same-host component timing identified type checking as a material part of the parse/lower/check budget.
- Decision: store `Environment`'s function registry in an `Arc<HashMap<…>>` and use `Arc::make_mut` only while registering functions or when the standalone `check_function` API must add its own recursive signature. Module-driven function checks share the already complete registry, borrow the registered parameter slice while binding locals, and continue to clone the ordinary bindings map. This supersedes D-041 only for how the immutable function registry and its resolved signature are read; global and local binding isolation is unchanged.
- Alternatives: keep deep-cloning the registry (rejected as repeated allocation with no semantic benefit); use `Rc` (rejected because it would unnecessarily remove `Send`/`Sync` compatibility from the public `Environment`); borrow the registry through a lifetime-parameterized environment (deferred because it would expand the public type and API surface for the same immutable-sharing result).
- Consequences: function checks avoid repeated function-registry and registered-parameter allocation while preserving lookup behavior, standalone recursion, and clone isolation through copy-on-write mutation. Against the same saved local Criterion baseline, the complete parse/lower/check fixture moved from a `7443.3 ns` mean to `6979.6 ns`; Criterion reported a `-6.23%` mean change with a `-7.40%` to `-4.76%` interval. Tests cover mutation after cloning, direct `check_function`, sibling calls, global visibility, and the hard 100% line/region gate.

## D-053: Correct the staged paired-artifact extraction layout before activation

- Status: accepted (corrects D-051's inert prospective workflow; D-048 remains the live transport until a separate activation pull request)
- Context: independent pre-activation review inspected the source of pinned `actions/download-artifact@d3f86a106a0bac45b974a628896c90dbdf5c8093` (v4.3.0) and found that its `artifact-ids` selector takes the multi-artifact extraction path even when the input contains exactly one ID. Without `merge-multiple: true`, each download adds its artifact name under the requested destination, while the reviewed fail-closed step requires `previous/estimates.json` and `current/estimates.json` directly. Activating the original D-051 fixture would therefore fail every run before comparison. The trusted `pull_request_target` policy still knows only the already merged D-051 shape and digest, so correcting the fixture and activating it in one pull request would also bypass the staged base-owned review boundary.
- Decision: keep `.github/workflows/ci.yml` byte-identical to the active D-048 steady-state fixture and retain both D-048 and D-051 authorizations during this corrective staging change. Add `merge-multiple: true` to each single-ID download in the inert `tests/fixtures/d51-paired-ci.yml`, require that exact input in the structural model, cover its removal from either download with fail-closed tests, and replace only the prospective D-051 SHA-256 with `4b1d11afba108745a2bc375e3447d92ecde843376c3bea95ab32f76b3fc53249`. After this staging change merges, a new branch from that exact default-branch state may activate the corrected fixture byte-for-byte and retire D-048.
- Alternatives: adjust the consumer to accept artifact-name subdirectories (rejected because the trusted upload IDs, not replaceable display names, are the intended input identities); use `name` so the action treats each invocation as a single-artifact download (rejected because deletion and same-name replacement would no longer fail closed); include staging and activation as separate commits in one pull request (rejected because the trusted workflow executes the default-branch base checker, not an earlier head commit from the same pull request).
- Consequences: this staging merge changes no live benchmark behavior and cannot repair the existing D-048 cross-host false failure by itself. It makes the exact extraction layout part of the reviewed D-051 contract and lets the subsequent fresh-base activation pass the base-owned policy without weakening artifact identity, the 2% threshold, comparator isolation, `ci-gate`, or the exact 100% line/region coverage invariant.

## D-054: Recover the D-048 exact-base deadlock with one audited exception

- Status: accepted (executed once for staging PR #119; the exception is closed and normal protection is restored — the "grants no reusable permission" framing in Consequences below is narrowly superseded by [D-125](#d-125-session-driven-temporary-ci-check-relaxation-narrowly-superseding-d-024d-054) for exactly that decision's own session-driven mechanism; this entry's own one-time historical exception is otherwise unchanged)
- Context: D-048 admitted a baseline only from a successful `main` run whose head was the exact pull-request base. The current `main` revision `65d67f30101ea2f051371a059ca1f70e0038e53d` produced no successful run because repeated cross-host measurements triggered the known D-051 false-positive class. The corrected D-051/D-053 trust anchor therefore could not merge: every up-to-date staging PR required `ci-gate`, while `ci-gate` required an artifact that could exist only after a successful run of that exact already-failing revision. Further reruns would select for a convenient host, and weakening the threshold or accepting an older artifact would violate the reviewed performance contract.
- Decision: use D-024's administrator-only emergency path exactly once. After public [incident #125](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/issues/125) recorded the control, justification, owner, expiry, and rollback, explicit administrator authorization permitted removing only `ci-gate` from the required-status set for at most ten minutes and exactly one merge. Strict up-to-date protection, the base-owned `audit`, administrator enforcement, pull-request review rules, conversation resolution, and the force-push/deletion prohibitions remained enabled. [PR #119](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/pull/119) merged as `416f626fcd8406cc60781d9415589367d4d9c18a`; an unconditional exit trap immediately restored the app-bound `audit` plus `ci-gate` set, and a full readback verified every protection. The corrected D-051 activation is outside the exception and must pass the restored ordinary controls. The activation removes the D-048 digest and fixture, and its allowlist test rejects their return, so the exceptional condition cannot persist as repository configuration.
- Alternatives: rerun D-048 until a favorable host passes (rejected as selection bias); accept an older or failed-run artifact (rejected because it breaks exact successful-base provenance); lower or skip the greater-than-2% comparison (rejected because it weakens D-044); merge through an undocumented administrator bypass (rejected because D-024 requires a public, scoped, auditable incident); keep `ci-gate` optional through activation (rejected because the paired workflow is designed to pass normal protection without bootstrap state).
- Consequences: the one staging merge is publicly auditable in [#125](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/issues/125), the temporary control was restored within seconds, and there is no standing bypass, repository variable, reusable fallback, or relaxed threshold. The active paired workflow measures both revisions on one runner and therefore removes the exact-base artifact dependency that caused the deadlock. Any future governance deadlock requires a new incident, new explicit authorization, its own expiry and rollback, and independent evidence; D-054 grants no reusable permission.

## D-055: Classify function-local bindings before resolving global reads

- Status: accepted (closes #120 and supersedes D-041's explicit `UnboundLocalError` deferral)
- Context: D-041 correctly made module globals readable inside functions, but its sequential clone-and-overwrite model treated a later local assignment as though it could not affect an earlier read. Python classifies bindings for the entire code block before execution: absent `global` or `nonlocal`, any assignment or `for` target makes that name local throughout the function. As a result, `x = 1; def f(): print(x); x = 2` deterministically raises `UnboundLocalError` in CPython, while pycc previously accepted it and resolved the read to the global. Private-helper constraint collection inherited the same incorrect fallback.
- Decision: construct each function's lexical-local list once from its parameters plus a recursive, deduplicating body scan for the remaining binding forms implemented by the v0.1 HIR: assignment targets and `for` targets under nested `if`, `while`, and `for` statements. The borrowed lists are reused by private-helper inference and final validation. A function environment starts from the completed module environment but removes every classified name before binding parameters and walking the body. Reads of a classified-but-not-yet-bound local produce `T0021` (“local name `<name>` is not bound before this use”) instead of consulting a same-named global. A call target is resolved as a name before its arguments, builtin handling, or function-registry lookup; a classified unbound target gets the same `T0021`, and a bound primitive value or parameter cannot fall through to a same-named callable. Until runtime exception preservation exists end to end, strict static rejection is the supported behavior.
- Alternatives: keep sequential last-write-wins shadowing (rejected because it silently changes deterministic Python behavior); emit code that raises `UnboundLocalError` at runtime (deferred because the current backend does not yet preserve this function-body case end to end); implement complete branch/loop definite-assignment joins here (rejected as conflating lexical classification with #118's separately tracked control-flow problem).
- Consequences: ordinary reads of globals that have no local binding form remain valid regardless of whether the module assignment appears before or after the function definition. A first local assignment establishes its own type without inheriting the global's representation, and a read in that assignment's right-hand side is correctly rejected. Nested binding forms affect the whole function, including a `for` target while its range operands are evaluated. Parameters remain bound at function entry. Calls cannot bypass local-value or parameter shadowing through the builtin or function registry. The checked CPython-differential source, exact human/JSON diagnostic snapshots, and direct call-target regressions lock this behavior; #118 remains responsible for whether a local bound on only some runtime paths is definitely available after a control-flow join.

## D-056: Identical executable inputs make paired timing non-blocking telemetry

- Status: accepted (active; supersedes D-051 only as the live workflow/comparator authorization while retaining its paired provenance controls)
- Context: D-051 removed cross-runner variance but did not remove order and thermal drift inside one hosted runner. Post-merge main run [30198852753](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/actions/runs/30198852753) measured byte-identical compiler inputs at `7031.66 ns` then `7252.75 ns` and failed at `+3.14%`; the next unchanged-input main run [30199477003](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/actions/runs/30199477003) measured `6573.61 ns` then `6630.12 ns` and passed at `+0.86%`. Both runs had valid exact revisions, sealed artifacts, and comparator execution, so retrying until favorable would select runner state rather than compiler evidence. [Criterion's own guidance](https://bheisler.github.io/criterion.rs/book/user_guide/command_line_output.html) says noisy environments may require longer warm-up and measurement, and its comparison logic uses uncertainty as well as a noise threshold; however, no statistical treatment can turn a timing difference between identical executable inputs into evidence that source code regressed. Google Benchmark's merged [random-interleaving change](https://github.com/google/benchmark/pull/1105) reduces order-dependent variance, while rustc-perf's [dedicated-machine experiment](https://github.com/rust-lang/rustc-perf/issues/1450) reduced wall-time variance by disabling turbo boost and simultaneous multithreading. Neither mechanism is directly available here: interleaving would execute candidate code before the trusted predecessor evidence is sealed, and the repository has no reviewed self-hosted runner.
- Decision: first stage `tests/fixtures/d56-source-aware-ci.yml` while leaving `.github/workflows/ci.yml` byte-identical to D-051, then activate the reviewed fixture byte-for-byte in a fresh-base pull request and retire D-051's active authorization. The active workflow retains exact predecessor/candidate revisions, the immutable predecessor upload, distinct exact artifact IDs, the isolated predecessor-owned comparator, and the greater-than-2% threshold. Before any candidate code executes, a fixed trusted step compares the complete `src/` and `crates/` trees and emits exactly `true` or `false`. The already-enforced benchmark contract separately requires `benches/`, root and workspace Cargo manifests, `Cargo.lock`, both root toolchain filenames, root `.cargo/`, and every tracked local `build.rs` to be identical. Together those checks cover every repository-owned executable input to this benchmark. Both benchmarks and artifacts are still produced. The hash-verified `check_source_aware_perf_regression.rb` records the observed delta but returns success for an exact `true`, because that delta is environment telemetry; for `false` it applies the unchanged `>2%` failure rule. Missing, malformed, late, incomplete, or unbound identity evidence fails closed.
