- Alternatives: raise or average the 2% threshold (rejected because it weakens D-044 and can hide real regressions); use Criterion confidence intervals alone (rejected because systematic order drift can produce narrow non-overlapping intervals for identical code); lengthen sampling or rerun a failed job (rejected as insufficient evidence for the same executable and, when repeated until green, selection bias); randomize or interleave predecessor and candidate execution (rejected because candidate execution before the predecessor upload violates the reviewed trust boundary); require a self-hosted performance machine now (deferred because no such runner or operating procedure exists); skip performance jobs for documentation-only changes (rejected because retaining both measurements exposes runner drift and continuously exercises the artifact and comparator path).
- Consequences: the staging change did not alter the live gate; the byte-exact activation makes D-056 current. Non-code changes cannot be blocked by a timing delta that is logically incapable of proving a code regression, while every change under `src/` or `crates/` remains subject to the same hard median threshold. Any future repository-owned build input outside the bound sets requires a reviewed contract extension before it can affect the benchmark. The D-051 fixture and comparator remain historical audit evidence, but their digest is no longer accepted by the active policy.

## D-061: `int`'s fast-path `i64` slot is a low-bit-tagged 63-bit fixnum

- Status: accepted (PR-5 Task 3 is the task that depends on it; renumbered from this branch's original draft ID D-052 -- see D-071's own renumbering-record table row)
- Context: D-001 says "`i64` fast path, overflow promotes to heap bigint" without fixing a concrete bit-level representation. With `pycc_own` deferred to v0.5 (no escape/ownership analysis), codegen cannot statically distinguish an `int` value that will always stay small from one that might later overflow -- a loop accumulator or a function parameter is runtime-unknown either way. Every `int` value must therefore share one storage layout from the first codegen task that touches `int` at all, or a later task migrating the layout would have to rewrite every earlier task's `alloca`/parameter/return/print/compare codegen -- exactly the kind of silent representation drift this plan's own type-consistency self-review exists to catch. Two alternatives were weighed against this constraint (see Alternatives).
- Decision: represent every `Ty::Int` value, in every context (local `alloca`, function parameter, function return, a literal constant), as one LLVM `i64`. Its low bit is the discriminant: `1` means the remaining 63 bits (arithmetic-shift-recovered: `tagged >> 1`) are the real value (`tag_smallint(n) = (n << 1) | 1`); `0` means the full 64 bits are a real heap pointer to a `BigInt` (D-058), which Rust's global allocator guarantees is at least 2-byte aligned on every Tier-1 target, so a genuine heap pointer's low bit is always `0` -- disjoint from a tagged fixnum's low bit, which is always `1`. Every arithmetic/comparison/print operation on `int` is a call to a `pycc_rt` function taking/returning this exact tagged `i64` (never a raw untagged value) -- `pycc_codegen` itself never inspects the tag bit or constructs a `BigInt` pointer; all of that logic (and 100%-coverage-friendly unit testing of it) lives in `pycc_rt`. Task 9 (bigint promotion) only ever changes what these same functions do internally on overflow -- their signatures, and every call site this task (and Tasks 4/5/10) write, stay fixed.
- Alternatives: a 16-byte `{ tag: i64, payload: i64 }` struct passed by value -- rejected: this project has already hit real, expensive-to-diagnose cross-platform ABI mismatches for far simpler cases (D-027/D-028/D-029's ~3 CI round-trips over Windows linking alone), and Windows x64's C ABI passes aggregates larger than 8 bytes *by hidden reference* rather than in two registers, unlike System V -- a fresh source of exactly that kind of bug, for no benefit this project's stated v0.1 perf bar requires. Always-boxed (every `int`, even a small one, is a heap `BigInt` from the start) -- rejected: contradicts this plan's own header ("every scalar keeps its unboxed representation... until `int` overflows") and would require building the bigint runtime (Task 9's job) before Task 3 could emit any arithmetic at all, inverting this plan's dependency order for no correctness benefit. Emitting raw LLVM `add`/`sub`/`mul`/overflow-intrinsic instructions directly in `pycc_codegen` instead of calling `pycc_rt` functions -- rejected for v0.1 only: it avoids a function-call's overhead (irrelevant, since v0.1 has no generated-code speed requirement -- see TESTING.md Layer 7, a v0.2+ item) at the cost of moving all the tagging/overflow/floor-division-semantics logic into hand-built LLVM IR, which is far harder to get right and to unit-test in isolation than equivalent Rust code in `pycc_rt`; revisit once a real perf bar exists.
- Consequences: `RUNTIME.md`'s "scalars... unboxed, never touch the runtime" describes the eventual, optimized steady state (once direct-intrinsic codegen replaces these calls, a documented future item) -- v0.1's actual `int` fast path does call into `pycc_rt` for every arithmetic/comparison/print operation; `RUNTIME.md` is annotated to say so honestly rather than silently contradicted (see Task 11). `bool` gets its own, simpler, untagged `i8` representation (0/1) -- not folded into this tagging scheme, since Python `bool` never needs bigint promotion. A **known, load-bearing gap accepted for the rest of PR-5**: `pycc_rt_int_cmp` (and any future bigint-comparison need) explicitly panics if either operand's tag bit shows it's already a heap `BigInt` -- comparing a bigint-valued `int` is out of scope for v0.1 and stays an honest, named limitation (like D-026's cross-OS gap), not a silent wrong answer. `str`'s own refcounting (Task 7) follows the same honesty convention, and this entry's own first-draft wording of that gap (added alongside Task 7) understated its scope -- corrected here rather than left standing (see D-070, which similarly flags D-060's own overclaim): codegen inserts `incref`/`decref` calls only at codegen-time *binding sites* it can see in one static pass over the MIR -- an `Assign` target's slot, a `Call` argument/return, and a final top-level-locals completion pass right before `main` returns -- never via real control-flow- or lifetime-aware liveness tracking, which only `pycc_own` (v0.5) can provide. Two distinct, memory-safe (no double-free, no use-after-free), and potentially loop-unbounded leak classes follow from that scope, not just one: **(a) an unbound temporary** -- a `str` value that is produced but never bound to any name, e.g. a string literal or a comparison/concatenation/non-duplicate-`Call` result consumed directly as an `if`/`while` condition, as a comparison or concatenation operand, or as an intermediate concatenation result -- is never decremented at all, at any point; and **(b) a loop-body-first binding** -- a `str` local whose *first* assignment (within the function currently being compiled) lexically occurs inside a `while`/`for` body is not recognized as "already bound" by codegen's single static visit to that `Assign` statement, so `decref_old_str_if_reassigning` never fires for it and every loop iteration but the last leaks its predecessor's value (only the final iteration's value is later freed, by whichever exit mechanism applies -- the top-level completion pass, or case (c) below). The originally-documented gap is real but narrower than either of these: **(c) a function-scoped `str` local/parameter never reassigned before its function returns** is never decrefed at any of that function's `return` sites. All three are accepted for the rest of PR-5, closed only once `pycc_own` lands.

## D-062: Confirm paired frontend regressions with five fixed complete replicates

- Status: accepted (active; supersedes D-056 only as the live workflow/comparator authorization while retaining its executable-input classifier and telemetry rule)
- Context: D-056 deterministically removes false failures when every executable input is identical, but deliberately leaves changed-source comparisons on D-051's single predecessor-then-candidate Criterion median. PR #131 measured exact base `619d23291b85418169aded93ae1b58246b3951b1` and head `900bf429ebaee65011ec8b522b2d52f4646ff4b3` at `6752.36 ns -> 6759.24 ns` (`+0.10%`, pass), while its immediate post-merge main run `30201385971` measured the same predecessor and merge commit `62dfc05c97b119c6a9aa8592ccd1e26e8ba1b28d` at `6646.81 ns -> 6889.97 ns` (`+3.66%`, fail). The PR head and merge commit have the identical Git tree `29466decb37627d443d79e18dc18ead7c409e52d`; both runs therefore measured the same changed-source pair, yet reached opposite gate decisions. D-056 would correctly classify that pair as changed and would still expose it to the same single noisy observation. Artifact identity, extraction, predecessor-owned comparator verification, and file checks all succeeded; the remaining defect is changed-source within-run phase, scheduling, and outlier variance.
- Decision: first stage the exact `tests/fixtures/d62-replicated-paired-ci.yml` successor and authorize it alongside active D-056, then activate those reviewed bytes in a fresh-base pull request and retire D-056's live digest. The active workflow retains D-056's complete pre-execution executable-input classifier and deterministic telemetry path: an exact `true` remains non-blocking regardless of timing. For `false`, retain exact predecessor resolution, the benchmark/build contract, separate Cargo target directories, predecessor-first trust order, distinct exact artifact IDs, flattened downloads, the predecessor-owned hash-verified comparator, literal-success `ci-gate` fan-in, and the unchanged greater-than-2% threshold, but replace each revision's single Criterion result with exactly five complete, predeclared runs. The measurement job records every `round-1.json` through `round-5.json`; it uploads and seals all five predecessor files before executing candidate code, then records all five candidate files. The isolated comparator requires exactly those ten regular files with no symlinks or extras, extracts every per-run median, computes the median of each fixed five-value set, and applies the 2% threshold to those aggregates only when inputs changed. No sample is retried, discarded, or added after its result is known.
- Alternatives: rerun a failed workflow until one pair passes (rejected as selection bias); widen the 2% threshold or compare confidence bounds permissively (rejected because it weakens D-044's product invariant); alternate predecessor and candidate executions (rejected because candidate code would run before a later predecessor sample and could influence supposedly trusted evidence through a lingering same-user process); average all runs (rejected because the severe outliers already observed can dominate a mean); use a self-hosted benchmark host (deferred because the repository has no reviewed, available host or operating procedure).
- Consequences: the staging merge changed no live behavior; byte-exact activation makes D-062 current and leaves D-056 as historical audit evidence whose digest the public policy rejects. For changed executable inputs, a fixed majority of three samples, rather than one observation, determines each revision's aggregate while every sample remains auditable for 90 days; identical inputs preserve D-056's logically deterministic non-blocking result. The extra eight Criterion runs add deterministic CI time but remain below the slower Windows matrix leg in local timing. An identical-tree local 5+5 validation retained all results and measured predecessor medians `7068.84, 7046.87, 7033.93, 7093.08, 7100.54 ns` versus candidate medians `7081.96, 7078.49, 7030.96, 7054.06, 7043.53 ns`; aggregate medians were `7068.84 ns -> 7054.06 ns` (`-0.21%`). Synthetic tests prove D-056's identical-input bypass, an isolated extreme outlier cannot decide a changed-input gate, a three-of-five regression above 2% still fails, exactly 2% passes, and missing, extra, symlinked, malformed, non-positive, non-finite, or wrongly shaped evidence fails closed. Activation alone does not resolve #109: issue closure still requires repeated green changed-source PR and post-merge measurements without result selection, because unchanged-source activation runs exercise the telemetry path rather than the blocking aggregate.

## D-066: Maintain an agent retrospective log and a session handoff log

- Status: accepted
- Context: this project runs sustained autonomous agent work, sometimes with multiple independent agents active on the repository concurrently (see D-013). Two related but distinct kinds of context were being lost between sessions: process mistakes worth learning from (a session repeating the same failed dispatch pattern four times before adapting; three staged-digest reconciliation rounds against a moving CI target before deciding to decouple; an ADR-numbering collision from two independent actors reserving the same ID range days apart) had no durable home, and a long autonomous session's current state (what's done, what's in flight, what a fresh session needs to know to resume) existed only in conversation transcripts, which do not survive a handoff to a different session or a different agent platform.
- Decision: add `docs/AGENT_RETROSPECTIVE.md` (process-mistake journal, newest entry first, for retrospective learning — never a merge gate, never containing credentials or personal information) and `docs/SESSION_LOG.md` (running handoff snapshot: overall status, what's in flight, known follow-ups, where to look to resume — snapshots, not a full transcript). Both are ordinary tracked markdown documents, reviewed like any other documentation change; neither is machine-generated or CI-enforced. An agent should add a retrospective entry when a mistake cost meaningful time and the lesson is actionable by a future session, and should update the session log at meaningful checkpoints (a PR opened or merged, a milestone reached, before ending a long session) rather than after every action. Each session snapshot must identify the commit and repository state actually inspected and must distinguish uncommitted or unmerged work from delivered work.
- Alternatives: fold this into `docs/DECISIONS.md` itself (rejected — that log is for irreversible design choices with alternatives considered, not process narrative, and mixing the two would dilute both); keep it only in conversation memory or an external tool (rejected — does not survive a handoff to a different session, different agent, or different platform, which is exactly the failure mode this decision addresses); a single combined file (rejected — retrospective entries are timeless lessons revisited occasionally, while the session log is a fast-changing snapshot meant to be read once at resume time; combining them would make the useful lessons hard to find under fast-changing status noise).
- Consequences: future sessions gain a place to check for known process pitfalls before repeating them and a concrete resume point after a handoff, at the cost of two more documents to keep current — both are explicitly snapshots/journals rather than normative contracts, so they carry none of the enforcement machinery (CI checks, roadmap-evidence markers) that governs `docs/ROADMAP.md` or `docs/DECISIONS.md` itself.

## D-067: Publish the pre-commit hook from the main pycc repository

- Status: accepted
- Context: consumers need an immutable revision containing both the hook manifest and the exact `pycc` implementation it runs. pycc is pre-alpha, and the manifest, CLI batching contract, diagnostics, source-encoding support, installation requirements, and tests are still evolving together. A separate hook repository would require a second release and synchronization boundary before there is an independent adapter worth versioning.
- Decision: the main `rotnov/pycc` repository is the source repository for the `language: rust` `pycc-check` hook. `.pre-commit-hooks.yaml` invokes `pycc check --`; consumers pin a release tag or immutable commit from this repository. The hook remains read-only and serial, and its distribution and verification contract is owned by `docs/DISTRIBUTION.md`.
- Alternatives: create a dedicated pre-commit repository immediately (rejected because it would duplicate or remotely pin the manifest while adding cross-repository release drift); publish a wrapper that downloads a binary (rejected because binary distribution, signing, and provenance are not specified yet); use `language: system` (rejected because it would silently depend on an unpinned user installation).
- Consequences: hook releases and compiler revisions are atomic, and integration tests can exercise the exact implementation named by the manifest. The initial install builds the current root package and therefore requires pinned Rust 1.97.1 and LLVM 22.1.1 until the package boundary is deliberately changed. Splitting the hook into a separate repository or a lightweight package requires a superseding ADR and equivalent Codex/Claude documentation and validation updates.

## D-068: Use a pinned local reviewer as the required review loop

- Status: accepted
- Context: asynchronous GitHub `@codex review` requests can be delayed or unavailable across pull requests, so they cannot reliably serve as a merge gate. The repository has one human maintainer, whose self-approval does not satisfy GitHub approving-review requirements. The project still needs an independent, broad correctness, contract, security, test, and documentation pass before significant work is completed or merged.
- Decision: select only from the repository's explicitly security-reviewed reviewer dependencies in `docs/AGENT_TOOLING.md`, prefer the eligible read-only reviewer with the broadest checklist, verify its immutable artifact digests, and launch it directly in a fresh independent local context. Review staged or working changes before commit and the full merge-base-to-`HEAD` range for a clean pull-request branch. Fix actionable findings and rerun when the diff changes materially. GitHub review comments are optional and are requested only when the user explicitly asks for them.
- Alternatives: keep `@codex review` as a required gate (rejected because asynchronous service availability would block unrelated pull requests); install or select an arbitrary global or marketplace reviewer (rejected because mutable, unreviewed instructions are a supply-chain risk); require an approving GitHub review with one maintainer (rejected because author self-approval cannot satisfy the rule and deadlocks ordinary work); rely only on tests and CI (rejected because deterministic checks do not replace cross-contract review).
- Consequences: local review no longer depends on GitHub comment turnaround and remains reproducible across Codex and Claude Code through the same pinned artifacts and contract. Reviewer pin updates require the documented security review and marketplace validation. If a client cannot bind the exact pinned reviewer, the local review is reported unavailable rather than silently weakened; branch protection, required CI, 100% coverage, specifications, resolved conversations, and future independent human review remain separate gates.

## D-072: `print()`'s `None` result cannot be used as a nested expression in v0.1

- Status: accepted (surfaced by `build-test-coverage` failing on this branch's own merge with `main`: PR-5's real MIR/codegen lowering makes D-035's tracked panic unreachable, breaking `scripts/run_alpha_skill_evals.py`'s `classify-planned-backend-boundary-without-write`/`refuse-accepted-pr5-boundary-publication` fixtures, which hard-code that exact now-closed panic as their reproducer)
- Context: D-035 defined `pycc_mir`'s "codegen lands in PR-5" panic as PR-4's frontend/backend gap, and the `pycc`/`pycc-feedback` alpha skills (and their offline eval harness) cited it as the canonical example of an "intentional temporary alpha boundary, not a reportable defect." PR-5 closes that exact gap for real -- `pycc_mir` has no such panic left (`grep -n "panic!" crates/pycc_mir/src/lib.rs` shows only one internal-invariant panic, unrelated). Verified directly: `def main() -> None:\n    value = 1\n    print(42)\n\nmain()\n` (D-035's own fixture) now builds and runs cleanly. But not every construct a valid, type-checked v0.1 program can express is lowered: `pycc_types` types `print(...)` as `Ty::None` (matching CPython), and assigning that result to a name (`value = print(42)`) type-checks fine, yet `pycc_codegen::emit_expr`'s `MirExpr::Call` arm has no general representation for a `None` value flowing through an `alloca`/parameter/return -- no PR-5 task needed one, since RUNTIME.md's `None` mention is about the scalar's eventual representation, not a promise that every expression position accepts it in v0.1.
- Decision: keep this a named, explicit panic (`"pycc_codegen: using print()'s result as a nested expression is not supported yet"`, `crates/pycc_codegen/src/lib.rs`'s `Call` arm) rather than inventing a placeholder `None` value -- consistent with this project's standing convention that unsupported-but-out-of-scope constructs panic loudly instead of silently miscompiling. Point the `pycc` and `pycc-feedback` alpha skills, and their eval fixtures, at this exact message and this decision instead of D-035's now-unreachable one. D-035's own text is left unedited (it accurately described PR-4's state); this is a new decision, not a rewrite.
- Alternatives: implement full `None`-as-a-general-expression-value support now (a real singleton/sentinel that can be stored, compared, and passed) -- rejected as scope creep beyond PR-5's stated v0.1 feature set (int/float/str/bool arithmetic, control flow, recursion, f-strings), with no `docs/ROADMAP.md` acceptance criterion depending on it. Silently returning a garbage/zero value for `print()`'s result -- rejected, violates the no-silent-wrong-result convention this same file already applies elsewhere (D-026, D-061's own accepted-leak notes).
- Consequences: `value = print(x)` (or any other nested use of `print(...)`'s return value) is a documented, intentional exit-101 alpha boundary, not a defect -- agents using the `pycc` skill must recognize this exact message and not route it to `pycc-feedback`. Closing this gap for real is a documented future item, not a v0.1 requirement. `scripts/run_alpha_skill_evals.py`'s `classify-planned-backend-boundary-without-write` and `refuse-accepted-pr5-boundary-publication` cases are updated in this same change to reproduce this message instead of D-035's.

## D-073: `pycc_codegen`'s target machine uses `RelocMode::PIC`

- Status: accepted (surfaced by PR #132's own CI: `native-build-test (ubuntu-latest, x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu)` failed exactly one fixture, `mandelbrot_ascii_produces_a_grid_of_the_expected_dimensions_and_palette`, after the D-073-adjacent `-lm` fixes already resolved every other Linux link failure)
- Context: `compile_to_object`'s `create_target_machine` call used `RelocMode::Default`. On this LLVM/target pairing that resolves to absolute (non-position-independent) addressing on Linux, but Ubuntu's `cc` (GCC) links as a position-independent executable (PIE) by default -- a hardening default, not something this project's linker invocation controls. A small program's `.text`/`.rodata` fits within a PIE's 32-bit-relative addressing envelope regardless, so most fixtures link fine either way; `mandelbrot_ascii`'s larger constant data (its ASCII palette string plus several float literals) pushed at least one relocation past what an absolute 32-bit reloc can express inside a PIE, and GNU `ld` refused with "relocation R_X86_64_32 against `.rodata' can not be used when making a PIE object; recompile with -fPIE" followed by "failed to set dynamic section sizes". macOS and Windows's own native-build-test legs never hit this, because Apple's linker requires PIE unconditionally (silently rejecting or accepting compatible input either way in practice) and this project's Windows path routes through explicit `-target x86_64-pc-windows-msvc` clang/lld-link, whose own default already tolerated `Default`'s output.
- Decision: set `RelocMode::PIC` instead of `RelocMode::Default` for every target this function builds for, unconditionally (no per-OS branch) -- position-independent code is not merely a Linux workaround here, it is the correct steady-state choice, since it is mandatory on macOS, standard on Windows/MSVC, and now effectively required on Linux too given every Tier-1 distribution's PIE-by-default toolchain.
- Alternatives: pass `-no-pie` to the Linux linker invocation instead (rejected -- this only patches `src/main.rs`'s and `pycc_codegen`'s own test helper's two linker invocations; the underlying object file would still carry non-relocatable code, an accident waiting to recur anywhere else the object is ever linked, and some hardened Linux distributions refuse `-no-pie` outright when system libraries are built PIC-only, trading one Linux-specific gotcha for another instead of removing the class of bug). Branch `RelocMode` per target OS (rejected as unneeded complexity -- `PIC` is fine everywhere D-073 checked, so there is no reason to special-case it).
- Consequences: every object `pycc_codegen` emits is now position-independent on every Tier-1 target, closing this exact relocation-overflow failure mode for any future fixture with large enough constant data, not just `mandelbrot_ascii`. No known v0.1 test or acceptance criterion depends on absolute addressing.

## D-074: Preserve checked lexical scope and scalar representations through MIR and LLVM

- Status: accepted (PR #132 Codex review found that backend lowering did not yet preserve several already-accepted type-system and control-flow contracts)
- Context: D-041/D-055 make module bindings available to function bodies regardless of declaration order while classifying every nested function assignment and `for` target as local from entry. The type checker also accepts `bool` wherever an `int` is required and fixes an inferred binding's representation at its first assignment. The original MIR/codegen walk was source-order and allocation-on-first-emission: functions could miss later globals, branch-local allocas did not dominate post-branch uses, and accepted `bool` values could reach an `i64` parameter, return, range, or existing binding as `i8`. A two-return `if` also left a synthetic merge block unterminated, and `None`-returning calls interpolated in f-strings reused an internal false placeholder instead of Python's text `None`.
- Decision: MIR registers functions, lowers top-level statements sequentially to preserve top-level forward-read errors, and lowers function bodies only after the module binding environment is complete. Codegen declares every supported module binding as an LLVM global before emitting bodies and seeds function name resolution from those globals. Every global and non-parameter local slot has an initialization flag: assignment sets the flag only after evaluating and storing its right-hand side, while an earlier read traps before an LLVM initializer, `undef`, or null can become a fabricated Python value. Parameters are initialized at entry and need no flag; #118 still owns earlier static rejection and structured definite-assignment diagnostics. Codegen recursively classifies each function's assignment and `for` targets and allocates all function-local slots in the entry block; parameters and local targets override same-named globals. A `for` uses hidden SSA induction state and writes the Python-visible target only on entry to a real iteration, so an empty range leaves a new target unbound, the post-loop target remains the last element, and body reassignment cannot alter iteration. The first binding type remains stable in MIR. At assignment, call-argument, return, and `range` boundaries, an accepted `bool` flowing to `int` is widened into the tagged D-061 representation. A synthetic `if` merge with no predecessor is terminated by `unreachable`, and a `None`-returning call in an f-string preserves the call side effect then materializes `None` text. General nested `None` values remain outside D-072.
- Alternatives: reject later module globals or accepted `bool`→`int` programs in the backend (rejected because `check` already promises they are valid); allocate on the first dynamically emitted assignment (rejected because an allocation inside one control-flow predecessor cannot safely serve other predecessors or a post-join read); add SSA and definite-assignment analysis now (rejected because D-057 deliberately keeps PR-5 MIR structural and #118 owns the later join analysis); store every scalar in one oversized tagged union (rejected because it discards the fixed representations that type checking exists to establish).
- Consequences: backend name resolution now matches D-041/D-055 without exposing a binding before execution initializes it, all local allocas dominate their uses, `for` target lifetime matches Python for empty, positive, negative, and body-reassignment paths, and `bool`→`int` compatibility has one representation-preserving realization across storage and ABI boundaries. Until Python exception objects land, an early global or local read is a deterministic process trap rather than a formatted `NameError`/`UnboundLocalError`; it cannot silently return a fabricated scalar or dereference a null string. Preallocating string slots also closes D-061/D-070's loop-body-first reassignment leak because every iteration can decref the slot's previous value. D-061/D-070 remain historical descriptions of the accepted PR-5 scope; D-074 supersedes only that loop-body-first leak. Unbound string temporaries and function-scoped string parameters/locals at return remain documented memory-safe leaks until `pycc_own`.

## D-075: Carry `None` parameters as a canonical zero unit

- Status: accepted (PR #132 Codex review found that the frontend accepted a `None`-typed parameter while codegen panicked merely declaring the function)
- Context: LLVM `void` is a return type, not a legal parameter or storage type. pycc's frontend nevertheless accepts the standard Python annotation `None` for a parameter, and a `None`-returning call or another `None` parameter can supply that argument. Rejecting the program only in codegen violates the checked-program contract and made even an unused `def f(value: None) -> None: return` crash during function declaration.
- Decision: keep `None` function returns as LLVM `void`, and use the canonical LLVM `i8 0` as the unit carrier whenever a `None` value crosses the v0.1 user-function parameter ABI or is held in that parameter's entry slot. MIR's static `Ty::None` is the semantic tag; the identical physical width of `bool` does not make the values interchangeable. Evaluating `return value` in a `None` function preserves expression side effects and emits a void return. Printing or interpolating a `None`-typed parameter materializes Python's `None` text. D-072's explicit rejection of using `print()` itself as a nested expression remains unchanged.
- Alternatives: reject `None` annotations on parameters during HIR lowering (rejected because the standard annotation and unit type are already part of the v0.1 frontend contract); use an empty LLVM struct (rejected because an internal aggregate adds target-ABI complexity without carrying information); implement a general tagged `None` object now (rejected because the singleton carries no payload and D-072 still deliberately excludes `print()`'s result from general expression flow).
- Consequences: every source program accepted solely because it declares or passes a `None` parameter now reaches codegen without a declaration-time panic, and the carrier has one deterministic representation across Tier-1 targets. The representation is an internal ABI detail, not a new user-visible value or a relaxation of D-072.

## D-076: Normalize unsuccessful `pycc run` children to exit 101

- Status: accepted (PR #132 Codex review found that a generated runtime panic terminated by signal and surfaced as exit 1 on Unix)
- Context: CLI_SPEC.md promises exit 101 for a compiled-program panic or uncaught runtime failure. `pycc run` previously converted the child's `ExitStatus::code()` directly and substituted 1 when no code existed. Unix reports signal termination with no code, while Windows can report an abort status outside the portable one-byte range, so the implementation leaked platform process details and contradicted the stable CLI contract.
- Decision: generated programs in the current CLI have no user-controlled non-zero exit status. `pycc run` therefore returns 0 only when the child succeeds and maps every other child termination to 101, regardless of whether the platform represents it as a normal non-zero code, signal, exception, or abort status. Build and invocation failures retain their existing compiler-owned exit codes because they occur before child execution.
- Alternatives: preserve ordinary child codes and special-case Unix signals (rejected because no current language surface can request those codes and the result would remain platform-dependent); map only missing codes to 101 (rejected because Windows abort statuses would still be truncated); make every runtime failure unwind back through the compiler process now (deferred until Python exception objects and runtime propagation exist).
- Consequences: `pycc run` now fulfills one cross-platform exit contract for runtime panics and traps; callers need not decode platform-specific child status. A future explicit `sys.exit(n)` feature must supersede this decision with a documented rule for user-requested statuses.

## D-077: Project-local iEvo hook lifecycle is symmetric

- Status: accepted (closes #34; implements D-023's local-hook rollback)
- Context: D-023 deliberately relocates iEvo's generated Claude hooks to ignored `.claude/settings.local.json`, but the paired iEvo disable workflow through v0.61.1 removed entries only from shared `.claude/settings.json` before deleting their scripts. The result was a configured local hook whose target no longer existed and exited 127 on every prompt. Upstream `ievo-ai/skills#446` was later resolved by tracked dispatcher shims in PR #455, but this repository's stricter D-023/D-025 policy still keeps generated hooks local; equivalent project-local Codex wiring belongs in `.codex/hooks.json`.
- Decision: `scripts/manage_ievo_hooks.py` is the repository-owned lifecycle boundary. After an iEvo enable or refresh, `localize` moves only the exact known iEvo Claude hook entries from shared to local settings, deduplicates them with refreshed shared metadata taking precedence over a stale local copy, preserves every unrelated setting and hook (including empty groups/events), restores `.ievo/hooks/` to the machine-local ignore policy if upstream added tracked-shim exceptions, and refuses missing or symlinked targets. It writes the local destination before removing shared entries so an interrupted relocation cannot lose the only hook record. `check --smoke` validates the shared/local split, both ignored client configurations, every generated target, and one synthetic zero-exit invocation per distinct hook script. `disable` first parses every present configuration, removes the exact iEvo entries from shared/local Claude and Codex configuration, and fails before mutation if any managed script remains referenced through an unsupported event or command form, including a separator, shell-escaped, lexical, quoted, case-insensitive, or Unicode-prefixed alias of the managed directory; only then does it delete the three generated hook targets and companions plus their vendored fallback directory. It preserves the tracked `.ievo/evo-auto.flag`, because that file records repository-wide intent and disabling it requires a separate reviewed D-023 rollback. All three operations are idempotent; malformed or unsupported configuration fails before mutation.
- Alternatives: adopt upstream's tracked shims (rejected because D-025 requires a separately registered clean-clone contract and the project intentionally keeps hook execution local); teach contributors to hand-edit local JSON before every disable (rejected because it is neither symmetric nor testable); run generic disable and tolerate stale local entries (rejected because `UserPromptSubmit` turns the resulting exit 127 into a per-prompt failure).
- Consequences: `.claude/settings.local.json`, `.codex/hooks.json`, and `.ievo/hooks/` remain ignored and machine-local while `.ievo/evo-auto.flag` preserves shared corrections-only intent. Required agent-policy discovery exercises a tracked-clean-clone enable/refresh → smoke → disable cycle and covers preservation, refreshed metadata, invalid JSON, effective-ignore ordering, unknown managed-subtree references, symlinked hook/config ancestors, interrupted writes, and repeated operations. Every lifecycle transition, including disable, requires the complete non-conflicting intent before mutation. Future iEvo hook names or event shapes fail closed until the helper and tests explicitly support them.

## D-078: External repository monitoring is checkpointed and event-driven

- Status: accepted
- Context: repository documents correctly retain historical evidence, including D-054's closed issue #125 and merged staging PR #119. Treating those citations as current work caused a monitor to poll completed objects that had not changed after the task began, creating status noise and obscuring the actually live repository state. Documentation relevance and post-checkpoint repository activity are different signals and need an explicit boundary.
- Decision: every monitoring cycle records the exact refreshed remote default-branch commit plus the number, state, draft status, and exact head of every open pull request; task-active pull requests additionally record mergeability, unresolved review threads, and required checks. After that checkpoint, only a newly observed default-branch commit; a newly opened or reopened pull request; a state, draft-status, or head change relative to an inventoried pull request's baseline; or a mergeability, review-thread, or required-check change on a task-active pull request enters the live set. An `updated_at` change caused only by comments, reactions, labels, or activity outside those fields does not qualify. A task-active close or merge is evaluated once and then removed. Documentation, ADR, retrospective, and session-log citations never add an item to the live set by themselves; closed issues are inspected only when the active task explicitly names one for a bounded audit. Before waiting for CI, the monitor rechecks PR state, draft status, mergeability, exact head, and unresolved threads. For each new default-branch merge it inspects the introduced commit range and the post-merge workflows for that exact commit before advancing the checkpoint.
- Alternatives: poll every PR or issue mentioned by project documentation (rejected because durable historical evidence then creates perpetual status work); use only an object's `updated_at` timestamp (rejected because bot reactions and unrelated comments can change a timestamp without making the object relevant to the active task); poll check runs without rechecking PR state and head (rejected because a merged, closed, conflicting, or superseded PR makes the old wait meaningless).
- Consequences: monitoring is bounded, repeatable, and focused on repository transitions observed after an explicit base. Historical incidents remain discoverable without re-entering the work queue, while explicitly requested issue audits and new PR activity remain possible. Canonical agent instructions carry the operational rule, repository governance specifies its scope, and agent-asset validation fails if its exact unindented list items disappear from the exact D-078-linked active monitoring section. Plain prose, fenced, raw-HTML, HTML-commented, indented-code, blockquoted, nested-container, duplicate, renamed, and out-of-section copies cannot satisfy that gate; CommonMark fence, container-boundary, comment-boundary, tab-stop, and invalid-info-string handling prevents hidden text from becoming policy or literal code from hiding it.

## D-079: A separate plain-timed check for the `<50ms/1000 LOC` floor, not an extension of the paired-regression Criterion gate

- Status: accepted
- Context: `frontend-perf-measure`/`frontend-perf-gate` (D-042/D-044/D-046/D-056/D-062) already benchmark `pycc check`'s frontend path via `benches/check_bench.rs`'s single Criterion benchmark (`pycc_check_frontend_fixture`, a tiny inline `fib`+loop fixture), enforcing a *paired, relative* `>2%` regression threshold against the immediate predecessor commit — never an absolute wall-clock or throughput number. `frontend-perf-gate`'s own "Verify exact benchmark revisions" step does a byte-for-byte `git diff --no-index --exit-code` of `benches/`, `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`, `rust-toolchain.toml`, `rust-toolchain`, `.cargo`, and every `crates/**/Cargo.toml`/`build.rs` between the predecessor and candidate commit, hard-failing if anything there differs. DELIVERY_PLAN.md row 6's acceptance criterion is a different shape entirely: an *absolute* floor (`<50ms` for `1000` lines of source), independent of any predecessor comparison.
- Decision: implement a wholly separate, plain-timed CLI check: a checked-in ~1000-line synthetic Python fixture (`tests/fixtures/pr6_1000_loc_bench.py`) and a small script (`scripts/check_frontend_throughput.rb`, mirroring this project's existing Ruby-script convention for CI-gate logic) that runs `pycc check` against it, measures wall-clock time via the shell's own timing (not Criterion — a single `time`-style measurement is sufficient for an absolute floor, no replicate-averaging needed since this isn't comparing two noisy measurements against each other), and fails if the elapsed time exceeds 50ms. This never touches `benches/`, `Cargo.toml`, or any file the existing gate's integrity check diffs, so `frontend-perf-gate` continues to pass unmodified.
- Alternatives: add a second `#[bench]`/Criterion function to `benches/check_bench.rs` (rejected — touches `benches/`, which the existing gate's own "exact benchmark revisions" step treats as a hard-failing diff unless that same PR also updates the integrity-check's file list, expanding the trusted surface of an already-hardened security-relevant script for a feature that doesn't need Criterion's statistical machinery at all — an absolute floor needs one measurement, not a distribution). Reuse `frontend-perf-gate`'s job as a second assertion inside it (rejected — conflates two different failure semantics, relative-regression vs. absolute-floor, in one job whose whole design is built around the paired-comparison shape).
- Consequences: a new, small, independently testable script and fixture; zero risk to the existing hardened perf-regression gate's integrity checks; CI wiring (Task 7) adds this as its own step, not a change to any existing perf job's steps.

## D-080: The conformance oracle's CI setup runs after the D-014 coverage gate, never inside its trusted boundary

- Status: accepted
- Context: Task 6 wires `tests/conformance.rs`'s CPython oracle (D-085) into all 5 Tier-1 CI targets, including `build-test-coverage` (macos-14), which also runs the D-014 100%-coverage gate under `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb`'s security-reviewed `coverage_gate_present?` validation: a byte-for-byte match of the coverage job's step prefix (`TRUSTED_COVERAGE_STEPS`) and the coverage step's own script (`COVERAGE_SCRIPT`), plus a whole-file SHA256 pin (`REVIEWED_PERF_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256S`) over `ci.yml` itself. A first pass at this wiring inserted `actions/setup-python` and an oracle-version-check step *before* the coverage step, and patched the coverage script itself to expose the oracle's directory on `PATH` inside the isolated `nobody` sandbox (via a new `ORACLE_PYTHON_DIR` lookup and `RUNNER_TOOL_CACHE` `chmod` calls). That correctly broke `check_roadmap_evidence.rb`'s structural checks — CI's `Workflow policy` audit rejected it three times running (`ruby scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb`: "evidence does not provide the exact 100% line and region gate") — because it silently expanded the coverage gate's reviewed trust boundary to include a live, network-installed interpreter, exactly what that boundary is reviewed to exclude. Separately, CI evidence from the same push showed `native-build-test`'s Windows leg failing too: `actions/setup-python` only produces a plain `python.exe` on Windows, never a version-suffixed `python3.14`, so the hardcoded oracle lookup in `tests/conformance.rs` (D-085) could not find it there.
- Decision: keep `build-test-coverage`'s coverage step's prefix and script byte-for-byte identical to `TRUSTED_COVERAGE_STEPS`/`COVERAGE_SCRIPT` — no oracle exposure inside the isolated sandbox at all. Mark both conformance tests `#[ignore]` by default (reason string on the attribute) so `cargo llvm-cov --workspace` — which the untouched coverage step still runs unparameterized — skips them cleanly; this costs nothing against the 100% gate because `tests/` files are already excluded from the coverage denominator (`docs/TESTING.md`'s "Code coverage" section predates this PR). The CPython oracle setup step and an explicit `cargo test --workspace -- --include-ignored` step move to *after* the coverage step — a region `coverage_gate_present?` does not constrain, since it validates only the step prefix through and including the coverage step, never what follows — so the conformance tests still execute for real, on every Tier-1 target, but never inside the coverage gate's reviewed, sandboxed boundary. `native-build-test`'s 4 legs (no coverage requirement, no structural step-prefix check) get the same oracle-setup-then-`--include-ignored` shape for consistency, plus a Windows-only step that copies the installed `python.exe` to a `python3.14.exe` alias immediately after `actions/setup-python`, so `tests/conformance.rs`'s one hardcoded lookup name keeps working uniformly across all 5 targets rather than special-casing the Rust lookup itself for one platform. That alias step resolves its source directory via `$pythonLocation` (the exact directory `actions/setup-python` itself exports and prepends to the real Windows `PATH`), converted with `cygpath -u` for bash's `cp` -- an earlier version used bash's own `command -v python` and failed in real CI, because Git Bash's own `PATH` view can be reordered relative to the real Windows `PATH` that the job's later `pwsh`-run steps (and the Rust test binary's own `Command::new` PATH search) actually search, so a bash-resolved "python" was not guaranteed to be the interpreter those later steps would find. The resulting whole-file digest is added to `REVIEWED_PERF_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256S` as `D80_CONFORMANCE_ORACLE_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256`, retiring `D62_REPLICATED_SOURCE_AWARE_PERF_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256` the same way `D51`/`D56` were retired before it (comment marked, dropped from the active allowlist, its fixture and structural-acceptance test kept as a historical audit fixture); a matching `tests/fixtures/d80-conformance-oracle-ci.yml` snapshot and `scripts/test_check_roadmap_evidence.rb` updates ship in the same commit. The checker's own literal error string is corrected from "D-062" to "D-080" to keep naming the workflow content it is actually anchored to. **Addendum, confirmed by real CI evidence after the alias landed:** the `$pythonLocation`/`cygpath` alias alone was not sufficient. Both bash-run steps (the alias `cp` itself and "Verify oracle version") found and ran `python3.14` fine, but the Rust test binary's own `Command::new("python3.14")` still failed with "program not found" — because `std::process::Command` on Windows resolves a bare program name by searching `PATH` for that literal file name and does not append `.exe` when the name already contains a `.` (the version dot in "python3.14" reads as an extension marker to that resolver, unlike bash's own exec resolution, which does try appending `.exe`). Same file, same `PATH`, two different resolvers. `tests/conformance.rs::oracle_python_bin` therefore requests `python3.14.exe` explicitly under `cfg!(windows)` (D-080 addendum), leaving the alias step exactly as-is — it already produces that exact file name.
- Alternatives: install the oracle inside the coverage step's own script and update `COVERAGE_SCRIPT`/`TRUSTED_COVERAGE_STEPS` to match (rejected — the heaviest possible trust-anchor change, expanding the isolated sandbox's surface to include a live network-fetched interpreter, for a correctness check that has no need to run inside that specific measured boundary at all). Add a `--ignore-filename-regex` exemption for `tests/conformance.rs` to `docs/TESTING.md`'s coverage exemption table (rejected as redundant and still just as heavy — `tests/` files are already excluded from the denominator by existing project convention, so an explicit regex entry would be a no-op that only adds confusion, and it would still require editing `COVERAGE_COMMAND`/`COVERAGE_SCRIPT`, the exact change this decision avoids). Rely on the CI-side alias alone, keeping `tests/conformance.rs`'s oracle lookup as one hardcoded, platform-uniform name (originally rejected the per-platform-lookup alternative on exactly this uniformity ground) — **superseded by the addendum above**: real CI evidence showed the alias alone cannot work, because Rust's Windows program-name resolution does not perform the extension-appending search bash performs, so D-085's single-hardcoded-name ideal does not survive Windows regardless of what the CI alias does. The lookup now takes the platform-specific extension explicitly; the alias step is unchanged.
- Consequences: `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb`'s `TRUSTED_COVERAGE_STEPS`/`COVERAGE_SCRIPT` constants and the coverage job's actual steps stay byte-for-byte aligned with the pre-PR-6 reviewed content; only the SHA256 allowlist gains one new active entry, with the prior one retired rather than deleted. `tests/conformance.rs`'s two tests require `-- --include-ignored` (or `--ignored`) to actually execute; a bare `cargo test`/`cargo llvm-cov` invocation silently skips them, matching the file's own doc comment. This PR's originally-planned D-080 slot (reserved in the implementation plan for Task 8's CLI_SPEC.md diagnostic-example fix) is renumbered to D-082, since this decision claimed D-080 first while resolving Task 6's CI failure, and a concurrent `main` PR claimed D-081 for its own unrelated iEvo-lifecycle hardening before this branch next merged it in. **Staging note:** `.github/workflows/workflow-policy.yml`'s `audit` job checks out `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb` at `ref: ${{ github.sha }}` under `pull_request_target`, which resolves to the base branch's (`main`'s) HEAD, never the PR branch's — so a single PR that both edits `ci.yml` and teaches the checker its new digest can never pass its own audit, since the checker used is always `main`'s prior version, which does not yet recognize the new digest. This branch's digest was therefore first staged onto `main` alongside D-062 by a separate pull request (#159, following this project's established `a0a4c47`/`b6f5a29` two-PR stage-then-activate precedent for the D-062 transition), reverting the checker's error string to a generic non-decision-specific message on that stage branch; only after #159 merged did this branch's own commit retire D-062 and make D-080 the sole active entry, matching the digest #159 had already authorized.

## D-081: Harden the project-local iEvo lifecycle boundary

- Status: accepted (strengthens D-077 after adversarial review)
- Context: implementing D-077 exposed additional ways that local shell syntax, conflicting intent fields, filesystem indirection, process interruption, or concurrent lifecycle invocations could make a destructive disable ambiguous. The original symmetric ownership decision remains valid, but its first implementation needs a stricter operational boundary. Arbitrary editors and upstream iEvo processes do not participate in a repository helper's advisory lock, and portable filesystems do not provide an atomic compare-and-swap replacement for a JSON path, so the helper must not claim that it can prevent every uncooperative external-write race.
- Decision: retain D-077's ownership and symmetry, while making `scripts/manage_ievo_hooks.py` fail closed on any supported shell/command form that can lexically reconstruct the managed subtree (including POSIX within-component escapes, unmodeled PowerShell call/control/method expressions, and ambiguous Windows DOS 8.3 components), incomplete or conflicting corrections-only intent before every lifecycle mutation, symlink/reparse/mount/device redirection, non-regular removal targets, traversal errors, or effective-ignore exceptions. Serialize cooperating helper invocations with one crash-released per-worktree OS advisory lock whose unresolved directory components, path entry, and opened descriptor are checked against link/reparse/type redirection; a non-git invocation keeps its fallback inside the validated root rather than shared temporary storage. During disable, snapshot configuration bytes and generated/vendor identities, reject external configuration changes observed immediately before each replacement, reparse written configuration, repeat ignore, identity, and complete ancestry validation before deletion, revalidate ancestry again before every individual unlink/rmdir, unlink only the validated regular files, and remove validated vendor directories deepest-first without `rmtree`. The byte comparison is a best-effort external-change detector, not an atomic CAS against an editor or upstream process that writes after the comparison; callers must not edit, regenerate configuration, or relocate managed filesystem ancestors concurrently with the lifecycle command. A Windows-only Rust integration harness runs the helper and policy-parser regressions inside the required native Windows matrix so reparse, advisory-lock, and DOS-short-name branches are not silently skipped by the macOS discovery run without changing D-062's byte-pinned workflow.
- Alternatives: treat every unknown reference as unrelated (rejected because a false negative can leave a dangling hook); recursively delete the known directory after a shallow check (rejected because late entries and redirected descendants enlarge the destructive scope); persist a PID lock file (rejected because crashes strand stale ownership); claim the advisory lock protects arbitrary tools (rejected because they do not acquire it); add platform-specific filesystem transactions (rejected because they are not portable across the Tier-1 lifecycle clients and still cannot coordinate unmodified external writers).
- Consequences: helper-to-helper operations are serialized and crash recovery needs no stale-lock override. External changes visible at the pre-replacement check abort safely, but an uncooperative writer in the remaining check-to-replace window can still be overwritten; likewise, portable path-based deletion narrows but cannot atomically eliminate a malicious check-to-operation ancestor race. These limitations are explicit and tested behavior must not be described as atomic concurrency protection. Focused tests cover matching/conflicting intent duplicates, shell/path/PowerShell-expression and DOS short-name aliases, ordinary/linked-worktree/root-local fallback lock paths plus redirected gitdir/lock entries, reparse and mount rejection, observed configuration edits, generated-file/vendor/ancestor snapshot drift, nested deepest-first vendor removal, and repeat-safe cleanup.

## D-082: Strip CPython's Windows-only newline translation before the conformance byte-for-byte comparison

- Status: accepted
- Context: an adversarial review of Task 6's CI-wiring fix (D-080) confirmed a real bug the review's own security lens didn't catch: once the fix makes `native-build-test`'s Windows leg actually resolve and run the CPython oracle (instead of panicking on a missing `python3.14`, D-080's own bug), `tests/conformance.rs`'s raw `assert_eq!(pycc_stdout, cpython_stdout)` would fail on essentially every line boundary. CPython's `sys.stdout` is a `TextIOWrapper` opened with `newline=None` on Windows, which translates every `\n` written by `print()` into `\r\n` at the io layer -- and this happens even when stdout is piped/redirected to a child-process capture (confirmed via CPython's own bug tracker: bpo-11990 "redirected output - stdout writes newline as \n in windows" and bpo-13119 "Newline for print() is \n on Windows, and not \r\n as expected," both resolved as intentional, stable since 3.2.4/3.3, and reproduced there with `subprocess.check_output`, the same capture shape `tests/conformance.rs` uses via `Command::output()`). `pycc_rt`'s `print()` is implemented with Rust's `println!`/`print!` macros, which never perform platform-specific newline translation on any target. This translation is a quirk of CPython's own C runtime's stdio layer, not part of the Python language's `print()` semantics -- nothing in the language specifies that `\n` becomes `\r\n` on any platform.
- Decision: add `tests/conformance.rs::strip_windows_newline_translation`, applied to the CPython oracle's captured stdout only (never to pycc's own output, which needs no correction) before the byte-for-byte comparison. It removes every `\r` immediately followed by `\n`, leaving any other byte -- including a lone `\r` not followed by `\n` -- untouched. This runs unconditionally on every platform (not gated behind `cfg!(windows)`), since it is a no-op on platforms where CPython never inserts the `\r` in the first place, keeping the function and its regression test (`strip_windows_newline_translation_removes_cr_before_lf_only`) simple and uniformly exercised everywhere rather than only under a Windows-only test path.
- Alternatives: reconfigure the fixture `.py` files themselves (e.g. `sys.stdout.reconfigure(newline='\n')`) to suppress CPython's translation at the source (rejected -- this adds Windows-specific plumbing into fixtures that are meant to be plain, portable Python programs, and D-085/Task 4/5 already fixed these fixtures' exact source; changing them again for an unrelated platform-io concern is out of scope and would need re-verifying their already-reviewed byte-exact provenance). Normalize both sides symmetrically, e.g. via a general `\r\n`/`\r`/`\n`-to-`\n` line-ending canonicalization (rejected as broader than necessary -- pycc_rt provably never emits `\r` on any target, so a one-directional, narrowly-targeted strip is simpler to reason about and cannot silently mask a genuine `\r` byte pycc itself produced). Skip or gate the conformance tests on Windows entirely (rejected -- defeats Task 6's own stated goal of running the real oracle diff on every Tier-1 target, and CPython's line-ending translation is well-understood, stable, decade-old behavior, not a source of genuine platform ambiguity).
- Consequences: the byte-for-byte comparison now checks program-output content across all 5 Tier-1 targets uniformly, independent of which OS's stdio layer produced the oracle's bytes. A future third conformance fixture needs no special Windows handling of its own -- `run_conformance_fixture` applies the strip for every caller. This PR's originally-planned D-080 slot for Task 8's CLI_SPEC.md fix, already renumbered once to D-081 and then to D-082 by two prior concurrent-ID collisions, is renumbered again to D-083, since this decision claimed D-082 first while closing out Task 6's review findings.

## D-083: Correct CLI_SPEC.md's diagnostic example to match reality instead of building new rendering features

- Status: accepted
- Context: `docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md` row 6's third acceptance bullet is "diagnostic output matches CLI_SPEC.md's example byte-for-byte." `docs/CLI_SPEC.md`'s example before this decision showed a short, message-independent caret label (`expected \`int\``), a populated `= help: did you mean \`int("35")\`?` line, and a precise span (`src/main.py:5:15`, a 4-column caret under `"35"`) -- none of which the compiler produces today. The implementation plan that authorized this decision assumed `crates/pycc_diag/src/lib.rs`'s existing `render_human_matches_cli_spec_format` unit test proved the real end-to-end shape; it does not -- that test hand-constructs a `Diagnostic` with a genuine `Span::new(26, 30)` and feeds it straight to `render_human`, exercising the *renderer's* capability to place a caret under an arbitrary span, never the type checker that actually produces `T0021` diagnostics. Running `pycc check` against a fully annotated fib-style fixture (`def fib(n: int) -> int: ...` called with a `str` argument) shows the real output is `error[T0021]: argument 1 of \`fib\` expects \`int\`, got \`str\`` at ` --> <path>:1:1` with a single-character caret on line 1 -- because every `T0021` (and every other `T0xxx`) diagnostic in `pycc_types` still constructs its `Span` as the `Span::new(0, 0)` placeholder (confirmed: all 19 `Span::new` call sites in `crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs` use the placeholder; every existing `tests/diagnostics/*.expected.txt` fixture for a `T0xxx` code already bakes in `:1:1` with a one-character caret, e.g. `d0021_range_argument_type.expected.txt`). This is D-043's own third named gap ("every diagnostic's `Span` is still the `Span::new(0, 0)` placeholder"), not a new discovery -- D-043 already directs that `tests/diagnostics/` fixtures bake in the placeholder location "for exactly this reason." The caret label repeating the full diagnostic message (rather than an independent short label like `expected \`int\``) is also current, unavoidable behavior: `Diagnostic::error` always sets `label: Some(message.clone())`, and no call site anywhere in the workspace ever supplies an independent label.
- Decision: rewrite `docs/CLI_SPEC.md`'s example to the real, current end-to-end `pycc check` output for a `T0021` argument-type mismatch: full-message caret label, the `Span::new(0, 0)` placeholder location (`line 1, column 1`, one-character caret), and no help line. Add a checked-in `tests/diagnostics/cli_spec_example.py` (a fully annotated `fib`-style function called with a mismatched-type argument) + `.expected.txt` pair that runs the real compiler through this project's existing `tests/diagnostics/*.py`+`*.expected.txt` fixture convention (`tests/diagnostics_test.rs`'s explicit per-fixture `#[test]` registration) and proves the corrected example matches byte-for-byte -- except the displayed file path, which the test harness's own established convention (`assert_diagnostic_matches_fixture`, confirmed against every existing sibling fixture) always renders as the fixture's real repo-relative path (`tests/diagnostics/cli_spec_example.py`), not CLI_SPEC.md's illustrative `src/main.py` stand-in filename. `crates/pycc_diag/src/lib.rs`'s `render_human_matches_cli_spec_format` unit test is left unchanged: it is a renderer-capability regression test (and, per its own doc comment inside `render_human`, the only test exercising the multi-character-caret branch), not a claim about the checker's current behavior, and changing it risks the D-014 100% coverage gate for no benefit to this decision.
- Alternatives: implement real per-node span threading through HIR, independent short caret labels, and populated `help:` suggestions now, so the *original* aspirational example becomes true (rejected -- real spans are D-043's own explicitly deferred, PR-sized follow-up ("a future PR ... should thread real spans through HIR"), and short labels plus help population are separate, undersized-for-this-PR feature work; none of it belongs in a PR titled "Conformance + benchmark gate"). Codify `render_human_matches_cli_spec_format`'s hand-fed-span rendering as the documented "reality" and add a fixture that exercises `render_human` directly with a synthetic `Diagnostic` instead of the real CLI (rejected -- every existing `tests/diagnostics/` fixture drives the real `pycc check` binary end-to-end; a fixture that instead hand-constructs a `Diagnostic` bypassing the type checker would prove only the renderer's capability, which the existing unit test already covers, not that the CLI's actual, user-visible output matches CLI_SPEC.md, which is DELIVERY_PLAN.md row 6's actual acceptance bullet). Leave CLI_SPEC.md's example as aspirational prose with no enforcing fixture (rejected -- this project's own standing convention is that every normative documentation claim should be enforceable by a test where practical, and an unenforced, currently-false "byte-for-byte" claim in a public spec file is exactly the kind of stale-docs risk AGENTS.md warns against).
- Consequences: `docs/CLI_SPEC.md`'s example is now truthful and permanently checked against the real compiler, including its currently-imprecise placeholder span -- readers see, in the spec itself, that today's caret points at a fixed placeholder location rather than the real argument, matching D-043's already-documented gap rather than contradicting it. D-043 remains the tracked, correct place for the real-span, short-label, and help-population feature work, whenever it happens; once real spans land, this fixture must be regenerated by re-running `pycc check` and re-copying its stdout (per D-043's own consequence), not hand-edited to guess the new span.

## D-084: `pycc check`'s throughput-floor CI step runs after `cargo test`, not right after `cargo build`

- Status: accepted
- Context: Task 7 wires `scripts/check_frontend_throughput.rb` (D-079) into `build-test-coverage`'s CI job, invoking the freshly built `target/debug/pycc` binary. A Task-7 implementer subagent's local testing found the exact measurement flaky on its dev host: 3 of 6 fresh-shell trials of `ruby scripts/check_frontend_throughput.rb target/debug/pycc tests/fixtures/pr6_1000_loc_bench.py 50` reported multi-second failures (2481ms/2853ms/2712ms) that `/usr/bin/time -p` proved were not `pycc`'s own compute -- one cold run split as `real 2.71s` vs `user 0.01s`/`sys 0.01s`, meaning over 99% of the wall time was spent in process-spawn/exec overhead (likely first-exec code-signature validation of the freshly-linked, ad-hoc-signed binary) before `pycc check` itself ran at all. Steady-state (warmed) runs were consistently ~15ms, comfortably under the 50ms floor -- the anomaly is a one-time cold-start cost, not a `pycc check` regression.
- Decision: place the new CI step immediately after the job's existing `cargo test --workspace (incl. ignored conformance tests, D-078)` step, not immediately after `cargo build --workspace`. `cargo test` already execs the same freshly-linked binary via existing integration tests, so any one-time first-exec cold-start cost lands there instead of inside the throughput measurement, keeping the measurement itself representative of `pycc check`'s own steady-state speed.
- Alternatives: place the step directly after `cargo build --workspace`, per the implementation plan's original Step 6 wording (rejected -- this would make the throughput check itself the first exec of the freshly-linked binary in the job, risking exactly the cold-start flakiness the implementer observed locally, on a check whose entire purpose is measuring `pycc check`'s own steady-state speed). Add a dedicated warm-up exec of the binary before measuring (rejected as unnecessary complexity -- the job already execs the binary via `cargo test`'s integration tests immediately before this step, so a separate warm-up step would be redundant).
- Consequences: `scripts/check_frontend_throughput.rb` and `tests/fixtures/pr6_1000_loc_bench.py` are unchanged from Task 7's implementer subagent's work; only the step's position in `ci.yml` differs from the plan's original Step 6 text. This changes `ci.yml`'s whole-file digest again, so `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb`'s `REVIEWED_PERF_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256S` gains `D84_THROUGHPUT_FLOOR_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256` as its new sole active entry, retiring `D80_CONFORMANCE_ORACLE_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256` the same way `D51`/`D56`/`D62` were retired before it -- staged onto `main` first via a separate stage PR (#173, mirroring D-080's own precedent), since a single PR cannot both change `ci.yml` and teach the base-branch-only `pull_request_target` audit checker its new digest (see D-080's "Staging note").

## D-085: A plain integration test, not a new `pycc_testkit` crate, for PR-6's two conformance fixtures

- Status: accepted (PR-6's own scope decision, per D-057's "simplest correct thing for the stated PR scope" precedent)
- Context: `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` and `docs/SPEC.md` both list `pycc_testkit` as a planned crate ("Conformance/differential test harness," `docs/TESTING.md`'s Layer 2), and D-018/D-037 deferred building it until PR-6 "once there's a PEP matrix for it to check against." PR-6's actual acceptance criterion (`docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md` row 6) is narrow: two named fixtures (fib, mandelbrot-ascii) diffed against a pinned CPython 3.14.6 oracle on 5 targets in `--debug` profile — not the full `py30`–`py315` cumulative-fixture-range matrix `docs/TESTING.md`'s Layer 2 describes for v1.0, which still has no PEP matrix to check against today.
- Decision: implement these two fixtures as a plain `tests/conformance.rs` integration test (compiled and run by `cargo test --workspace` like every other file under `tests/`), using `std::process::Command` to invoke both the freshly-built `pycc` binary and the pinned `python3.14` oracle on identical source, then assert `stdout` is byte-identical. No new workspace member, no new `Cargo.toml`, no new public library API.
- Alternatives: build the full `pycc_testkit` crate now (rejected — there is no PEP matrix yet for a real multi-file-fixture harness to iterate over; a crate with exactly two hardcoded fixtures would be pure ceremony over `tests/conformance.rs`'s equivalent, simpler mechanism, and would need to be substantially reworked anyway once the real v1.0 harness design is known). Extend `tests/slice1_codegen_depth.rs` in place instead of a new file (rejected — that file is PR-5's own codegen-depth suite; a byte-for-byte CPython oracle diff is a distinct concern from "does this compile and run to a plausible result," and deserves its own file per this project's existing per-concern test file split, e.g. `slice0.rs` vs. `diagnostics_test.rs` vs. `slice1_codegen_depth.rs`).
- Consequences: `pycc_testkit` stays absent from the workspace `[members]` list until a PR actually needs its full harness design; `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`'s crate table entry and `docs/SPEC.md`'s `pycc_testkit` references remain forward-looking documentation of a planned crate, not a claim that it exists yet (matching how `pycc_own`/`pycc_std`/`pycc_lexer` are already documented as planned-but-absent). `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s "Quality gates" row's bolded "the conformance harness ... remain planned" sentence is updated in this same PR (Task 9) to say a narrow 2-fixture conformance check is now live via `tests/conformance.rs`, while the full multi-version matrix remains planned. **Renumbering note:** this decision was originally recorded as D-078. A concurrent `main` PR independently claimed D-078 for an unrelated decision (checkpointed external repository monitoring) and merged before this branch did; since that decision is already accepted on `main`, this one is renumbered to D-085 instead, per this project's established convention of renumbering the unmerged branch's own decision rather than rewriting an already-accepted one. Every reference to this decision elsewhere in this PR's own text (D-080/D-082/D-084's bodies, `tests/conformance.rs`'s doc comment, the implementation plan, `docs/ROADMAP.md`, `docs/SPEC.md`) is updated to say D-085, except the already-digest-pinned `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s own comments, which still literally say "D-078" -- those bytes are frozen by `D84_THROUGHPUT_FLOOR_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256`'s already-reviewed pin, and editing them would invalidate an already-authorized digest for a cosmetic rename alone.

## D-086: Two type-boundary strictness decisions -- equality confirms, numeric widening exceeds, `mypy --strict`

- Status: accepted
- Context: two independent read-only frontend audits (#116, #122) each found a case where `pycc check` rejects standard, unambiguous Python that CPython accepts: `1 == "1"` (#116, `crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs`'s `HirExpr::Compare` arm reuses `numeric_or_bool_compatible` for `Eq`/`NotEq` and ordering alike) and `identity(1)` for `def identity(value: float) -> float` (#122, `is_assignable` in the same file only permits exact-type equality plus `bool`→`int`). `docs/TYPE_SYSTEM.md`'s own contract states "checking is strict -- what mypy calls `--strict` is pycc's only mode." Empirically verifying both cases against `mypy` 2.3.0 settles which side of that contract each one falls on: `mypy --strict` (specifically its `--strict-equality`/`comparison-overlap` check) rejects `1 == "1"` exactly as pycc does today, but `mypy --strict` *accepts* `identity(1)` unconditionally -- the Python typing spec's numeric-tower special case ("an `int` is acceptable where `float` is annotated") is not gated behind any mypy strictness flag, because rejecting it would make a type checker non-conformant with the spec it implements, not merely stricter. Ordering operators (`<`/`>`/`<=`/`>=`) between incompatible types are untouched by this decision in either direction: CPython itself raises `TypeError` for these at runtime, so pycc rejecting them at compile time was never in question (both issues' own "control case" reproductions confirm this).
- Decision: keep both diagnostics as hard `T0021` compile errors, unchanged from current behavior, and record the rationale explicitly rather than leave it an undocumented accident of `numeric_or_bool_compatible`/`is_assignable`'s shared implementation:
  1. `==`/`!=` keep using `numeric_or_bool_compatible`'s existing rule: operands are comparable if both are in the numeric-like group (`int`/`float`/`bool`, interchangeably -- so `1 == 2.5` and `True == 1` both type-check as `bool`) or both `str`. This is *not* the same, stricter relation `is_assignable` enforces at parameter/return/assignment boundaries (exact type, plus `bool`→`int` only) -- it is the same relation ordering operators already use. Confirming this rejects genuinely incompatible pairs (`int`/`str`) is not a new deviation -- it is `docs/TYPE_SYSTEM.md`'s already-declared "mypy --strict is our only mode" contract, made explicit, since neither the spec nor the deviation ledger previously named this case (#116's own finding).
  2. Numeric widening (`int` accepted where `float` is annotated) at a parameter, return, or annotated-assignment boundary requires an explicit conversion rather than an implicit one. This *does* go beyond `mypy --strict`, deliberately: pycc's own runtime representation makes `int`→`float` a real, non-trivial bit-level conversion at every crossing (D-001/D-061's tagged 63-bit fixnum vs. a native, unboxed `f64`, per `docs/TYPE_SYSTEM.md`'s representation table), not a free reinterpretation the way CPython's uniform object model makes it look. Requiring the conversion to be visible in source, rather than silently inserted by the compiler at a boundary, matches this project's general preference for explicit over implicit (the same reasoning already applied to `Any`, rule 3, and implicit narrowing, rule 4). **This decision's remedy is not yet usable**: pycc has no callable `float(...)` (or any other numeric-conversion builtin) today -- `HirExpr::Call`'s type-checking (both `infer_expr_in` and the private-helper inference path `collect_expr_constraints` in `crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs`) only special-cases `callee == "print"`, so `float(x)` itself produces `T0021: call to undefined function `float``. Implementing it is tracked as separate follow-up work in #181, reusing the existing `int_to_float`/`pycc_rt_int_to_float` runtime conversion (`crates/pycc_rt/src/lib.rs`) and the existing `to_float` codegen helper (`crates/pycc_codegen/src/lib.rs`) that already promotes `int`/`bool`/`float` scalars for arithmetic-operator promotion. Until #181 lands, an `int` value cannot actually be moved across a `float`-typed boundary in pycc at all; this decision fixes what the *rule* should be, not that a conversion path already exists. No `Severity::Warning` path exists anywhere in the compiler today (`pycc_diag`'s `Severity::Warning` variant is exercised only by that crate's own unit tests, under a placeholder `W1001` code; no production diagnostic uses it, and no exit-code logic in `src/main.rs` distinguishes severities) -- introducing one for this single case, where the only sensible non-blocking behavior would be to silently perform the conversion anyway, would both require new infrastructure and undermine the explicitness this decision exists to enforce.
  3. Arithmetic-operator promotion is explicitly out of scope and unaffected: `1 + 2.5` still computes a fresh `float` result via `numeric_result_type`'s existing operator-level rules -- that is a new value produced by an operation, not an existing value re-typed across a boundary, and no accepted decision (D-001/D-058/D-061) or `docs/ROADMAP.md` text governing it is touched here.
- Alternatives: accept both as CPython/typing-spec compatibility (the issues' own proposals) -- rejected for #122 specifically per this decision's rationale above; for #116, rejecting is actually the status quo consistent with the project's own stated contract, so "accepting" would have been the deviation, not the fix. Add a genuine warning severity and silently promote `int` to `float` at boundaries -- rejected: no warning infrastructure exists, building it for one case is disproportionate, and silent promotion defeats the purpose (a warning nobody reads while the compiler still does the implicit thing is not meaningfully different from no diagnostic at all). Split equality and ordering into fully separate compatibility functions even though they now enforce the same rule -- rejected as unnecessary: the current shared `numeric_or_bool_compatible` already produces the correct answer for both under this decision, so splitting it would add indirection with no behavioral benefit.
- Consequences: no production code changes -- `numeric_or_bool_compatible` and `is_assignable` are correct as they stand. `crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs` gains one new regression test, `an_int_argument_for_a_float_parameter_is_a_clean_error`, alongside the pre-existing `comparing_a_string_and_an_int_is_a_clean_type_error`, so both halves of this decision have an explicit, named regression rather than resting on incidental coverage. `docs/TYPE_SYSTEM.md` gains an explicit strictness rule for equality-vs-ordering and rewrites rule 4 from "no implicit numeric narrowing" to "no implicit numeric narrowing or widening" without naming `float(...)` as a working remedy, closing the gap both issues' own audits found in the specification.
