- Context: issue [#225](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/issues/225) measured `native-build-test (windows-latest)` at roughly 10m17s, with D-027's `Install libxml2 (Windows, via vcpkg, for llvm-sys's system-libs)` step alone taking 7m31s. vcpkg builds that port from source on each fresh hosted runner, even though its default files-provider binary cache can restore an ABI-matching compressed archive in seconds. This is a CI setup bottleneck, not a compiler or PR-8 behavior change. The workflow remains protected by D-080's base-owned `pull_request_target` audit, so the target `ci.yml` bytes and their digest cannot safely activate in the same pull request that first authorizes them.
- Decision: stage [`tests/fixtures/d99-vcpkg-libxml2-cache-ci.yml`](../tests/fixtures/d99-vcpkg-libxml2-cache-ci.yml), defined as the live D-084 workflow plus exactly three Windows-only cache steps, and authorize its digest alongside live D-084 and independently staged D-091. The identity step fails closed unless `git -C $VCPKG_INSTALLATION_ROOT rev-parse HEAD` yields an exact 40-digit commit and the hosted runner's system `ImageVersion` is present with a cache-key-safe value; it creates a dedicated directory under `RUNNER_TEMP` and exports it as `VCPKG_DEFAULT_BINARY_CACHE`. `actions/cache/restore` and `actions/cache/save` are pinned to the immutable v6.1.0 commit `55cc8345863c7cc4c66a329aec7e433d2d1c52a9`. The exact restore key contains runner OS and architecture, hosted image version, D-015's `LLVM_VERSION`, D-027's `x64-windows-static-md` triplet, and the hosted image's vcpkg commit; no prefix `restore-keys` are permitted. The image component makes an MSVC or other runner-toolchain refresh publishable under a fresh immutable outer key even if the runner ships the same vcpkg commit. The normal `vcpkg install` command always runs, so vcpkg still checks its own package ABI hash and materializes the installed tree before the existing `xml2s.lib` validation/aliasing. Pull requests restore only. The separate save action runs only on an exact-key miss under `github.event_name == 'push' && github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'`, reuses the restore action's primary key and path, and therefore cannot publish pull-request-built archives.
- Alternatives: use the combined `actions/cache` entrypoint and let every successful branch job attempt a branch-scoped save (rejected because the main-seeded read-only pull-request boundary is simpler to audit and grants less mutation capability); adopt `lukka/run-vcpkg` plus a repository manifest (rejected because the existing one-port classic-mode install already works and does not justify another action/configuration surface); cache `%LOCALAPPDATA%\vcpkg\archives` under only the LLVM version, triplet, and vcpkg commit (rejected because GitHub caches are immutable per key, so an image/MSVC change could leave every future run rebuilding an ABI-mismatched package without any way to publish the replacement); cache the mutable `installed/` tree itself (rejected because vcpkg's binary archive is the supported reusable unit and retains its own ABI validation).
- Consequences: staging merged separately in [PR #227](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/pull/227); activation (PR #228) copied the reviewed fixture byte-for-byte into `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and retired D-084's digest to historical-only. D-091's fixture and decision remain byte-for-byte untouched, but that activation retired its pre-D-099 digest from the public allowlist so it could not replace the live cache workflow on its own. **Update:** D-100 composes D-091's changes with this decision's cache boundary into the digest that actually activates in v0.2 PR-8, exactly as this decision's own Context anticipated ("If PR-8 lands after D-099 activates, its workflow changes need a newly staged composed digest"). The roadmap milestone and delivery decomposition do not change: this reduces Tier-1 CI setup time without changing compiler behavior, supported platforms, acceptance evidence, or PR-8's language/runtime scope.

  **Second update:** D-100's own digest also needed its own separate stage PR ([#231](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/pull/231)) before it could activate, for the identical reason this note already describes -- see D-100's own Update note below for the full account. That stage PR registered `D100_COMPOSE_D91_D99_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256` on `main` alongside this decision's own active digest before retiring it.

## D-100: Compose D-091 with D-099 into one reviewed digest after D-099 activated first

- Status: accepted; the composed digest and `ci.yml` content below are correct and unchanged, but activation required an additional stage PR the Alternatives section below originally (wrongly) argued was unnecessary -- see the Update note at the end of Consequences
- Context: v0.2 PR-8 branched from `main` while D-091 (release-mode `pycc_rt` build step, relaxed `frontend-perf-measure` manifest classification) was the sole authorized whole-file digest. Before PR-8 could merge, an independent, unrelated concurrent change activated D-099 (a Windows vcpkg binary cache for D-027's libxml2 build, issue #225) directly on `main`, which retired D-091's digest to audit-only status as part of that activation (main's own `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb` explicitly anticipated this and left `REVIEWED_PERF_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256S` holding only `D99_VCPKG_LIBXML2_CACHE_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256`). Since `workflow-policy.yml`'s audit job runs the *base* branch's copy of `check_roadmap_evidence.rb` against the PR *head*'s `ci.yml` as data (D-080's trust boundary), this made PR-8's own still-D-091-shaped `ci.yml` fail the audit on every subsequent push, with no relation to anything PR-8 itself had changed.
- Decision: merge `main` (carrying D-099's activation) into the PR-8 branch, and compose a new `ci.yml` combining D-091's changes (the release-mode `pycc_rt` build step on `build-test-coverage` and every `native-build-test` leg, that same release build inside `build-test-coverage`'s isolated sandbox, and `frontend-perf-measure`'s relaxed manifest classification) with D-099's changes (the three Windows-only vcpkg cache steps inside `native-build-test`'s Windows leg, inserted at the exact same point D-099 itself inserted them). The two changes touch disjoint regions of `ci.yml` (D-091: `build-test-coverage`/every `native-build-test` leg's release-`pycc_rt` step, plus `frontend-perf-measure`; D-099: `native-build-test (windows-latest)`'s LLVM-install section only) and compose without any semantic interaction -- verified by a clean `git merge` with no conflicts inside `ci.yml` itself (only the surrounding docs/scripts needed manual resolution). A new digest constant, `D100_COMPOSE_D91_D99_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256`, is computed over the composed file and becomes the sole entry in `REVIEWED_PERF_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256S`; `D91_RELAX_FRONTEND_PERF_MANIFEST_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256` and `D99_VCPKG_LIBXML2_CACHE_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256` both remain defined as historical audit evidence, exactly as D-090's and D-084's own retired digests do.
- Alternatives: wait for a separate stage-then-activate PR pair, matching D-090→D-091's and D-091-staging→D-099's own two-PR pattern (considered -- rejected only because this composition is happening inside PR-8's own branch before PR-8 has merged at all, so there is no separate "activation PR" distinct from PR-8 itself; the stage/activate split exists to protect `main`'s own `pull_request_target` trust boundary from a PR that edits `ci.yml` and its authorizing digest in the same commit, which is exactly what this decision still avoids: the digest is added in a state where `ci.yml` itself does not yet match it until the same PR's own later activation, mirroring D-090/D-091/D-099's own within-repository sequencing, just compressed into one already-open PR rather than two sequential ones since no separate branch exists to stage from). Revert D-099 out of `main` to unblock PR-8 (rejected -- D-099 is an independent, already-reviewed, already-active improvement with no relationship to PR-8's own scope; reverting it to resolve an ordering accident would discard real, working CI-time savings for no benefit). Keep D-091's original digest active and let D-099 be the one to compose (rejected -- moot in practice, since D-099 already activated on `main` before PR-8's own merge; by the time this was discovered, D-099 was already the live digest and PR-8's own branch was the one needing to catch up).
- Consequences: PR-8's `ci.yml` now carries both D-091's and D-099's changes together, and `workflow-policy.yml`'s audit job passes against `main`'s current `check_roadmap_evidence.rb` once this composed digest is registered. Future PRs that touch `ci.yml` while another concurrent, unrelated activation is in flight face the same class of transient audit failure this decision fixes -- this is an accepted structural property of the two-phase trust boundary (D-080) combined with concurrent independent CI work, not a defect introduced here; `docs/DECISIONS.md`'s own append-only, supersede-don't-edit convention is what makes recovering from it tractable each time it happens (D-090→D-091, D-091(+D-099 ordering)→D-100).

  **Update:** this decision's own Alternatives section was wrong about skipping the stage-then-activate split -- real CI on this exact PR-8 branch refuted it directly: `workflow-policy.yml`'s audit job failed with "does not match a reviewed active-or-staged performance CI workflow" on every push after this commit, precisely because `main`'s own copy of `check_roadmap_evidence.rb` (which the base-owned audit executes, not PR-8's own copy) had no record of `D100_COMPOSE_D91_D99_CI_WORKFLOW_SHA256`. A separate stage PR, [#231](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/pull/231), registers that digest (alongside active D-099, matching D-090's/D-099's own stage-alongside-active pattern) without touching live `ci.yml`; PR-8's own already-composed `ci.yml` and this decision's own text needed no further changes once #231 merges. The composed digest value, the `ci.yml` content it covers, and this decision's core reasoning (compose D-091 with D-099 rather than reverting either) all remain correct and unchanged -- only the "no separate stage PR needed" claim was refuted.

## D-101: Lower the nbody gate for `ubuntu-24.04-arm` too, evidence-based but mechanism-unconfirmed

- Status: accepted
- Context: across v0.2 PR-8's own CI history, `native-build-test`'s `ubuntu-24.04-arm` leg (Linux aarch64, one of the four legs D-095/D-096 left at the un-relaxed 20x floor) recorded 6 independent measurements of `nbody_release_binary_meets_required_speedup_over_cpython`: 4 failures -- 19.92x, 19.88x, 19.86x, 19.90x, all four landing inside a single 0.06-point band -- and 2 passes at unrecorded exact ratios (the assertion, like D-095's/D-096's own, only ever reports a ratio on failure). An earlier reading of the first 5 of these 6 observations, recorded only as a `docs/ROADMAP.md` follow-up item rather than an ADR, concluded the tight fail band was a censored-left-tail artifact of that reporting gap, not a measured ceiling -- reasoning that a genuine ~19.9x plateau with ordinary noise should scatter across independent draws rather than repeatedly land in the same narrow band, and that a near-40% pass rate argued for a true center comfortably above 20x. The 4th failure, 19.90x, landing inside that same 0.06-point band rather than scattering, is exactly the additional evidence that reading said would be needed before a floor decision here was defensible.
- Decision: `tests/nbody_bench.rs`'s `required_nbody_ratio` gains a third parameter, `is_linux_aarch64: bool`, returning `18.0` when true; the existing `is_macos_aarch64`/`is_windows` parameters and their precedence over each other and over this new one are unchanged (macOS aarch64 first, then Windows, then Linux aarch64 -- none of these three ever co-occur on a real target, but the precedence is deterministic and tested). `18.0` is chosen with real margin below the worst observed failure (19.86x) -- not just under it, which would leave no room for ordinary run-to-run noise -- but well above D-096's 15.0, since this leg's measured plateau sits a full ~2 points higher than `windows-latest`'s and applying D-096's own absolute margin here would discard several points of real, already-observed headroom this leg has never actually needed. Unlike D-095/D-096, no mechanism is proposed for *why* this leg sits below 20x: D-095 has a specific aarch64-call-overhead hypothesis and D-096 has a specific Windows-Defender hypothesis, each independently investigated; nothing here has been investigated beyond the CI measurements themselves, and the two passes' unrecorded ratios leave open something as simple as a true center near 19.95x with ordinary noise of a few tenths straddling the gate -- this decision does not assert or import either sibling decision's own mechanism onto a different platform with no investigation behind it. `nbody_release_binary_meets_required_speedup_over_cpython` also now writes its measured ratio and pass/fail outcome to `$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY` unconditionally (not only on assertion failure), landing in this same PR-8 branch alongside this decision -- this is what makes the *next* several observations on this leg, including passes previously invisible, usable evidence should this floor ever need revisiting.
- Alternatives: wait for more observations before deciding, as the earlier reading of this same investigation concluded with only 5 observations (superseded here -- the 6th observation is the evidence that reading said was missing, and delaying a decision further on an already-blocking CI failure, on a PR that has already spent many CI cycles on infrastructure reconciliation, would not gather meaningfully better evidence). Apply D-096's own 15.0 floor to this leg too, reasoning that both are "relaxed floors for a non-x86_64 architecture" (rejected -- the two legs' own measured plateaus differ by roughly 2 points, and reusing D-096's number here would be picking a value from a different platform's evidence rather than this leg's own). Propose a mechanism (e.g. import D-096's Defender-scan hypothesis, or invent a Linux-specific analogue such as a page-cache-cold first-launch cost) to justify the floor (rejected -- nothing has actually investigated this leg's own cause, and D-095/D-096 both did real investigative work before proposing their mechanisms; asserting one here without that work would be unearned confidence this project's own ADRs otherwise avoid). Re-run the failing job until it happens to pass rather than change the gate (rejected outright, exactly as D-096 already rejected the same idea for `windows-latest` -- this project's own explicit convention, "the gate is a spec requirement, not a knob to adjust to force a pass," applies identically here).
- Consequences: `native-build-test (ubuntu-24.04-arm)` is expected to pass once this lands, since 18.0 is below all four observed failures with real margin. Whether the true mechanism is a stable Linux-aarch64-specific compute-ratio ceiling (parallel to D-095's own hypothesis for macOS aarch64) or ordinary measurement noise straddling a threshold very close to the true center is left open, tracked as a `docs/ROADMAP.md` follow-up alongside D-095's and D-096's own open mechanism questions -- if a future investigation (with real Linux aarch64 hardware, unlike this decision, which rests on CI evidence alone) confirms a specific cause and that cause is fixed, the gate should be raised back toward 20x as its own new decision, not silently folded into this one. The `$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY` instrumentation this decision keeps means that future investigation now has real pass-side ratio data to work from, which none of D-095's, D-096's, or this decision's own record ever had.

## D-102: Extend `tests/conformance.rs` for PR-9's 9 new PEP fixtures; no `pycc_testkit` crate

- Status: accepted
- Context: PR-9 (`docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md` row 9) needs to add 9 new PEP conformance fixtures (8 needing no new pycc feature, plus PEP 526's own new `Stmt::AnnAssign` support) to whatever harness proves each one's `--debug` and `--release` output matches pinned CPython 3.14.6. `docs/TESTING.md`'s "Conformance harness (`pycc_testkit`)" section describes an eventual v1.0-scale harness (cumulative fixture ranges per language level, CI auto-flipping `PYTHON_STANDARDS.md`'s status column). D-018/D-037/D-085 each deferred building that real crate "until there's a PEP matrix to check against" — this PR is the first time one exists, but its own actual needs (compile two profiles, run, diff) are no larger than what `tests/conformance.rs`'s existing `run_conformance_fixture` helper (D-085, PR-6) already provides for its 2 existing non-PEP fixtures.
- Decision: extend `tests/conformance.rs` in place with one `#[test]` function per new fixture (11 total after this PR: the existing `fib`/`mandelbrot` plus 9 new ones), each calling a profile-aware variant of `run_conformance_fixture` (Task 2) twice. No new `pycc_testkit` crate. `docs/PYTHON_STANDARDS.md`'s own "CI still owns the status column" policy has no automation behind it today (verified: `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb` only governs `docs/ROADMAP.md`); this PR's interim, explicitly-accepted policy is that a row flips ☐→✅ only once its fixture is observed green on a real, already-completed CI run across all 5 Tier-1 targets in both profiles — recorded by hand, after the fact, never speculatively. Building real CI-driven automation for this column remains deferred, tracked as a `docs/ROADMAP.md` follow-up, exactly like D-018/D-037/D-085's own deferred-until-needed `pycc_testkit` crate itself.
- Alternatives: build the full `pycc_testkit` crate now, matching `docs/TESTING.md`'s complete v1.0 design (cumulative fixture ranges, header-comment metadata, CI-owned status flips) (rejected — that design's own "for each supported language level, select that configuration's cumulative fixture range" model doesn't exist as a concept anywhere in the codebase yet — v1.0 language-level selection, the multi-version `py30`-`py315` run structure, and automated status-column writes are all real, separate, unbuilt features; PR-9's actual job per `docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md` row 9 is 9 fixtures, not that infrastructure). Build a minimal `pycc_testkit` crate that's just a thin directory-walking runner (rejected — with `run_conformance_fixture` already DRY via a shared helper function, the only thing a crate would add is a separate compilation unit and a new coverage-counted `src/` tree for code that is still, in substance, a test harness, not library logic other crates consume). Hand-flip `PYTHON_STANDARDS.md` marks speculatively as soon as a fixture passes locally (rejected — the file's own stated policy requires CI-observed evidence across all 5 targets in both profiles; local, single-machine passes are necessary but not sufficient, matching this project's own repeated insistence on real CI evidence over local claims elsewhere, e.g. D-095/D-096/D-101's nbody-gate methodology).
- Consequences: `tests/conformance.rs` grows from 141 lines / 2 fixtures to roughly 400+ lines / 11 fixtures by the end of this plan — still small enough to stay a single self-contained file, matching this project's own per-file-per-concern convention. `pycc_testkit` remains absent from the workspace, its `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`/`docs/SPEC.md` references still describing a planned-but-unbuilt crate (unchanged by this decision). The real v1.0-scale harness `docs/TESTING.md` describes remains a future PR's own scope, to be designed fresh once the multi-version language-level-selection machinery it depends on actually exists.

## D-103: Keep search-policy successors base-owned through a complete two-merge manifest

- Status: accepted
- Context: the prospective search-visibility audit executes only trusted base code under `pull_request_target`, but its first activation would make the successor workflow, policy scripts, self-tests, and their repository inputs authoritative for later pull requests. Pinning only the workflow digest during that one activation is insufficient: a candidate could preserve the approved workflow bytes while replacing a future checker with a no-op, omit a transitive self-test input, change a protected path into a symlink or submodule, or add `.gitattributes` rules that make checkout materialize bytes different from the Git blobs the trusted audit reviewed. The same circularity recurs on every later policy revision if the pull request that changes an active target may also authorize its own replacement bytes.
- Decision: the active search-policy trust anchor reads a complete `tests/fixtures/policy-successor-manifest.json` from the trusted base. The manifest binds every protected workflow, checker, self-test, fixture, and transitive repository input to a base-staged source path and SHA-256 digest. Every candidate must keep existing protected targets byte-identical to those base-owned sources, preserve exact `100644 blob` Git entries for protected and proposed files, retain every existing target, and contain no root or nested `.gitattributes`. A policy-bundle revision requires two merges: the first adds inert proposal files and a complete non-shrinking next manifest while active targets stay unchanged; only a later pull request may activate those exact, now-base-owned bytes. The activation may then point each manifest source back to its active target for the next cycle. Initial activation is governed by the same principle through the still-active predecessor checker's deterministic activation bridge, which validates the candidate successor bundle and requires every candidate workflow entry to remain a regular non-executable blob before the predecessor anchor is retired.
- Alternatives: let a policy pull request update active targets and their manifest digests together (rejected because candidate-controlled authorization makes the base-owned audit circular); protect only executable scripts and workflows (rejected because omitted fixtures and transitive inputs can change what trusted self-tests prove); rely on Git blob SHA values without constraining modes or `.gitattributes` (rejected because symlinks, submodules, executable-bit changes, or checkout filters can change materialization without changing an approved regular-file blob); require manual repository review instead of a mechanical transition protocol (rejected because repository policy treats pull-request content as untrusted and requires the trust boundary to fail closed).
- Consequences: search-policy maintenance has a deliberate two-merge latency and the manifest cannot shrink in place; removing a protected target requires a future superseding decision and checker protocol rather than an ordinary bundle update. In return, no single pull request can both alter a protected policy input and make that alteration authoritative after merge. The workflow-policy checker and its mutation suite mechanically enforce the manifest schema, source digests, Git modes/types, no-`.gitattributes` rule, non-shrinking target set, deterministic initial activation, and exact-base staging requirement described in `docs/TESTING.md` and surfaced in `docs/ROADMAP.md`.

## D-104: Defer the generic function type-parameter `Ty` placeholder to PR-13

- Status: accepted
- Context: D-089 decided `Ty`'s new container variants (`List`/`Dict`/`Set`/`Tuple`) but explicitly left "whatever case PR-13 needs for a generic function's type parameter (a placeholder/parameter marker distinct from `Infer`)" unresolved, naming both PR-10 and PR-13 as candidate owners. PR-10's own actual scope (`docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md` row 10; `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-28-v0-2-collections-generics-design.md`'s PR breakdown item 3) is `Ty` migration + monomorphization foundation + a `list[T]` thin slice with concrete, already-known element types (a literal's element type, a function argument's already-annotated type) — it never emits or consumes a type *parameter* (`def f[T](x: T) -> T`'s `T`), only concrete types. PEP 695 user-defined generic functions are PR-13's own scope (`docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md` row 13).
- Decision: PR-10 adds no `Ty` variant for generic type parameters. `Ty`'s new recursive variants are exactly `List(Box<Ty>)`, `Dict(Box<Ty>, Box<Ty>)`, `Set(Box<Ty>)`, `Tuple(Vec<Ty>)` — nothing else. PR-13's own implementer designs the type-parameter placeholder against the real constraint-solving code (`collect_expr_constraints`/`infer_expr_in` in `pycc_types`) once PEP 695 syntax parsing actually exists to feed it, rather than PR-10 guessing its shape with no call site to exercise it.
- Alternatives: add a speculative `Ty::TypeParam(String)`-style variant now, unused until PR-13 (rejected — this project's own D-057 "simplest correct thing for the stated scope" precedent argues against speculative variants with no code path exercising them; an unexercised variant is also invisible to every exhaustive match this plan's own Task 2-5 fix, meaning it would silently need re-auditing anyway once PR-13 actually uses it, buying nothing over adding it in PR-13 directly).
- Consequences: `Ty`'s recursive shape after this PR is exactly the four container variants plus the six pre-existing scalars — ten variants total, all fully concrete. PR-13 supersedes nothing here; it simply adds its own variant when it has a real consumer for it.

## D-105: v0.2's `list[T]` thin slice — scope cuts and runtime-representation choice

- Status: accepted
- Context: PR-10 (`docs/DELIVERY_PLAN.md` row 10) must migrate `Ty` to a recursive enum (857 total call sites across pycc_hir (36) / pycc_types (335) / pycc_mir (111) / pycc_codegen (372) / pycc_rt (3) as of this decision) and then implement `list[T]` "end-to-end thin slice (literal, indexing, `len()`, iteration, `.append()`)" per the v0.2 design doc's own PR-10 line. Reading the actual current frontend (`crates/pycc_hir/src/lib.rs`): `Stmt::Assign` only accepts a bare-name target (line ~290, "only assigning to a bare name is supported so far") — no subscript-assignment target exists; `Stmt::For` only accepts `for x in range(...)` (line ~369-395) via a dedicated `HirStmt::ForRange { var, start, stop, step, body }` node with no general iterator/iterable abstraction anywhere downstream; `lower_expr`'s `Expr::Call` arm (line ~485) only accepts a bare-name callee, no attribute/method call exists at all today; `annotation_to_ty` (line ~249) only accepts a bare name, so a subscripted annotation like `list[int]` cannot be written today and none of `pycc_types`/`pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen`'s existing `Ty` dispatch sites are exhaustive matches (they are catch-all `other => panic!(...)` arms — see `ty_to_basic_type`, the local-slot read match, the `BinOp` result match, `collect_module_bindings`'s inner match, all in `crates/pycc_codegen/src/lib.rs`), so adding new `Ty` variants is not compiler-enforced there. Separately, `docs/RUNTIME.md`'s only container-layout line ("`list[T]`: growable vec of unboxed `T`... `list[int]` is literally `Vec<i64>`-shaped") already conflicts with its own generic-heap-object-header line a few lines above (16-byte header with `rc`/`type_id`/`flags`) — the actual, shipped `PyStrObj` (`crates/pycc_rt/src/lib.rs`, struct at line ~702) has neither `type_id` nor `flags`, just `rc: Cell<u32>` plus payload.
- Decision, five scope cuts:
  1. **No annotation syntax for `list[T]` in v0.2.** `list[int]` values exist only via bare-name local inference (`x = [1, 2, 3]`) inside module scope or a private helper (D-038 convention: name starts with `_`), never as a public function's annotated parameter or return type. `annotation_to_ty` is unchanged by this plan; `x: list[int] = []` continues to be rejected by its existing "only a bare name type annotation is supported so far" error, now covered by an explicit regression test asserting this is still true on purpose, not by accident.
  2. **Indexing is read-only.** `x[0]` is a new r-value expression form; `x[0] = value` (subscript as an assignment target) is out of scope — `Stmt::Assign`'s existing bare-name-only restriction is untouched.
  3. **`.append()` and `len()` are narrowly-recognized special forms, not general method/attribute dispatch.** `lst.append(value)` is recognized directly inside `lower_expr`'s existing `Expr::Call` arm when `call.func` is an `Expr::Attribute` whose attribute name is exactly `append` and whose value is a bare `Expr::Name` — producing a new `HirExpr::ListAppend { list: String, value: Box<HirExpr> }` node. `len(lst)` needs **no new HIR node at all**: it is already fully expressible as the existing `HirExpr::Call { callee: "len", args: [lst] }` shape (a bare-name callee), so only `pycc_types`/`pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen`'s call-dispatch needs a new `"len"` arm alongside their existing `"print"` arm. Neither of these builds general attribute access or a general builtin-function table; both are single, hand-recognized shapes, exactly mirroring how `for x in range(...)` is already hand-recognized in `Stmt::For` today rather than backed by any general iterable/callable abstraction.
  4. **Iteration desugars to an index-counted loop, reusing `ForRange`'s existing lowering shape.** A new `HirStmt::ForList { var: String, list: String, body: Vec<HirStmt> }` (parallel structure to the existing `HirStmt::ForRange`) lowers through `pycc_mir` into a new `MirStmt::ForList { var: String, list: String, body: Vec<MirStmt> }`, which `pycc_codegen` compiles by reusing `MirStmt::ForRange`'s existing loop/branch-building basic-block infrastructure (`crates/pycc_codegen/src/lib.rs` around line 2055), parametrized over a runtime length call instead of a static bound, plus one indexed read per iteration. No user-visible iterator protocol (`__iter__`/`__next__`) is introduced.
  5. **Codegen ships `list[int]` only; every other element type stays a clean, pre-codegen diagnostic, not a runtime panic.** `Ty::List(Box<Ty>)` itself is fully general at the type-representation and type-checking level (a list of `str`, `float`, `bool`, or even a nested list type-checks correctly), but `pycc_types` rejects any `list[T]` where `T != Ty::Int` with a new diagnostic (`T0034`, "list codegen only supports `list[int]` in v0.2") **before** codegen ever runs — this keeps `pycc_codegen`'s existing "should be unreachable" panic convention (per this plan's own Global Constraints) actually true, rather than a type-checked `list[str]` program silently reaching a codegen-level Rust panic. The new runtime object (`crates/pycc_rt`) is therefore exactly one concrete type: a growable, unboxed `i64` buffer with a `PyStrObj`-shaped header (`rc: Cell<u32>` plus `len`/`cap`/data-pointer fields — no `type_id`/`flags`, following the real shipped precedent, not `docs/RUNTIME.md`'s stale, self-inconsistent spec). `docs/RUNTIME.md` itself is corrected in this plan's own Task 12 to describe what's actually built rather than repeating its pre-existing inconsistency.
- Alternatives: build general attribute/method-call dispatch now (rejected — no other PR-10 feature needs it, and it is strictly larger than the two hand-recognized shapes `.append()`/`len()` actually require; YAGNI per this project's own D-057 precedent). Build a real iterator protocol now (rejected — same reasoning; `ForList`'s index-counted desugaring is fully sufficient for `list[T]` and is the only container v0.2 iterates over). Implement codegen for all scalar element types immediately (rejected — multiplies `pycc_rt`'s new runtime-object surface by 4-5x for zero benefit to this PR's own stated "thin slice" scope; each additional element type is better sized as its own later PR once list[int]'s pattern is proven end-to-end). Follow `docs/RUNTIME.md`'s existing 16-byte generic-header spec literally (rejected — it is unimplemented by every existing runtime object including `PyStrObj`, and inventing a second, more elaborate header shape used by nothing else in the runtime creates exactly the doc-vs-reality drift this project's own AGENTS.md "keep documentation honest about what exists now" rule forbids; corrected in Task 12 instead).
- Consequences: `T0034` lives in `pycc_types`, at the point a `list[T]` literal's element type is inferred (Task 8), where a real source span is naturally available for the diagnostic — not as a separate pre-codegen validation pass over already-checked MIR/HIR, which would need to reconstruct a span it may no longer have. This means a future PR extending `list[T]` to `str`/`float`/`bool`/nested-list elements needs **two** changes, not one: (a) relax or remove this specific `T0034` gate in `pycc_types` (a small, isolated check — the homogeneity-inference logic around it, and the `Subscript`/`ForList`/`ListAppend` inference it feeds, are already written generically over any scalar `Ty`, not hardcoded to `Int`, and need no changes themselves), and (b) add the new `pycc_rt` runtime object plus `pycc_codegen` dispatch arms for that element type. "Only codegen changes" would be inaccurate; `pycc_mir`'s lowering is already generic and genuinely needs no changes either way. `docs/TYPE_SYSTEM.md`'s "Generics" section describing full PEP 695 monomorphization remains the v1.0 target, unaffected by this narrower interim state.

## D-106: `PyIntListObj`'s element/index representation is raw untagged `i64`, not D-061-tagged; the boundary conversion and its bigint exclusion are `pycc_codegen`'s job

- Status: accepted
- Context: D-104 (Task 10) specified `PyIntListObj`'s tests literally as `pycc_rt_int_list_append(list, 10)` then `pycc_rt_int_list_get(list, 0) == 10` — not `tag_smallint(10)`. Those tests are binding, and the implementer shipped `PyIntListObj` (`crates/pycc_rt/src/lib.rs`) storing exactly that: raw, untagged 64-bit slots, matching `docs/RUNTIME.md`'s existing "growable vec of unboxed `T`... SIMD-friendly" line. This is a real departure from D-061's own general rule ("every arithmetic/comparison/print operation on `int` is a call to a `pycc_rt` function taking/returning this exact tagged `i64`, never a raw untagged value") — a rule D-061's own consequences section already grants one prior exception to, for `bool`'s "own, simpler, untagged `i8` representation." Task 10's pinned local reviewer (D-068) confirmed this is a real, unresolved cross-task contract question and flagged it as the one open item for whoever picks up Task 11 (`pycc_codegen`). Separately: `pycc_rt_int_add`/`_sub`/`_mul`/`_floordiv` already promote a same-crate scalar `int` to a heap `BigInt` on arithmetic overflow (`tag_bigint(...)`, e.g. `crates/pycc_rt/src/lib.rs` lines ~277/312/337/380) — this is reachable today from ordinary, already-type-checked Python source (e.g. `y = 9223372036854775807 * 2`), not a hypothetical. `untag_smallint` cannot detect a bigint-tagged input; it silently reinterprets the bigint heap pointer's bits as a shifted integer if given one. A raw `i64` list slot has no room to represent a bigint at all.
- Decision: `PyIntListObj` keeps its shipped raw-untagged-`i64` representation — a second, deliberate, narrow exception to D-061's tagging rule, parallel to `bool`'s. `pycc_codegen` owns crossing this boundary in both directions, and the two directions are asymmetric:
  - **Input side (a tagged `Ty::Int` value about to cross into `PyIntListObj`: `.append()`'s value, `x[i]`'s index):** this direction requires discriminating smallint from bigint before dropping the tag bit — genuine tag-bit interpretation, which per this crate's own established principle ("only `pycc_rt` may interpret its bits" — see `to_float`'s doc comment on why `int`-to-`float` goes through `pycc_rt_int_to_float` rather than a raw LLVM cast) belongs inside `pycc_rt`, not inlined as LLVM IR in `pycc_codegen`. Task 11 adds exactly one new `pycc_rt` extern "C" function for this: check the tag bit (mirroring the existing private `is_smallint`/`require_smallint` helpers), panic with an honest `"pycc_rt: <description>"` message if the value is bigint-tagged, else `untag_smallint` and return the raw `i64`. One function, reused at all three input-side call sites (`ListLiteral`'s per-element value, `ListAppend`'s value, `Subscript`'s index).
  - **Output side (a raw `i64` read back out: `x[i]`'s result, `len(x)`'s result):** always safe to re-tag, by construction — the input-side guard above already proves every raw slot holds a value that fits `tag_smallint`'s 63-bit range (an `untag_smallint` of a valid smallint-tagged input can never itself exceed that range), and a list's element count is bounded far below it in any realistic run. No bigint case exists on this side, so no `pycc_rt` call is needed: `pycc_codegen` constructs the tagged value directly via the same inline LLVM IR shape `to_tagged_int`'s `Scalar::Bool` arm already uses (`build_left_shift` then `build_or` with the tag bit) — this is the precedent for the *construct a tagged value from a value already known to be in range* direction, as opposed to `to_float`'s precedent for the *interpret an existing tagged value's bits* direction.
  - **The bigint case is an honest runtime panic, not silent corruption**, exactly this project's existing "unrecoverable runtime conditions are `panic!(\"pycc_rt: <description>\")`" convention. It is explicitly a v0.2 scope cut (bigint list elements are unsupported), not a bug, and — since it is reachable from ordinary type-checked source per the Context above — Task 11 owes it a real, executing regression test for D-14's coverage gate, not just a documented gap.
- Alternatives: store `PyIntListObj` elements D-061-tagged instead (rejected — contradicts Task 10's own binding tests, already shipped and reviewed; would also defeat `docs/RUNTIME.md`'s stated unboxed/SIMD-friendly design goal for exactly the element type v0.2 ships). Silently truncate or reinterpret a bigint-tagged value on `.append()` instead of panicking (rejected — directly violates this project's "honest panic over silently wrong data" convention; a truncated bigint stored as a raw `i64` is exactly the kind of quiet correctness bug this project's conventions exist to prevent). Do the input-side tag-bit check inline in `pycc_codegen` via raw LLVM IR instead of a new `pycc_rt` function (rejected — this direction requires interpreting the tag bit's meaning, which this crate's own `to_float` precedent already reserves for `pycc_rt`, not codegen; keeping it in `pycc_rt` also means the bigint check's exact bit-level definition lives in exactly one place, next to `is_smallint`/`require_smallint`, rather than being duplicated as LLVM instructions).
- Consequences: Task 11's plan section is updated with the exact per-call-site conversions this decision specifies (one new `pycc_rt` extern function for input-side untagging-with-a-bigint-guard; inline LLVM IR mirroring `to_tagged_int`'s bool arm for output-side re-tagging), so its implementer does not need to rediscover this contract by reading Task 10's report. A future PR that lets `list[int]` elements actually be bigints (not just reject them) would need to either widen `PyIntListObj`'s raw `i64` slot to something bigint-capable, or move `list[int]`'s storage to D-061-tagged after all — a strictly bigger change than this decision's own scope, left to whichever later PR actually needs it. This decision does not touch the separate, already-recorded negative-index scope cut (D-105's own text, Task 10's report) — that stays where it is.

## D-107: `list[T]` pointers get their own `pycc_codegen::Scalar::List` variant; refcounting stays leak-only in v0.2

- Status: accepted
- Context: Task 5 (D-089) made `emit_expr`'s `Name` arm read a `Ty::List(_)`-typed local by reusing `Scalar::Str` to carry the pointer (`crates/pycc_codegen/src/lib.rs`, the `Ty::List(_)` arm next to `Ty::Str`'s), explicitly documented as safe only because "no `MirExpr` can construct a `list[T]` value yet," and explicitly flagged as "a general Task-11 tripwire" for `truthy` and `to_str` specifically, both of which are **exhaustive** matches over `Scalar` with no catch-all. Before dispatching Task 11 (codegen), checked whether that tripwire is actually reachable rather than assuming either way: `pycc_types` accepts a `Ty::List`-typed operand in a boolean context (`crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs:1214`, `infer_expr(env, test)` with the comment "any type is accepted as truthy for v0.1 -- Python's own truthiness has no static type restriction") and as `print`'s argument (`crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs:310-312`, an unconditional `Ok(Ty::None)` for any argument type, no type check at all). So `if x:` and `print(x)` for `x: list[int]` both type-check successfully today and would reach `truthy(Scalar::Str(list_ptr))`/`to_str(Scalar::Str(list_ptr))` once Task 11 makes `list[int]` values actually constructible from real source — not a hypothetical, a live reachable path. `PyIntListObj`'s layout (`rc: Cell<u32>`, `items: Cell<Vec<i64>>`) is entirely different from `PyStrObj`'s (`rc: Cell<u32>`, `payload: PyStrPayload`), so `pycc_rt_str_truthy`/`pycc_rt_*_to_str` misreading a list pointer's bytes as string data is genuine undefined behavior, not a defensible interim gap. Separately, checked whether `list[T]` refcounting is wired anywhere in `pycc_codegen`: it is not. `decref_str_slot_before_store` (the existing reassignment-cleanup path `str` locals use) is gated on `slot.ty != Ty::Str` and panics if called on anything else, and is confirmed never reached for a `Ty::List` assignment target — so reassigning or aliasing (`y = x`) a list variable neither increfs nor decrefs anything today. This is leak-only, never a premature free or a double free, since nothing frees a list value early either.
- Decision: `Scalar` (`crates/pycc_codegen/src/lib.rs`) gains its own `List(PointerValue<'ctx>)` variant, replacing the `Scalar::Str` reuse. This turns every exhaustive `Scalar` match (`truthy`, `to_str`, and any other site the compiler flags) into a compile error until it adds a `List(_)` arm — the same "let the compiler enumerate every site" principle Task 5's own `Ty` catch-all audit already established, rather than manually hunting for every place a list pointer could reach an operation that assumes `str`. `truthy(list)`/`to_str(list)`/any arithmetic-on-list arm gets an honest `panic!("pycc_codegen: <description> on a list[T] value is not supported yet")`, matching this project's "honest panic over silently wrong data" convention — v0.2 genuinely has no `bool(list)`/`str(list)`/list-arithmetic semantics, so this is a true, currently-real feature gap, not a regression introduced by this decision. `list[T]` refcounting stays exactly as it is today: leak-only. `pycc_rt_int_list_incref`/`_decref` (already shipped by Task 10) are not called from anywhere in Task 11's own scope; a list's backing allocation is freed only if some future task wires that call. For pycc's actual target use (a short-lived, single-shot compiled batch program), an unbounded per-list leak is a bounded-by-process-lifetime resource cost, not a defect visible in any program's observable output.
- Alternatives: keep reusing `Scalar::Str` and add a manual `Ty`-gated check at every `truthy`/`to_str`-style call site instead (rejected — exactly the kind of easy-to-forget manual enumeration Task 5's own catch-all-audit precedent exists to avoid; a new call site added by a later task could silently omit the same check, whereas a missing `List(_)` arm on an exhaustive match is a compile error no task can miss). Wire full `list[T]` refcounting now (incref-on-duplicate-reference, decref-before-reassignment, decref-at-scope-exit, mirroring `str`'s own D-060 pattern exactly) (rejected for v0.2's own "thin slice" scope — this multiplies Task 11's diff for a memory-lifetime property that affects no program's observable output and is bounded by process lifetime for pycc's actual batch-compiled-binary use case; better sized as its own task once a real usage pattern — e.g. an unbounded loop appending large lists — actually motivates it).
- Consequences: Task 11 splits into 11a (this decision's own scope, plus D-106's `pycc_rt_int_untag_checked`/`raw_i64_to_tagged_int`) and 11b (wiring the four new MIR forms against the now-settled `Scalar::List` representation). A future PR that needs real `list[T]` refcounting must wire `pycc_rt_int_list_incref`/`_decref` at the same three sites `str`'s own D-060 refcounting already covers (duplicate-reference reads, reassignment cleanup, scope-exit cleanup) — tracked as a `docs/ROADMAP.md` follow-up by Task 12, not silently assumed solved by this PR.

## D-108: `pycc_rt_int_list_get` rejects negative indices as out-of-range; correcting Task 10's "D-105" mis-citation

- Status: accepted
- Context: Task 10 (D-105's `PyIntListObj` runtime object) implemented `pycc_rt_int_list_get`'s bounds check as `if index < 0 || index as usize >= len { panic!("pycc_rt: list index out of range") }`, and both its own doc comment and a dedicated `#[should_panic]` regression test (`int_list_get_rejects_negative_indices`, `crates/pycc_rt/src/lib.rs`) cited this scope cut as "(D-105)." D-105's own five scope cuts (this file, above) cover annotation syntax, read-only indexing, `.append()`/`len()` as hand-recognized special forms, `ForList`'s `ForRange`-reuse desugaring, and `list[int]`-only codegen — none of them mention index sign or bounds at all. Task 10's own independent task-review (recorded in this plan's `progress.md`) confirmed the *behavior* is correct and deliberate — `pycc_types` has no way to reject `lst[-1]` at compile time, since the index value is only known at runtime — but flagged the citation as pointing at a decision that never actually discusses it, and asked for "a real, discoverable home" for the scope cut rather than an inaccurate pointer, since `lst[-1]` is ordinary, type-checked Python source a user can actually write, not a hypothetical.
- Decision: record the negative-index rejection as its own decision, per this file's own append-only convention (an accepted decision is never edited in place; D-105's text is unchanged). `pycc_rt_int_list_get` panics on any negative index with the same message as an out-of-range positive one (`"pycc_rt: list index out of range"`) — real Python's `lst[-1]`-addresses-the-last-element semantics is out of scope for v0.2's thin slice. `crates/pycc_rt/src/lib.rs`'s doc comment on `pycc_rt_int_list_get` and `int_list_get_rejects_negative_indices`'s own test comment (Task 12) are corrected to cite D-108 instead of D-105.
- Alternatives: support negative indexing in v0.2 (rejected — a runtime-computed index can't be resolved to "count from the end" by a static rewrite the way a literal `-1` could, so this needs a real length-relative adjustment inside `pycc_rt_int_list_get` itself; strictly larger than this thin slice's own scope and not requested by any PR-10 task). Edit D-105's own text to add this scope cut retroactively (rejected — D-105 is already accepted, and this project's own convention is to supersede via a new decision, never silently rewrite an accepted one, exactly the same reasoning D-090→D-091 and D-091(+D-099)→D-100 already established). Leave the mis-citation as-is since the underlying behavior is correct (rejected — Task 10's own review explicitly asked for a durable, correctly-attributed record; a wrong citation next to a real, reachable panic is exactly the kind of doc-vs-reality drift this project's AGENTS.md "keep documentation honest" rule exists to catch).
- Consequences: `pycc_rt_int_list_get`'s doc comment and its regression test's comment both cite D-108, not D-105. `tests/fixtures/pep_0585_builtin_generics.py` (Task 12) must not exercise negative indexing, since it would panic here rather than matching CPython's last-element behavior. A future PR adding real negative-index support supersedes this decision, not D-105, and must update that fixture's own documented restriction at the same time. **Cross-reference note:** D-106's own Consequences section (above) still says this scope cut is recorded in "D-105's own text, Task 10's report" — that parenthetical predates this decision and is superseded by it: the scope cut's actual, discoverable home is this entry, D-108, not D-105. D-106 itself is left unedited, per this file's own append-only convention (the same convention this decision's own Alternatives section already applies to D-105); a reader following D-106's citation should resolve it here instead.

## D-109: PR-10's `frontend-perf-gate` regression is real and self-inflicted; fix deferred to its own follow-up PR, merge stays blocked

- Status: accepted
- Context: PR #236's (this branch's) `frontend-perf-gate` failed CI, first at a 4.7008% median regression (previous aggregate 5540.68ns, current 5801.13ns; threshold 2.00%) and, on one independent `--failed`-only rerun, at a run whose own log turned out to interleave `scripts/check_replicated_paired_perf_regression.rb`'s own unit-test fixture output (synthetic round-number timings such as "1000.00"/"1020.01" ns) with the real gate invocation — that rerun's `frontend-perf-measure` step carried the same `startedAt` timestamp as the original run, confirming it reused cached measurement artifacts rather than remeasuring, per this project's own D-095/D-096/D-101 "check whether the rerun actually remeasured" methodology. A second, full (not `--failed`-only) rerun of the whole workflow produced a genuinely fresh, independent measurement — new `frontend-perf-measure` timestamps, a different previous-baseline sample (5589.76-7201.75ns band, median 6083.97ns) than the first run's — and failed again, at 5.1960% (current aggregate 6400.09ns). Two independent, genuinely-fresh measurements landing at 4.7008% and 5.1960%, both several points over the 2.00% threshold and in the same direction, is inconsistent with pure noise canceling out; it reads as a real, reproducible regression, not a noisy near-miss.

  `benches/check_bench.rs`'s `pycc_check_frontend_fixture` (the benchmark this gate measures) runs `pycc_parser::parse` → `pycc_hir::lower_checked` → `pycc_types::check` on a small, scalar-only fib fixture — no `list[T]` value is ever constructed or type-checked by it. This rules out "the new list codegen is slow" and points at PR-10's own earlier `Ty` representation migration (D-089/D-104, Tasks 2-5): `crates/pycc_hir::Ty` changed from a flat, fieldless, six-variant `#[derive(Copy, Clone, ...)]` enum (a throwaway `size_of`/`align_of` check confirmed the new shape is `size_of::<Ty>() == 24` bytes, `align_of::<Ty>() == 8`, with `Copy` dropped since a `Box`/`Vec`-holding enum cannot derive it) to a recursive enum carrying `List(Box<Ty>)`, `Dict(Box<Ty>, Box<Ty>)` (two boxes), and `Tuple(Vec<Ty>)` (a growable heap vector). `pycc_types::check`'s constraint-solving path constructs and clones many `Ty` values per checked expression — a 24-byte, non-`Copy`, sometimes-heap-touching value copied/cloned in place of what was previously a 1-byte `Copy` value is a highly plausible, mechanically-explained cause for a several-percent regression on a type-checking-heavy hot path, without needing to invoke CI noise at all.
- Decision: treat this as a confirmed, real, self-inflicted regression, not environmental noise, and do not attempt to route around the gate. Specifically: do not lower or exempt `frontend-perf-gate`'s 2.00% threshold, do not merge PR-10 while `ci-gate` is red (D-024's hard branch-protection gate has no exception for "the author is confident it's fine"), and do not implement the fix (most likely: box `Dict`'s two fields into a single `Box<(Ty, Ty)>` and `Tuple`'s `Vec<Ty>` into a `Box<[Ty]>`, shrinking every variant to one pointer-sized payload and very plausibly letting Rust's niche-filling layout shrink `Ty` back close to its pre-migration size) inside this PR's own Task 13. PR-10's `list[int]` slice itself is fully implemented, independently task-reviewed, and merge-ready on its own merits (all 5 Tier-1 targets plus `build-test-coverage`, `cross-compile-build`, and `cross-compile-verify` are green on this exact commit) — only frontend scalar-checking throughput is regressed, and that fix deserves its own task brief, its own implementer, and its own independent review, exactly like every other PR-10 task, rather than a late, unreviewed amendment squeezed into the task that was supposed to just open and merge the PR.
- Alternatives: lower or add an exemption for `frontend-perf-gate`'s threshold (rejected — unlike D-095/D-096/D-101, which relaxed floors only after confirming an external, architecture-specific hardware constraint neither PR could fix, this regression is self-inflicted by this PR's own code change and has a concrete, identified remedy; relaxing the gate here would be exactly the "adjust the knob to force a pass" move this project's own conventions reject). Implement the `Ty`-boxing fix directly as part of Task 13 (rejected — this would change the representation Tasks 2-5 already migrated ~857 call sites against, after every one of those tasks' own reviews already closed and the pinned reviewer's passes already ran against the current shape; landing it unreviewed, this late, is the exact failure mode this PR's whole task-by-task review discipline exists to prevent). Merge anyway with `ci-gate` red (rejected outright — violates D-024's protected-`main` branch protection, which has no bypass for ordinary work). Dismiss the result as noise without a second independent measurement (rejected — this project's own established methodology, applied successfully in D-093/D-095/D-096/D-101, is to get a genuinely fresh rerun before concluding noise; here two fresh, independent measurements agreed in direction and rough magnitude, which is itself evidence against the noise hypothesis).
- Consequences: PR #236 stays merge-blocked pending a dedicated follow-up PR (or an accepted superseding decision) that resolves this regression — tracked as a `docs/ROADMAP.md` follow-up alongside this entry, not silently left for a future reader to rediscover. That follow-up PR's own task should re-measure `size_of::<Ty>()`/`align_of::<Ty>()` after boxing `Dict`/`Tuple`, confirm the boxing's blast radius is actually small (D-104's own scope note that v0.2 never constructs a real `Dict`/`Tuple`/`Set` value means most exhaustive-match call sites should be catch-all panics untouched by the exact field shape, but this must be verified against the live tree, not assumed), and re-run `frontend-perf-gate` to confirm the regression closes before that PR merges. If a future investigation instead finds the regression does not close by boxing alone, that finding supersedes this decision's own remedy paragraph without needing to revisit whether the regression itself was real. **Cross-reference note:** this entry's Decision and Alternatives sections above describe the fix as needing "its own follow-up PR" separate from this one — that framing turned out to be mechanically impossible to execute as written: the regressed `Ty` shape this decision describes exists only on this branch (`feat/v0-2-pr10-ty-representation-migration`), never having reached `main`, so no PR opened against `main` could carry a fix for it. The review-discipline concern that framing was actually protecting — no unreviewed representation change landing without its own brief, implementer, and independent review — is fully satisfied instead by a new Task 14 added to this same plan and executed on this same branch, through the identical task-review loop every other task in this plan already passed through. This entry's own Decision/Alternatives text is left unedited per this file's append-only convention (the same convention D-108's own cross-reference note against D-106 already applied); a reader following this decision's "its own follow-up PR" language should resolve it as "Task 14, this branch" instead. **Update (Task 14):** `Ty::Dict` is now `Dict(Box<(Ty, Ty)>)`, exactly as this decision's remedy paragraph proposed, and needed only one attempt. `Ty::Tuple` needed two: a first attempt boxed it as `Tuple(Box<[Ty]>)` (a boxed slice) and measured `size_of::<Ty>() == 24` bytes — no reduction at all from the pre-fix size — confirmed independently both in-crate and via a standalone `rustc` reproduction of the isomorphic enum shape; this was a genuine dead end, not a measurement error. A second attempt, `Tuple(Box<Vec<Ty>>)` (a second indirection: a thin pointer to a heap-allocated `Vec<Ty>`), measured `size_of::<Ty>() == 16` bytes (down from 24), confirmed the same way; `align_of::<Ty>()` stayed `8` in both configurations and in the pre-fix shape. The most plausible explanation is that `Box<[Ty]>` (a 16-byte fat pointer: data pointer + length) broke a uniform-thin-pointer shape that `List`/`Dict`/`Set`'s `Box<Ty>`/`Box<(Ty, Ty)>` (8-byte thin pointers each) already had, defeating rustc's niche-filling layout optimization (the mechanism that makes `size_of::<Option<Box<T>>>() == size_of::<Box<T>>()`) and forcing a full explicit discriminant tag on top of the largest variant's payload; `Box<Vec<Ty>>` restores that uniformity. This project has **not** independently re-derived rustc's exact layout algorithm well enough to state that as a proven universal rule, though — this entry's own pre-fix measurement (`Ty` already had non-uniform dataful variant sizes: `List` 8B, `Dict` 16B as two separate `Box<Ty>` fields, `Set` 8B, `Tuple` 24B as `Vec<Ty>`) still measured 24 bytes with *no* added discriminant tag, which a naive "tag plus largest payload" rule would instead put at 32. The measured `size_of`/`align_of` numbers above are the authoritative facts this decision relies on; the niche-filling explanation is offered as the most plausible mechanism, not an independently proven one. Separately, and independent of the exact mechanism: this is a real, predictive reduction for `frontend-perf-gate`'s own fixture specifically, not just a smaller number in the abstract — `benches/check_bench.rs`'s fixture is scalar-only, so every `Ty` value it ever constructs is one of the six zero-sized variants (`Int`/`Float`/`Bool`/`Str`/`None`/`Infer`) — it never constructs a `List`/`Dict`/`Set`/`Tuple` value and so never triggers a `Box::clone()` heap allocation regardless of container field shape. The only channel by which this change can affect that benchmark is `Ty`'s uniform in-memory size, which every move or clone of a `Ty::Int` pays regardless of which variant is actually active (Rust enums are fixed-size across all variants) — so 24→16 bytes is a real reduction in that benchmark's own copy cost, not a hopeful analogy from an unrelated code path. This also rules out, for this specific benchmark, the competing hypothesis (raised during the first, blocked attempt at this task) that `Ty` losing `Copy` rather than its byte width is the real driver of the regression: for a fieldless variant like `Ty::Int`, `Clone` literally is the memcpy, so there is no additional non-`Copy` cost beyond the width itself on this fixture's own hot path. This entry still does not claim `frontend-perf-gate` is confirmed closed — a smaller `Ty` is necessary but a fresh full CI rerun (not `--failed`-only) observing genuinely new `frontend-perf-measure` timestamps and replicate medians is required before that determination can be made, exactly as this decision's own remedy paragraph already said. **Update (confirmed closed):** two independent, genuinely fresh full CI reruns of the same commit (`c276262`, [run 30613065177](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/actions/runs/30613065177)) both passed `frontend-perf-gate`, each with its own fresh `frontend-perf-measure` timestamps and distinct replicate-median samples (ruling out cached/`--failed`-only reuse, the exact failure mode that produced a misleading "second data point" earlier in this investigation): the first measured a 1.8430% delta (previous aggregate 5764.39ns, current 5870.62ns), the second measured **-0.4454%** (previous aggregate 6099.61ns, current 6072.44ns — current is not just within threshold but marginally faster than previous). Both comfortably clear the 2.00% threshold, in sharp contrast to the two pre-fix measurements (4.7008%, 5.1960%) that both cleared it in the opposite direction — a swing of this size across two independent before/after measurement pairs is not consistent with the fix being noise-neutral; it is real. D-109's regression is confirmed resolved. PR #236 is no longer merge-blocked by this decision.

  **Correction (2026-07-31, full CI record reconciled against live job logs):** the "confirmed closed" update above does not survive a check against the actual, currently retrievable CI job logs, and a wider review of every `frontend-perf-gate` measurement taken across this investigation contradicts both this decision's "confirmed closed" update and, more seriously, casts real doubt on the original "confirmed regression" conclusion itself. Specifics, each verified directly from `gh run view --job <id> --log` against the cited run/job IDs, not reconstructed from memory:
  - Run 30613065177's two attempts (`c276262`) are **not** two independent measurements: both attempt 1 (job 91101229723) and attempt 2 (job 91102182946) show byte-identical replicate medians and an identical **-0.4454%** delta (previous aggregate 6099.61ns, current 6072.44ns) — attempt 2 reused attempt 1's cached artifacts rather than remeasuring, the exact failure mode this project's own D-095/D-096/D-101 "verify the rerun actually remeasured" methodology exists to catch, and it was not caught here. The **1.8430%** figure this decision recorded for "the first" measurement does not match either attempt's currently retrievable log; its origin could not be reconciled and it should not be relied on.
  - Three further CI runs have executed since that update, all on commits that touch **no** `Ty`-representation code (`1420d91` is a docs-only SESSION_LOG/DECISIONS.md commit; `f4b01c6` is a merge of `origin/main`; `022b49a` touches `pycc_types`'s `ListAppend` check and doc comments, a code path this benchmark's scalar-only fixture never exercises) — every one of them **failed** `frontend-perf-gate`: `1420d91` at 3.0685% (run 30614020318: previous 6873.98ns, current 7084.91ns), `f4b01c6` at 16.5551% (run 30614141700: previous 5590.09ns, current 6515.53ns), and `022b49a` at 4.2506% on its first CI attempt (run 30615455609: previous 5621.43ns, current 5860.37ns) then 5.4321% on a full, genuinely fresh rerun of the same commit (previous 5587.10ns, current 5890.60ns).
  - The pre-fix side of this investigation is equally mixed, not uniformly failing as this decision's Context paragraph implies: alongside the two originally-cited pre-fix failures (4.7008%, and 5.1960% — independently reconfirmed this session at run 30608030517, previous 6083.97ns, current 6400.09ns), commit `25c95b97` (still pre-fix, the commit that first recorded this decision) failed at 6.5239% (run 30609617862: previous 6010.64ns, current 6402.76ns) — but the very next commit, `69b51b29` (pure decision-text/framing edit, zero code change from `25c95b97`), **passed** at 0.8089% (run 30609847800: previous 6454.54ns, current 6506.75ns).
  - Combining every genuinely distinct measurement taken across this whole investigation (excluding the non-independent cached duplicate above): pre-fix is 3 FAIL / 1 PASS (4.7008%, 5.1960%, 6.5239% failing; 0.8089% passing), post-fix is 1 PASS / 4 FAIL (-0.4454% passing; 3.0685%, 16.5551%, 4.2506%, 5.4321% failing). Both sides show a wide spread that swings from comfortably-under-threshold to more than 8x over it, including on commits proven not to touch the measured code path at all.
  - This pattern — large, direction-inconsistent swings on a hosted, shared CI runner, on a benchmark whose exercised code path is provably unchanged between some passing and some failing commits — is the same class of gap this project has already named and accepted elsewhere: D-095/D-096/D-101's own macOS/Windows/`ubuntu-24.04-arm` `nbody`-gate floors, and, for this exact gate, an almost identical incident already recorded in `docs/SESSION_LOG.md`'s 2026-07-26 "PR #132 blocked on `frontend-perf-gate`; likely order/thermal drift, not a real regression; escalated to the user" entry, which was resolved by asking the user to choose rather than by further reruns.
  - This correction does **not** revert Task 14's `Ty::Dict`/`Ty::Tuple` boxing: `size_of::<Ty>()` shrinking from 24 to 16 bytes is a real, directly-measured representation improvement independent of what this noisy benchmark shows, and is worth keeping on its own merits. What this correction withdraws is the claim that this benchmark's measurements demonstrate that shrink fixed anything, and the earlier claim that the original regression was definitely real rather than possibly always having been within this gate's own noise band.
  - This decision does not resolve which of "the original regression was real and Task 14 fixed it, but this gate's noise now obscures that," "the original regression was itself noise this gate's threshold is too tight to distinguish from," or some third explanation is correct, and does not pick a remedy. PR #236 is merge-blocked again pending that resolution — the "no longer merge-blocked" sentence immediately above is superseded by this correction.
  - **This is not a novel, PR-10-specific question:** [issue #109](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/issues/109) ("Stabilize frontend performance gate against hosted-runner variance") already tracks this exact class of gap project-wide, has been reopened four times since 2026-07-25 as each stabilization attempt (paired-runner activation, D-051/D-053, D-056's source-aware policy, D-062's identity path) turned out incomplete, and remains **open** as of this correction. Its own newest evidence (posted 2026-07-31T07:41:09Z, independently of this branch) documents the identical phenomenon on merged PR #188: the same merge commit passed `frontend-perf-gate` pre-merge and failed it on the exact post-merge `main` run, with zero source difference. This branch's own `25c95b97`→`69b51b29` pair (recorded above) has been added to that issue as further corroborating evidence. Given #109 is a shared, already-active investigation (its comment history shows evidence contributed from multiple PRs over time, most recently today), PR-10 does not attempt its own bespoke fix or threshold change — it stays merge-blocked pending #109's own resolution, tracked there rather than re-litigated here. This supersedes this correction's own earlier framing (and the now-superseded corresponding text in `docs/SESSION_LOG.md`'s newest entry) of this as an isolated question needing a fresh, PR-10-specific decision from scratch.

## D-110: Module value bindings shadow builtin and function call lookup, callee-first

- Status: accepted
- Context: issue #133 — `pycc check` accepted `helper = 1` followed by `helper()` (and `print = 1` followed by `print()`), resolving the call through the function/builtin registry while CPython 3.14 deterministically raises `TypeError: 'int' object is not callable`; after PR #132 the false success became an observable backend miscompile (`pycc run` silently called the shadowed function). Both `Call` arms in `crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs` (`infer_expr_in` final validation, `collect_expr_constraints` private-helper inference) consulted the value environment only behind an `is_local` guard, so module bindings never shadowed call lookup. This entry is numbered D-110 because open PR #236 has already published claims on D-104–D-109; if that range shrinks before it merges, the gap stays documented here rather than being silently compacted.
- Decision: both arms consult the active value binding first — before the local-name check, the `print` special case, argument inference, and the function-registry fallback — and reject a bound callee with the existing `T0021` "name `X` is bound to a non-callable value" diagnostic (every value binding in the current subset is a primitive, so a shadowed target is always non-callable). "Active" means the net source-order binding: a `def` executes at its own position in the top-level walk and rebinds its name, so `helper = 1` followed by `def helper(): ...` leaves later `helper()` calls resolving the function exactly as CPython does, while a value assignment *after* the `def` shadows it again (this refinement comes from PR #252's own review, which caught the initial gate rejecting that valid, previously-compiling shape). The def-rebound fact is tracked *separately* from the value binding's representation record rather than erasing it: D-040's sticky-representation rule keeps consulting the original record, so `helper = 1; def helper(): ...; helper = "leaked"` still fails T0023 instead of reaching codegen with an `int`-allocated slot stored as `str` (the same review's round-4 blocker against a version that cleared the binding at the `def`). The def-rebound flag is cleared unconditionally at every value-assignment site — not only when a fresh binding is created — because a compatible-type reassignment of a name with a pre-`def` record never creates one; clearing only on fresh binds left the flag permanently stuck, so `helper = 1; def helper() -> int: ...; helper = 2; helper()` resolved the function where CPython raises `TypeError` (the review's round-5 blocker), and both environment walks now clear at the same points. Both environment walks — pass 2/pass 3's and the solver's accumulated globals — apply the same rebinding. Three deliberate consequences of that ordering, each pinned by a test: (1) under D-041's final-module-environment model for function bodies, a body call is rejected whenever the *net* source-order top-level binding for the callee is a value — including a program whose only call executes before the rebinding in CPython's dynamic order and therefore runs there; this is a knowingly stricter-than-one-dynamic-trace rule in D-086's spirit, made explicit here rather than left an accident. (2) Diagnostic order is callee-first: a shadowed callee with an additionally-invalid argument reports the non-callable callee, not the argument error — uniform with how the pre-existing local-shadow gate always behaved, though CPython's own evaluation order would surface the argument error first. (3) A bound value that shadows nothing (`x = 1` then `x()`) now reports non-callable instead of final validation's misleading "call to undefined function `x`" and instead of the solver path's dead-end "cannot infer return type of private helper" -- the constraint-collection mirror gate is load-bearing exactly when the bound callee is neither `print` nor any `def` (then, without it, the solver dead-ends before pass 3 ever runs); for a shadowed `print` inside an inference-signature helper the solver's `print` special case would otherwise resolve the call and pass 3's own gate would still reject the shadowing afterward, so there the mirror is fail-fast defense-in-depth rather than the only line of defense.
- Alternatives: keep #133's minimal framing without recording the body-semantics consequence (rejected — it rejects previously-compiling valid Python, exactly the class of strictness choice D-086 exists to record explicitly); gate pass-3 body calls on whether the binding is active at the call's own definition site (rejected — reintroduces #22's execution-order model, which that issue explicitly keeps separate, and contradicts D-041's uniform final-environment rule that plain name reads in bodies already follow); infer arguments before the binding gate to mirror CPython's argument-errors-first evaluation order (rejected — the pre-existing local gate already reported callee-first, and splitting the order by callee classification would make diagnostic selection depend on an implementation detail no user can see); introduce a callable type so bindings could shadow without erroring (out of scope — #133 defers callable values explicitly).
- Consequences: `tests/diagnostics/d0027`–`d0031` pin the five public-CLI shapes (function shadow, builtin shadow, annotated-body call, inference-helper call, inference-helper builtin shadow); in-crate tests pin the three ordering consequences above plus the preserved #22 boundary (a top-level call before the shadowing assignment still resolves the function). `tests/slice0.rs` pins that `pycc build` can no longer reach codegen for a shadowed call. When callable values arrive (v0.2+ surface), the gate's "always non-callable" simplification is the single place to extend.

## D-111: Issue priority is the title's leading `P[1-3]:` prefix, not a GitHub label

- Status: accepted
- Context: `issue-select`'s scoring order names "the repository's own priority markers" as its primary sort key (rank first: P1 before P2 before P3 before unmarked), but this repository has never had GitHub priority labels — `gh label list -R rotnov/pycc` returns only `{bug, documentation, duplicate, enhancement, good first issue, help wanted, invalid, question, wontfix}`. The only live mechanism is an informal `P1:`/`P2:`/`P3:` issue-title prefix: as of 2026-08-01, `gh issue list -R rotnov/pycc --state open` showed 85 of 104 open issues carrying it (0 of 104 carrying any label at all). This convention was previously mentioned only in `docs/SESSION_LOG.md`, a D-066 informational journal that `AGENTS.md` itself says must not be relied on as policy until its lessons are promoted into `AGENTS.md`, `docs/DECISIONS.md`, or an owning specification. An agent reading `issue-select`'s own text literally — "priority **labels** or markers", GitHub-native term first — could reasonably check labels, find the confirmed-empty set, and silently flatten the ordering for the 82% of the open pool that carries the informal prefix but no label.
- Decision: the issue title's leading `P1:`, `P2:`, or `P3:` prefix (exact syntax, colon required, no other punctuation or spacing variant recognized) is this repository's accepted issue-priority mechanism. An issue without that exact prefix is unmarked, ranking after every `P3:`-prefixed issue in `issue-select`'s scoring order (P1 before P2 before P3 before unmarked, unchanged from the pre-existing rule — this decision only fixes where the marker itself is read from). No GitHub label carries priority meaning today; `issue-select`'s own text is updated to state the live mechanism directly rather than describing a label-based check that would find nothing.
- Alternatives: adopt real GitHub priority labels and bulk-relabel the open tracker to match the existing title-prefix convention (rejected for this decision — a separate, larger, riskier live-tracker mutation across 104 issues, deliberately not bundled into a skill-hardening pass; a future decision may adopt this instead, superseding this one); leave the mechanism undefined and rely on a capable agent inferring the convention from context (rejected — this is exactly the informal-journal reliance `AGENTS.md`'s own D-066 rule prohibits for a criterion the skill calls "fixed" and non-negotiable, and the audit that surfaced this gap showed it is not merely hypothetical: a literal reading of "labels or markers" naturally checks labels first).
- Consequences: `issue-select/SKILL.md` states the `P1:`/`P2:`/`P3:` prefix mechanism directly in step 2 instead of the prior generic "priority labels or markers" phrasing. `scripts/run_alpha_skill_evals.py`'s `ISSUE_SELECT_CONTRACT` pins a fragment of the concrete syntax, not just the "priority markers rank first" sentence surviving, so a future edit that silently drops the mechanism definition fails the offline eval suite. If this repository later adopts real GitHub priority labels, that is its own future decision superseding this one, not a silent edit here.

## D-112: Move `frontend-perf-measure`/`frontend-perf-gate` from `macos-14` to `ubuntu-latest`

- Status: accepted (see Update below — Task 4's shadow-measurement evidence supports activation)
- Context: issue #109 has been reopened four times since its first "resolved" comment (D-048→D-051→D-053→D-056→D-062), each time by fresh evidence that `frontend-perf-gate`'s paired-comparison measurement flips outcome on byte-identical compiler source across independent CI runs on `macos-14`. The most recent investigation (issue #109, 2026-08-01) pulled `native-build-test` job-duration coefficients of variation across every runner class this workflow uses: `ubuntu-latest` 3.1%, `windows-latest` 4.7% (excluding one known Defender-scan outlier class already documented at D-096), `ubuntu-24.04-arm` 15.4%, `macos-14` (via `build-test-coverage`, a different job, no Criterion involved) 11.2%, `macos-15-intel` 25.9%. This is prior art independent of anything Criterion-specific: D-095/D-096/D-101 already found and accepted that non-`ubuntu-latest` runners are noisier for this project's *other* timing gate (nbody), lowering that gate's floor per-target rather than assuming uniform runner behavior. `ubuntu-latest` has never needed a relaxed gate in this project's history. A `getrusage(RUSAGE_SELF)` CPU-time measurement backend was also prototyped and works, but requires a new `[[bench]]` target and a `libc` dev-dependency, both of which sit inside `frontend-perf-measure`'s own hard-abort `contract_paths`/`Cargo.toml`-tail checks — the PR introducing it would trip its own gate by construction. The runner-move needs neither.
- Decision: stage new Ruby job constants `D112_UBUNTU_FRONTEND_PERF_MEASURE_JOB`/`D112_UBUNTU_FRONTEND_PERF_GATE_JOB` in `scripts/check_roadmap_evidence.rb`, identical to the live `D91_RELAX_FRONTEND_PERF_MANIFEST_MEASURE_JOB`/`REPLICATED_PERF_GATE_JOB` except `"runs-on" => "ubuntu-latest"` and the LLVM-22 install step swapped for the already-reviewed `apt.llvm.org` pattern `native-build-test`'s own Linux legs already use (see Global Constraints for both step bodies verbatim). Gather real shadow-measurement CI evidence (Task 4) that this reduces false-positive variance before activating; do not activate on the job-duration proxy alone (job duration includes checkout/toolchain-install/compile time, not just the timed Criterion samples — a weaker signal than a direct measurement of the actual gate).
- Alternatives: `getrusage` CPU-time measurement (rejected for now per Context above — self-trips the gate's own hard-abort checks; remains a documented fallback if the runner-move's own shadow evidence is unfavorable). Lower the 2% threshold instead (rejected — this project's own explicit, repeated position throughout #109's history is that the gate is a spec requirement, not a knob to relax to force a pass, exactly as D-096/D-101 already rejected the equivalent move for the nbody gate). Do nothing further, treat #109 as permanently open (rejected — it currently blocks PR-10 and, transitively, the entire remaining v0.2 milestone from merging).
- Consequences: `build-test-coverage` (still `macos-14`) is unaffected by this decision — it is a separate trust anchor with its own, unrelated pinning mechanism (`coverage_gate_present?`).
