- Alternatives: keep the policy as unversioned `AGENTS.md` prose only (rejected — the finding this ADR resolves is precisely that an authority/escalation model change needs a versioned record with alternatives and consequences considered, not only an instruction agents happen to follow); require the repository owner's explicit sign-off on every judgment fork (rejected — this is the status quo the policy is replacing; it does not scale to an autonomous PR-by-PR delivery cadence and contradicts D-013's own premise that the project is built by autonomous agents against mechanically checkable specs); let each agent session decide its own escalation threshold ad hoc (rejected — inconsistent behavior across sessions is worse than a single documented default, and a written policy is exactly what lets a later session or a human reviewer check whether an agent followed it); route judgment forks through a second AI reviewer chosen ad hoc each time rather than a consistent advisor/independent-reviewer pattern (rejected as an unnecessary extra decision per fork — the D-068 pinned-reviewer precedent already establishes that a consistent, previously-vetted reviewer beats an arbitrary one picked in the moment).
- Consequences: a session working on this repository no longer needs to stop and ask before an ordinary engineering judgment call, which is easier — but every such call now needs its reasoning recorded where a human or later session can audit it after the fact, which is a real obligation, not just a courtesy. The named exceptions (D-024's emergency path, credential/account actions, other user-reserved actions) remain hard stops regardless of how much autonomy this entry otherwise grants, so this decision must never be read as broadening those carve-outs. Because the repository owner's own intervention always takes precedence, this entry can never be used to justify overriding an explicit owner instruction on the theory that "the policy says agents decide" — the policy is a default for the owner's absence, not a ceiling on their authority. Superseding or narrowing this policy later (for example, once a second human maintainer is available and some judgment forks should route to them instead of an independent reviewer) requires a new ADR, not a silent edit to this one or to AGENTS.md's section alone.

## D-128: Preserve ordinary imports while making CPython interop policy-controlled

- Status: accepted (planned v0.7 contract; no current compiler behavior is claimed)
- Context: pycc's invariant is standard Python input, but the original v0.7 sketch in `docs/RUNTIME.md` required users to replace ordinary code such as `import numpy as np` with `from pycc.interop import cpython` plus `cpython.import_module("numpy")`. That source rewrite made otherwise-portable code pycc-specific. It also treated an empty `[interop].allow` list as pure mode, so every CPython-backed package needed a redundant permission even though its source `import` already expressed intent. Existing NumPy extension modules cannot load directly into `pycc_rt`: they export CPython extension entry points and depend on the CPython object model, C API, allocator, interpreter state, and GIL, whereas pycc uses its own unboxed/native representations and no libpython. Source compatibility therefore requires pycc to generate and package a real CPython boundary, not pretend those ABIs are interchangeable. The current v0.1 compiler still rejects every `import` with `C0001`; this decision changes only the future v0.7 contract.
- Decision:
  1. Ordinary standard-Python imports are the primary v0.7 interop surface. The resolver classifies each import as native pycc or CPython-backed. `import numpy as np` remains unchanged source; a CPython-backed classification makes pycc generate the bridge and bundle a pinned CPython 3.14 runtime plus the resolved package/native-library closure. The deployment artifact must not require a separately installed Python and must not search ambient `site-packages`. A low-level `pycc.interop.cpython` API may remain for advanced explicit handles, but normal package use cannot require it.
  2. The interop policy is a closed three-value enum shared by `pycc.toml` and the CLI: `auto` (default) permits every direct CPython-backed import root present in the source; `allowlist` permits only roots named by `[interop].allow`; and `deny` rejects every CPython-backed import while leaving native pycc imports available. `--interop-policy auto|allowlist|deny` overrides project configuration. `--pure` is shorthand for `deny` and conflicts with an explicit `--interop-policy` instead of depending on argument order.
  3. An allowlisted root covers its submodules and the exact transitive dependency closure selected for that root. Another direct source import root needs its own entry. The resolver records the complete interpreter, package, wheel/native-library, target, version, and integrity identity in a reproducible `pycc.lock`; the embedded environment exposes only that locked closure. Exact lock schema, resolver source, artifact layout, and update command are mandatory v0.7 planning work before implementation, not silently chosen by the first code change.
  4. CPython-owned values remain behind the compiler's typed interop representation. Package stubs supply the public type surface; a genuinely untyped value leaking into pure pycc code remains `I0401`, and a policy-rejected import is `I0402`. Standard-Python scalar/container conversions and supported buffer protocols provide source-compatible marshalling. The boundary benchmark must report copied and zero-copy transfers separately; automatic compatibility is not a claim that the bridge is free.
  5. The embedded interpreter owns its GIL only while executing CPython-backed operations. Native pycc execution remains GIL-free, and packages may release the embedded GIL internally according to their own contracts.
- Alternatives: keep `pycc.interop.cpython.import_module()` as the only supported spelling (rejected because it violates the standard-source invariant and forces code forks); make `allowlist` the default (rejected because the source import already expresses intent and ordinary compatibility should not require duplicate configuration); load the developer's or target machine's system Python and `site-packages` automatically (rejected because it makes builds and deployments environment-dependent rather than autonomous and reproducible); implement enough of the CPython C API inside `pycc_rt` to load existing extension modules without CPython (rejected because faithfully reproducing the CPython object/interpreter ABI is effectively another interpreter and would undermine pycc's own runtime model).
- Consequences:
  - **POS-001:** CPython-compatible source, including aliases and `from ... import ...`, can remain unchanged when compiled with pycc.
  - **POS-002:** `auto` provides zero-configuration compatibility, while opt-in `allowlist` and `deny`/`--pure` builds give deployment and supply-chain policy a fail-closed mode.
  - **POS-003:** Interop artifacts are autonomous at runtime because the interpreter and locked dependency closure travel with the application; pure artifacts retain the no-libpython guarantee.
  - **NEG-001:** Interop artifacts are materially larger and retain CPython's GIL and object-model costs inside the boundary.
  - **NEG-002:** Cross-compilation now includes target-specific interpreter, wheel, native-library, loader-path, signing, and license work; absence of a compatible locked artifact is a build failure, not a host-environment fallback.
  - **NEG-003:** The v0.7 implementation must build import classification, stub-aware typing, deterministic resolution/locking, bundling, diagnostics, and a measured marshalling layer before the feature can be called complete on any target.

## D-129: Keep the nbody gate on wall clock after CPU time fails to reduce variance across Tier-1

- Status: accepted (Phase B; closes issue
  [#226](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/issues/226))
- Context: D-126 required at least five independent exact-child CPU-time
  observations from every Tier-1 leg before changing the nbody assertion or its
  floors, and explicitly allowed a negative Phase B result. Five ordinary CI
  observations now exist from runs
  [30814975808](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/actions/runs/30814975808),
  [30815296286](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/actions/runs/30815296286),
  [30816067817](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/actions/runs/30816067817),
  [30817223376](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/actions/runs/30817223376), and
  [30817715798](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/actions/runs/30817715798).
  The first run used the final benchmark implementation before a manifest-only
  relocation of unchanged target-specific dev-dependency tables; the other four
  ran the reviewed Phase A tree or descendants containing it. The full
  observation table is recorded in
  [#226](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/issues/226#issuecomment-5166985278).
  Across the five ratio observations, the sample coefficient of variation was:

  | Tier-1 leg | Wall-clock CV | CPU-time CV | CPU-time change |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|
  | macOS aarch64 coverage | 2.069% | 1.548% | 25.1% lower |
  | Ubuntu x86_64 | 13.979% | 13.985% | 0.05% higher (effectively identical) |
  | Ubuntu aarch64 | 0.995% | 0.990% | 0.5% lower (effectively identical) |
  | macOS x86_64 | 10.636% | 9.230% | 13.2% lower |
  | Windows x86_64 | 6.055% | 8.729% | 44.2% higher |

  CPU time therefore improves two macOS legs, is effectively unchanged on both
  Linux legs, and is materially worse on Windows. Most importantly, it does not
  improve the motivating Ubuntu x86_64 instability: the first observation was
  19.7282x wall and 19.7377x CPU, so a 20x CPU gate would have failed the same
  byte-identical tree.
- Decision: retain the wall-clock ratio as the sole nbody assertion and retain
  D-093/D-095/D-096/D-101's existing 20x/12x/15x/18x target-specific floors.
  Continue recording exact-child CPU time, both ratios, all four medians, the
  required wall floor, and `wall_pass` through D-126's frozen summary schema.
  CPU time remains diagnostic telemetry, not a result selector or fallback
  gate. No CPU-time floors are introduced because the evidence does not support
  a cross-platform methodology switch.
- Alternatives: switch every leg to CPU time and derive margins below each
  observed minimum (rejected because the new clock leaves Ubuntu x86_64's
  variance unchanged and makes Windows variance worse); use CPU time only on the
  two macOS legs (rejected because a per-platform mixture of clock semantics
  would complicate the acceptance contract while leaving the known Linux
  failure mode untouched); gather more observations before deciding (rejected
  as a blocker because D-126 deliberately set five per leg as the decision bar
  and the negative result is already clear on the motivating and worst-regressed
  legs; retained telemetry can still support a later superseding decision);
  lower the wall floors or accept reruns (rejected for D-126's original reason:
  either would hide instability without improving the measurement method).
- Consequences: Phase B changes documentation, not benchmark code, workflow
  trust boundaries, launch count/order, correctness checking, assertion metric,
  or floors. The dual-clock telemetry remains useful for diagnosing future
  failures, but issue #226 is complete with an evidence-backed negative result
  rather than an unsupported CPU-time gate.

## D-130: Decompose the session handoff log into per-session files

- Status: accepted
- Context: [D-066](#d-066-maintain-an-agent-retrospective-log-and-a-session-handoff-log) established `docs/SESSION_LOG.md` as a single running handoff snapshot, updated at every meaningful checkpoint. By this entry, that file held 38 dated entries across 1791 lines and had grown into the exact kind of shared, ever-growing, must-read-then-append tail this project's other conventions already avoid for machine-local hooks ([D-023](#d-023-shared-auto-evolution-intent-with-local-hook-execution)) and for CI-check relaxation incidents ([D-125](#d-125-session-driven-temporary-ci-check-relaxation-narrowly-superseding-d-024d-054), one incident at a time rather than a standing shared state). The cost was concrete, not hypothetical: this same file's own migration hit a real merge conflict mid-session when a concurrent agent session (this repository routinely runs several independently, per [D-013](#d-013-development-model)) landed its own new entry at the same insertion point at the top of the file within minutes of this session's own commit, and the file's own internal separator convention (a `---` rule between entries) turned out to have been applied inconsistently across its history — some entry boundaries had no rule and, in two places, no blank line at all — a symptom of many independent hands editing one shared tail over time without a structural check catching the drift.
- Decision: replace `docs/SESSION_LOG.md` with a `docs/sessions/` directory holding one file per checkpoint, named `YYYY-MM-DD-NN-<slug>.md` — extending the dated-slug convention this repository already uses for `docs/superpowers/specs/` and `docs/superpowers/plans/` with a two-digit `NN` same-day sequence number (`01` = the day's oldest entry, ascending to the newest), since this project's own history already has dates with many same-day checkpoints (2026-07-26 alone has 14) that a bare date prefix cannot order — a first draft of this migration omitted `NN` and was correctly flagged by review as leaving same-day entries unordered by anything but topic slug. Each file keeps the snapshot shape D-066 already established (status grounded in an inspected commit/PR/CI state, what's in flight, known follow-ups, resume pointers) — only the storage granularity changes, not the content contract. `docs/sessions/README.md` is the one static file in the directory: a purpose statement (mirroring this file's own preamble), never appended to, so it never becomes a second shared mutable tail under a different name. A fresh session resumes by listing the dated entries (excluding `README.md`) sorted by filename — the date-then-`NN` prefix makes lexical order match true chronological order — and reading the most recent few, exactly as a session already discovers the newest file under `docs/superpowers/plans/` via `ls`/`git log` rather than a maintained index. All 42 existing entries were migrated verbatim: a script split the original file on its real entry boundaries (a line matching `## YYYY-MM-DD — ...`, confirmed to be the reliable delimiter once the `---` rule itself proved inconsistently applied) and a round-trip check — reassembling every generated file's header and body with whichever separator style the original actually used at that exact boundary — reproduced the original file byte-for-byte before anything was written or the original deleted; `NN` was then assigned per date group from that same verified top-to-bottom (newest-first) order, so it reflects the file's own real history rather than a guess. Migrated entries whose own body text linked a sibling `docs/` file (e.g. `./AGENT_RETROSPECTIVE.md`, `DECISIONS.md#...`) had those relative links rebased to `../...`, since the file now lives one directory deeper. `docs/DECISIONS.md`'s own D-066 entry is not rewritten (this project's own rule: a new decision supersedes, it does not silently edit, an accepted one) — its Status line gains this narrowing reference, mirroring the D-024/D-125 precedent, while its Decision/Alternatives/Consequences prose stands as the historical record of what was true when D-066 was written.
- Alternatives: keep one shared file but rotate older entries into a second archive file once it exceeds some size (rejected — still one shared mutable tail every session merges into, the exact conflict this decision closes, just with an arbitrary rotation trigger added on top); one file per PR number instead of per dated checkpoint (rejected — several existing entries are not tied to a single PR, e.g. mid-flight status snapshots, blocked-state escalations, and post-merge audits; the dated-checkpoint shape already generalizes to every entry shape actually observed in the migrated history); a maintained index file listing every session file (rejected — this is the shared-mutable-tail problem restated one level up: every session would still need to read and append to the same file, and a stale or conflicting index is worse than no index, since a directory listing is always authoritative and cannot go stale); a database or structured log store instead of markdown files (rejected for the same reason D-078/D-013 already give elsewhere: plain files reviewed like any other documentation change stay simplest-correct and add no new tooling dependency).
- Consequences: `AGENTS.md`'s D-066 section and `docs/SPEC.md`'s session-log table row point at `docs/sessions/` (and its `README.md`) instead of the retired file, updated in this same change. `docs/ROADMAP.md`'s own description of the D-066 tooling and its two specific cross-references into named historical entries are updated to the new paths in this same change; `docs/AGENT_RETROSPECTIVE.md`'s own historical mentions of `docs/SESSION_LOG.md`, and every historical ADR entry's own evidence citations into it (D-109's, D-111's, and others), are left untouched — they are accurate descriptions of what was true and where evidence lived at the time they were written, the same rule that already keeps this file from rewriting its own past entries. A future reference to "the session log" anywhere in the docs means `docs/sessions/`, never the retired file. Concurrent-session write conflicts on the handoff log itself become structurally rare rather than merely less likely: two sessions writing on the same calendar date now collide only if they also independently choose the same topic slug, not merely by both touching the file's shared top.
## D-131: Store materialized `None` values in canonical local and module slots

- Status: accepted (closes issue [#242](https://github.com/rotnov/pycc/issues/242); D-130 is reserved by concurrent PR #313)
- Context: the frontend accepts ordinary Python assignments whose right-hand side has `Ty::None`, including `y = xs.append(2)`, `result = values.add(2)`, and the result of a user function declared `-> None`. Codegen already preserves each operation's side effects and materializes D-075's canonical LLVM `i8 0` unit carrier, while name loads and printing retain the static `Ty::None` distinction from `bool`. Storage alone remained incomplete: `collect_stmt_bindings` excluded `Ty::None`, so a function-local assignment reached `emit_assign` without a predeclared slot and panicked; adding it there alone would make the equivalent module assignment reach `declare_module_globals`, whose type match also lacked `Ty::None` and would panic. D-074's consequence that general nested `None` values remained outside D-072 described that earlier backend limit, while D-075 named only parameter ABI and parameter entry slots.
- Decision: use the existing canonical LLVM `i8 0` unit carrier for ordinary function-local and module-global `Ty::None` assignment slots. Collect `Ty::None` assignment targets with the other storable MIR types; allocate module globals as zero-initialized `i8`; and retain each slot's separate initialized flag so the carrier's zero value can never make a read-before-assignment look valid. The MIR expression and `StorageSlot` static type, not the physical width or `Scalar::Bool` carrier variant, controls Python-visible `None` behavior. This decision supersedes only D-074's broad statement that general nested `None` values remain outside D-072 and extends D-075's parameter-only storage scope. D-072 itself remains accepted unchanged: using `print()`'s own result as a nested expression still takes its explicit named rejection and does not reach the storage path.
- Alternatives: reject every `None` assignment with a new spanned frontend diagnostic (rejected because it would introduce a pycc-only restriction after the backend already implements the value carrier and observable behavior); special-case only `list.append` (rejected because user-function and `set.add` results have the identical checked type and carrier, so storage policy belongs at the type boundary); add a heap singleton, pointer representation, or new tagged union (rejected because `None` carries no payload here and identity/optional-union work is outside this issue); relax D-072 and lower nested `print()` too (rejected because that separate accepted boundary is unnecessary to fix any currently materialized non-`print` result).
- Consequences: assigning, reading, returning, testing, and printing an already-materializable `None` result can now use ordinary local or module storage without an internal compiler panic; `list.append` and `set.add` side effects occur before the zero carrier is stored, and printing the stored name renders `None`, not `False` or `0`. Local/global read-before-assignment safety remains governed by D-074's independent initialization flags. `None` still has no heap object, optional/union representation, general identity/equality surface, or `dict.get` no-default support, and `value = print(...)` remains D-072's deliberate exit-101 boundary.

## D-132: Measure the D-084 throughput floor as a median of 3 samples

- Status: accepted
- Context: PR #313 hit a one-off `FAIL: exceeded 50ms threshold (measured 54.55ms)` on `scripts/check_frontend_throughput.rb`'s D-079/D-084 `pycc check` throughput-floor gate; a bare rerun passed. Before treating this as either a regression or accepting indefinite reruns, a dedicated investigation gathered historical evidence: ~209 sampled `build-test-coverage` runs on `main` and active PR branches (2026-08-01 through 2026-08-03) whose throughput step completed successfully showed a stable 14.60-31.92ms range against the 50ms threshold (mean 19.03ms, median 17.88ms), with zero passing runs within 10% of the threshold and zero historical failures of this specific step across the entire sample. The 54.55ms spike does not reproduce anywhere in that record. This matches classic single-wall-clock-sample noise on a shared GitHub-hosted runner (scheduler jitter, co-tenant contention, cold cache) rather than a systemic regression — the check has a wide, stable margin, not one it normally runs close to. D-126/D-129 already established that migrating a similar gate (nbody) from wall-clock to CPU-time measurement did not reduce variance on Ubuntu x86_64 and made it worse on Windows, so switching D-084 to CPU-time was ruled out without re-testing that already-answered question.
- Decision: change `measure_and_check` in `scripts/check_frontend_throughput.rb` to run `pycc check <fixture>` `samples` times (default 3, must stay odd) and gate on the median wall-clock elapsed time rather than a single sample, keeping the same 50ms absolute-floor threshold and the same immediate hard failure if any individual sample's `pycc check` invocation itself exits non-zero. This directly cancels a single-draw noise spike (an outlier can only win if it is not the middle value) while still catching a genuine, sustained regression, which would raise all three samples together. No CI workflow or threshold change is needed — `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s invocation (`ruby scripts/check_frontend_throughput.rb target/debug/pycc tests/fixtures/pr6_1000_loc_bench.py 50`) is unchanged; neither this script nor `ci.yml`'s job definition is D-103-manifest-protected, so no propose/activate sequence was required.
- Alternatives: switch to CPU-time (rusage) measurement (rejected — D-126/D-129's own evidence on the structurally similar nbody gate already showed this does not help and can hurt cross-platform variance; re-deriving that result here would waste the exact CI time this ADR exists to stop wasting); raise the 50ms threshold for headroom (rejected — the historical data shows headroom is already large and stable, so a higher threshold would only mask a future genuine regression sooner, unlike D-114's frontend-perf-gate threshold raise, which responded to a gate that genuinely ran close to its line); do nothing and rely on manual reruns (rejected — this is what already cost the session real time and prompted the investigation; an unattended median-of-N measurement removes the need for a human or agent to notice and rerun); use hardware performance counters (`perf_event_open`) (rejected without implementation — often unavailable or restricted on hosted GitHub Actions runners and has no cross-platform equivalent for the Windows/macOS legs this same job's matrix also runs; issue #297 already scoped profiler tooling to a local, non-gating diagnostic workflow, not a CI gate mechanism).
- Consequences: `scripts/check_frontend_throughput.rb` now invokes `pycc check` up to 3 times per CI run instead of once, adding a small constant runtime cost (roughly 2 extra ~15-30ms invocations) that is negligible next to this job's multi-minute build-and-coverage steps. A future sustained regression must clear the median of 3 runs, not just avoid one unlucky sample, which is a strictly stronger signal than the prior single-sample design. If historical evidence later shows the median-of-3 design itself still flakes, escalate the sample count or revisit CPU-time measurement citing fresh evidence, but do not do either preemptively without new data.

## D-133: PR-13 generic-function type-parameter representation is a new `Ty::Param(String)` variant, substituted before MIR lowering

- Status: proposed
- Context: D-089 defined `Ty`'s full recursive shape (`crates/pycc_hir/src/lib.rs`, `pub enum Ty { Int, Float, Bool, Str, None, Infer, List(Box<Ty>), Dict(Box<(Ty, Ty)>), Set(Box<Ty>), Tuple(Box<Vec<Ty>>) }`) but explicitly deferred the generic-function type-parameter case (`def f[T](x: T) -> T`) to whichever of PR-10/PR-13 implemented it first (design doc §4). Verified at PR-13 task-start (2026-08-03, commit `a2527a6`): PR-10 did not add it — `crates/pycc_hir/src/lib.rs`'s `lower_function` still contains an explicit `if def.type_params.is_some() { return Err(unsupported("generic function type parameters are not supported yet", def.range)); }` (the same shape as every other deliberately-unimplemented-syntax rejection in this compiler, e.g. `is_async`/`decorator_list`). No `Ty::Var`/`Ty::Param`/`TypeVar` construct exists anywhere in `pycc_hir`, `pycc_types`, or `pycc_mir` today. A representation is needed before `pycc_types`' `collect_expr_constraints`/`infer_expr_in` (`crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs`) can type-check a generic function's body at all, since every expression there is checked against a concrete `Ty`.
- Decision: add `Ty::Param(String)` (the type parameter's source name, e.g. `"T"`) as a new leaf variant alongside `Ty::Infer`, distinct from it: `Ty::Infer` means "not yet resolved, must become concrete before codegen, resolution is compiler-driven unification," while `Ty::Param` means "resolved to whatever concrete `Ty` this specific call site substitutes for it, resolution is call-site-driven substitution, and the *same* function body type-checks once per instantiation." A generic function's parameter/return/body `Ty`s are checked once, symbolically, against `Ty::Param` positions (proving internal consistency: every use of `T` in the signature must agree), and then, per D-134, exactly one concrete instantiation per call site is substituted (`Ty::Param(name) -> concrete_ty`, a plain recursive tree rewrite, no unification) before that call site's specialized copy of the function reaches `pycc_mir`. `pycc_mir` and `pycc_codegen` never see `Ty::Param` — by the time MIR lowering runs, substitution has already produced an ordinary concrete `Ty` tree, so no new `MirExpr`/codegen case is needed for genericity itself (only for whatever concrete `Ty` the substitution produced, which is already handled).
- Alternatives: reuse `Ty::Infer` for type parameters too (rejected — conflates "the checker hasn't finished unifying this yet" with "this is deliberately symbolic and only becomes concrete at a call site"; the existing invariant that `Ty::Infer` must never leak into MIR, guarded by dedicated pre-MIR assertions per `crates/pycc_types/src/lib.rs`'s own comments near line 2787, would then also have to hold for every generic-function body pre-instantiation, doubling what that invariant means without a comment or type-level distinction saying so); a full unification-variable/substitution-map (`HashMap<TypeVarId, Ty>`) infrastructure, matching Rust's or a general Hindley-Milner solver's own generics model (rejected as disproportionate to PR-13's own scope — D-134 restricts this PR to a single type parameter per function with a call-site-known concrete argument, so full multi-variable unification buys nothing not already available from a name-keyed substitution and would be net-new machinery unlikely to be exercised end-to-end before v0.3's class-generics work, if ever, revisits this); representing a generic function as a distinct `HirItem` variant carrying an uninterpreted body (rejected — every other `Ty`-shaped consumer downstream, diagnostics, `to_str`-style debug rendering, existing `Ty` equality/hash derives, would need a second parallel code path instead of participating in `Ty`'s existing recursive matching for free).
- Consequences: `Ty`'s `derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Eq)` set already covers a new unit-like `String`-carrying variant with no additional derive work. Every existing exhaustive `match ty { ... }` over `Ty` across `pycc_types`/`pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen` gains one new arm requiring an explicit decision (typically: type-checking accepts it symbolically inside a generic function body, but `pycc_mir` lowering and `pycc_codegen` treat an unsubstituted `Ty::Param` reaching them as an internal-invariant panic, mirroring how `Ty::Infer` reaching MIR is already treated as an invariant violation, not a diagnosable user error). `size_of::<Ty>()` must be re-measured after adding this variant against D-109's 16-byte ceiling and the `frontend-perf-gate`'s 7% (D-114) threshold; a `String`-carrying variant is no larger than `Tuple`'s existing `Box<Vec<Ty>>` pointer shape once boxed, so `Ty::Param(Box<str>)` (not a bare `String`, to stay one pointer wide) is the required concrete shape, not `Ty::Param(String)`'s naive two-word form.

## D-134: PR-13 thin slice — one type parameter, one concrete scalar per call site, every other generic shape gets a clean diagnostic

- Status: proposed
- Context: matching PR-10/PR-11/PR-12's own established pattern (D-105 `list[int]` only, D-122 `dict[str,int]`/`set[int]` only, D-116 `tuple[int|bool|float, ...]` only), PR-13 needs its own scope cut for which generic-function shapes reach real codegen versus which get a diagnostic. `pycc_types`' signature-inference solver is scalar-only by construction (D-116 point 4's correction note, reconfirmed unchanged as of this ADR — no PR between PR-11b and PR-13 touched it), which bounds what a type parameter can be substituted with from real, unannotated call sites regardless of `Ty::Param`'s own generality (D-133).
- Decision: v0.2 ships real codegen for a generic function with **exactly one type parameter** (`def f[T](...) -> ...`, any number of parameters/return positions referencing that same single `T`), called from **any number of call sites**, where **each call site's argument used to resolve `T` is one of `Ty::Int`/`Ty::Float`/`Ty::Bool`/`Ty::Str`** (matching the solver's existing scalar-only reach) — each call site is monomorphized independently by substituting `Ty::Param("T") -> <that call site's concrete Ty>` (D-133) and lowering a specialized copy of the function body through the existing `pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen` pipeline exactly as a non-generic function would, reusing D-105's monomorphization machinery (specialized-copy-per-instantiation, not a single polymorphic codegen path — matching this compiler's existing container monomorphization model, not a vtable). Two or more distinct concrete types instantiated at different call sites for the same generic function are both allowed and both reach codegen (this is what "monomorphization" means — `f(1)` and `f("x")` compile to two independent specialized function bodies, mirroring how `list[int]` and a hypothetical `list[str]` would be two independent instantiations if the latter reached codegen). Rejected before codegen with new diagnostic `T0042`: two or more type parameters (`def f[T, U](...)`); a type parameter used inside a container position (`def f[T](x: list[T])`, since D-105 already restricted container element types independently of genericity and stacking both gaps is out of scope); a call site whose argument type used to resolve `T` is not one of the four scalars above (e.g. passing a `list[int]` value where `T` is inferred); a call site where `T`'s occurrences in the signature are inconsistent (e.g. `def f[T](x: T, y: T) -> T` called as `f(1, "a")`, resolved via the same "every occurrence of `T` must substitute to the same concrete `Ty`" check the symbolic pass in D-133 already performs). Each rejection case gets its own snapshot test under `T0042`, matching `docs/DIAGNOSTICS.md`'s existing per-code quality bar (mirroring `T0034`'s own multi-case snapshot coverage, not a single combined test).
- Alternatives: ship zero call-site restriction and let the solver's own existing scalar-only limitation silently produce whatever diagnostic already exists for "type of this expression could not be determined" (rejected — that path was designed for ordinary type-inference failures, not deliberately-scoped generic instantiation limits, and would produce a confusing, non-actionable message for a case this PR can name precisely, breaking the project's established "clean diagnostic, not a leaked internal panic" convention from D-105 onward); support multiple type parameters from the start since `Ty::Param(Box<str>)` already generalizes to any number of distinct names (rejected — nothing in the design doc or delivery plan requires it for v0.2's conformance target, and D-105's own "exactly one concrete instantiation" precedent is the established v0.2-wide pattern this ADR is following, not inventing); restrict to exactly one call site per generic function (rejected — monomorphization's entire value is compiling the same generic body at multiple call sites with different concrete types, and the constraint solver's scalar-only limitation already bounds the real risk surface without needing this additional, artificial restriction).
- Consequences: `pycc_types` gains a symbolic type-checking pass for a generic function body (checking internal consistency of `T`'s occurrences against `Ty::Param`, D-133) plus a per-call-site substitution-and-recheck step (substituting the concrete argument type and re-running ordinary scalar type-checking on the specialized body). `pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen` require no new generics-aware logic beyond what D-133 already establishes, since every call site's specialized body is an ordinary concrete-`Ty` function by the time it reaches them. `docs/DIAGNOSTICS.md` gains `T0042` with its four named rejection cases. `docs/ROADMAP.md` records multiple type parameters, container-position type parameters, and richer bound/constraint syntax (PEP 696 defaults, `TypeVar` bounds) as explicit v0.2 follow-ups, not silently dropped scope.

## D-135: `type` statement and legacy `TypeAlias` are compile-time-only name substitution, no runtime or `Ty` representation

- Status: proposed
- Context: design doc §2 groups PEP 695's `type` statement and PEP 613's legacy `TypeAlias` syntax as "the same underlying alias mechanism." Verified at PR-13 task-start: `crates/pycc_ast/src/lib.rs`'s `stmt_range` already matches `Stmt::TypeAlias(ruff_python_ast::StmtTypeAlias { range, .. })` (the vendored parser already produces this node for `type X = ...`; a legacy `X: TypeAlias = ...` parses as an ordinary `Stmt::AnnAssign`/`Stmt::Assign` with `TypeAlias` as an ordinary annotation expression, not a distinct AST node), but `pycc_hir`'s `lower_stmt` has no dedicated arm for either shape today, so both currently fall through to the generic `C0001` "statement kind not supported yet" catch-all (the same fate PR-11 recorded for `CmpOp::In` and PR-12 for `Expr::UnaryOp`).
- Decision: both the `type X = <expr>` statement and a legacy `X: TypeAlias = <expr>` assignment are resolved entirely inside `pycc_types`/`pycc_hir` as a **compile-time name-to-`Ty` binding table**: lowering a `Stmt::TypeAlias` (or an `AnnAssign` whose annotation is exactly the name `TypeAlias`) evaluates its RHS expression as a type expression (reusing the existing annotation-to-`Ty` resolution path already used for parameter/return annotations) and records `alias_name -> Ty` in a module-scoped table consulted wherever an annotation expression is later resolved. No `HirItem`/`MirItem` is emitted for the alias itself — it produces zero HIR nodes, zero MIR nodes, and no runtime object; it is purely a preprocessing step that lets a later annotation spell a `Ty` two different ways. A generic alias (`type Alias[T] = list[T]`) is out of scope for v0.2 (no generic classes and no container-of-type-parameter per D-134): a `type` statement's own left-hand side is rejected pre-codegen with `T0042` if it declares type parameters, reusing the same diagnostic code as D-134's generic-function rejection cases since both are "generic shape beyond this PR's one-type-parameter, non-container thin slice."
- Alternatives: represent an alias as a real `Ty::Alias(String, Box<Ty>)` variant that downstream code has to unwrap (rejected — every other `Ty` consumer would need a new match arm purely to strip a wrapper that carries no information not already available by resolving the alias eagerly at the point of use, unlike `Ty::Param`, D-133, which genuinely carries information downstream code cannot discharge until a call site is known); defer resolution to `pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen` (rejected — an alias is a pure naming convenience with no runtime existence in real Python either; resolving it any later than annotation-checking time would require every later pipeline stage to know about aliasing for no benefit).
- Consequences: `pycc_hir`/`pycc_types` gain a small new preprocessing pass (collect alias bindings once per module, in source order, before checking any function that might reference one) and one new diagnostic reuse (`T0042` shared with D-134's generic-shape rejections, not a fifth new code). `pycc_mir`/`pycc_codegen` require no changes at all for this feature. `docs/TYPE_SYSTEM.md`'s Generics section, which already asserts "PEP 695 syntax ... and legacy `TypeVar` both supported" as an aspirational v1.0 statement, is corrected to describe the v0.2 thin slice precisely (D-134/D-135's own scope, not the full PEP 696-defaults/variance-inference model that sentence currently implies is already true).
